The Second Monopoly

The Patent Expired. The Exclusion Didn’t —
and It Doubled the Cost of American Living.

(formerly titled The Forgotten Half of the Patent Clause)

How a Constitutional Bargain Was Cancelled — Restoring It Frees Buyers from Surveillance, Stops Robots Replacing America’s Drivers & Operators, and Returns the Joy of Operating Real Machines

Founder, the Make American Living Affordable (MALA) movement on X

Apparatus Convention

The book uses no superscript reference numbers in the running text. Citations of sources, court cases, and statutes appear in the Endnotes section at the back of the book. The Endnotes, Endnote Commentary, Glossary, and Key Terms follow the Chicago Manual of Style, 17th edition (author-date system), with the house exceptions noted below.

The order of entries is set out in full in the Endnotes Ordering section, which opens the Endnotes. Two departures from Chicago apply throughout the apparatus. Every entry ends with a source URL. And same-year works by one author take no a, b, or c suffix; an in-text citation such as “Martin (2025)” is disambiguated by context, the running text making the specific work plain.

The Endnote Commentary section, which collects in alphabetical order — keyed to the same author surname or case name as the Endnotes section — substantive notes on those sources: full passages a chapter quotes only in part, additional context the running text omits for the sake of pace, related material the curious reader will want and the impatient reader can skip. Not every Endnote has a corresponding Commentary entry; only those that genuinely reward a longer note. An Endnote that does have a Commentary entry ends with the italicized notice “See Endnote Commentary.”

The two sections are designed to be read together by a reader who wants depth, and skipped together by a reader who does not.

The Endnote Commentary is followed by a Glossary section, which collects in alphabetical order by abbreviation the acronyms used throughout the book — primarily federal agencies, statutes, and regulatory frameworks defined parenthetically at first use in the running text. The Glossary is followed by a Key Terms section, which collects in alphabetical order by term the book’s specific terms of art (such as bootleggers-and-Baptists, regulatory ratchet, anti-backsliding provision, three-tier breakdown, public domain in the patent-law sense, the Expired Patent Commons, remanufacturing in the book’s specific sense, grandfather exemption, expired-patent commerce, constitutional forgetting, and two suspenders), each with a brief definition.

Each Endnote entry concludes with a URL — Justia for U.S. Supreme Court cases, Cornell University Legal Information Institute for federal statutes, Founders Online for Founding-era documents, the publisher’s canonical URL for periodicals, books, and reports, with Amazon as a last resort for older works without stable publisher URLs — so that readers using a digital edition of the book can follow the source directly.

The reader who wants to verify a claim in the running text consults the Endnote entry alphabetized by the author or case name as they appear in the text. There are no chapter-specific endnote groupings; the Endnotes section is a single book-wide alphabetical list. The reader who wants additional context on a source — the full passage a chapter quotes only in part, or material the running text omits for the sake of pace — consults the parallel entry in the Endnote Commentary section, which is keyed to the same anchor and ends with the italicized notice “See Endnote for full citation.”

The reader who wants the meaning of an acronym consults the Glossary; the reader who wants to anchor a recurring concept consults the Key Terms section. Each section does one job.

In-text citations follow Chicago author-date style: integrated into the sentence as “Yandle (1983) named the pattern,” with full names and titles reserved for the Endnote entry. Acronyms are spelled out in full at first appearance in the book with the abbreviation following in parentheses; thereafter only the abbreviation is used.

Endnotes Ordering

Sort order. Entries run by headword — the first significant element of the entry. The ten rules below govern placement throughout this section. Rules 2 through 5 also govern the Glossary and the Key Terms sections, each alphabetized by its own headwords.

1. Headword. The headword is the author’s surname, the organization’s name, the case name, the statute’s name, or — for an entry with none of these — the entry’s own leading words.

2. Leading articles. A leading “The,” “A,” or “An” is disregarded for placement. An article anywhere else in the headword counts as an ordinary word.

3. Word by word, not letter by letter. Comparison proceeds one word at a time, and a word boundary sorts ahead of any letter. New Civil Liberties Alliance therefore precedes Newbold, Charles, though a letter-by-letter sort would reverse them. The abbreviation “v.” counts as a word, so United States Code precedes United States v. Ballin.

4. Case and punctuation. Comparison ignores capitalization, commas, periods, apostrophes, ampersands, and hyphens. Sears, Roebuck & Co. v. Stiffel Co. files under Sears; D’Angelo files as DAngelo.

5. Numerals. A numeral inside a headword sorts by value and not as a string, so U.S. Patent No. 39,285 precedes 139,668, which precedes 5,357,751, which precedes 12,196,640. A headword beginning with a numeral sorts ahead of every headword beginning with a letter, so United States v. 50 Acres of Land precedes United States v. Ballin.

6. Court cases. A case files under the first party named in its style. United States v. Moghadam files under United States, never under Moghadam. In re and Ex parte file under In and Ex.

7. Statutes and code sections. A statute files under its own name — National Appliance Energy Conservation Act of 1987. A codified statutory section files under United States Code, and the sections there run by title number, then by section number. A codified regulation files under Code of Federal Regulations, and the entries there run by title number, then by part number, then by section number.

8. Names as written. A name files as the source writes it, not as it would read expanded. United States Code files under United; U.S. Department of Energy files under US. Spelled-out names therefore precede their abbreviated counterparts, and the two form separate runs.

9. Several entries under one headword. Entries sharing a headword run by date of publication, earliest first. An entry carrying no publication date — a continuing series such as the Consumer Price Index, or an organization’s standing web page — goes last among them, and those entries run alphabetically by title.

10. Topical entries. An entry standing for a body of material rather than a single document — Self-certification and certification by simulation; Nichia Corporation and the LED-to-laser lighting succession — files under its own leading word like any other entry.

Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña, 515 U.S. 200 (1995).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/200/.

Advanced Impaired Driving Prevention Technology Rulemaking. Advance Notice of Proposed Rulemaking, “Advanced Impaired Driving Prevention Technology,” 89 Fed. Reg. 830 (January 5, 2024). National Highway Traffic Safety Administration. Authorized by Section 24220 of the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act, Pub. L. No. 117-58, codified as a statutory note to 49 U.S.C. § 30111 (distinct from the AEB authority at § 24208, codified at 49 U.S.C. §§ 30129–30130). The statutory deadline of November 15, 2024 was missed; Section 24220(e) permits extension of up to three years on a determination that compliant technology is not yet available. NHTSA Docket NHTSA-2022-0079; rulemaking ongoing.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/01/05/2023-27665/advanced-impaired-driving-prevention-technology.

Aetna Life Insurance Co. v. Haworth, 300 U.S. 227 (1937).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/300/227/.

Ahlborn, Richard E., ed. Man Made Mobile: Early Saddles of Western North America. Smithsonian Studies in History and Technology, no. 39. Washington, DC: Smithsonian Institution Press, 1980. The standard scholarly reference on the trans-Atlantic and trans-continental development of the American working saddle, drawn from the Smithsonian’s Buckaroos in Paradise field project.

https://repository.si.edu/handle/10088/2470.

Aircraft Owners and Pilots Association. “Last of the Old vs. First of the New.” AOPA Pilot, June 2013. Account of Cessna’s 1996 return to single-engine production: the FAA wanted the restarted Cessna 172 certified to current Part 23, but because the design could not meet every later requirement, Cessna and the FAA agreed that it would be certified in part to older Part 23 amendments issued decades earlier. Cited in How a Remake Is Built and Certified for the 1996 restart under the original certificate, with the restarted design certified partly to older Part 23 amendments.

https://www.aopa.org/news-and-media/all-news/2013/june/pilot/last-of-the-old-vs-first-of-the-new.

Akorn, Inc. v. Provepharm, Inc., No. 14-CV-04106 (N.D. Ill. 2019). Federal antitrust litigation involving alleged monopolization of methylene blue through U.S. Pharmacopeia monograph revision; counterclaims survived motion to dismiss.

https://web.archive.org/web/20191114101913/https://lrus.wolterskluwer.com/news/antitrust-law-daily/monopolization-counterclaims-in-methylene-blue-drug-case-go-forward/81090/.

Alfred L. Snapp & Son, Inc. v. Puerto Rico, 458 U.S. 592 (1982).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/458/592/.

Allegheny Pittsburgh Coal Co. v. County Commission of Webster County, 488 U.S. 336 (1989). The unanimous Supreme Court decision holding that a county tax-assessment system that valued recently-acquired coal properties at full current value while leaving long-held properties at decades-old assessments violated the Equal Protection Clause. Cited in the chapter on the Takings Clause prong as the doctrinal cousin to the book’s argument that the regulatory wall imposes a constitutionally suspect inequality on similarly situated property — the original manufacturer held to his patent-year standards, the remanufacturer of the same now-expired patent held to ratcheted current-year standards.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/488/336/.

AllMachines. “Combine Harvesters.” AllMachines aggregator listing. Source for the U.S. retail price reference of approximately $646,000 for the John Deere S7 600 cited in the farm-equipment chapter alongside the IndiaMART listing for the India-market John Deere W70.

https://www.allmachines.com/combine-harvesters/view-all.

AllMachines. “Tractors — Brand: Mahindra.” AllMachines aggregator listing. Cross-reference for U.S.-market Mahindra tractor pricing cited in the farm-equipment chapter against India-market Mahindra pricing.

https://www.allmachines.com/tractors/view-all?brand=mahindra.

Alon, Noga, et al. “Remarks on the Disproof of the Unit Distance Conjecture.” arXiv preprint, May 2026. The companion paper released the same day as OpenAI’s announcement, in which nine mathematicians present a short, human-verified and somewhat generalized rendering of the machine-generated counterexample to Paul Erdős’s 1946 unit distance conjecture, together with their reflections on what the result does and does not establish about artificial intelligence in mathematics. Cited in the preface as the human verification that followed the machine’s origination.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20695

American Farm Bureau Federation. “Farm Bankruptcies Continued to Climb in 2025.” Market Intel report by AFBF economist Samantha Ayoub, February 2026. Source for the 46 percent year-over-year increase in Chapter 12 farm bankruptcy filings cited in the farm-equipment chapter (315 filings in calendar year 2025 against 216 in 2024), drawn from U.S. Courts data. The figure was reported during National Farmers Union President Rob Larew’s keynote at the NFU 124th Anniversary Convention in New Orleans, March 7–9, 2026.

https://www.fb.org/market-intel/farm-bankruptcies-continued-to-climb-in-2025.

American Hospital Association v. Becerra, 596 U.S. 724 (2022).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/596/20-1114/.

American Innovation and Manufacturing Act of 2020, Pub. L. No. 116-260, Div. S, Title I, Subtitle B, 134 Stat. 2253. Codified at 42 U.S.C. § 7675. Authorizes EPA to phase down hydrofluorocarbons by 85 percent over 15 years.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/7675.

American Transportation Research Institute. Issues and Opportunities with Driver-Facing Cameras. Arlington, VA: ATRI, April 2023. Survey of approximately 2,100 commercial truck drivers documenting that current driver-facing-camera users rate the technology 2.24 on a zero-to-ten approval scale and that female drivers rate the cameras’ privacy protection 24 percent lower than male drivers, with some reporting voyeurism, unwanted comments about their appearance, and sexual harassment from employees tasked with reviewing the footage. Cited in the trucking-section discussion of driver-facing-camera intrusion in the surveillance chapter.

https://truckingresearch.org/2023/04/new-atri-research-identifies-strategies-for-improving-driver-facing-camera-approval-and-utilization/.

American Transportation Research Institute. An Analysis of the Operational Costs of Trucking: 2025 Update. Arlington, VA: American Transportation Research Institute, 2025. The trucking industry’s standard annual accounting of marginal operating costs; the 2025 edition reports a fully loaded average operating cost of $2.26 per mile for 2024, of which driver wages and benefits account for roughly $0.995 per mile. Cited in the autonomous-vehicle chapter for the per-mile economics of the human-driven truck.

https://truckingresearch.org/2025/07/an-analysis-of-the-operational-costs-of-trucking-2025-update/.

American Trucking Associations. “Economics and Industry Data.” American Trucking Associations. The trade association’s compilation of industry statistics, reporting roughly 3.5 million professional truck drivers employed in the United States. Cited in the autonomous-vehicle chapter for the scale of the workforce the autonomous transition would displace.

https://www.trucking.org/economics-and-industry-data.

American Trucking Associations. “Maintaining Aftertreatment Systems: Operational and Cost Considerations.” ATA Technology and Maintenance Council report; cross-referenced through industry coverage including OEM Off-Highway and Heavy Duty Trucking. Source for the figure that ongoing aftertreatment maintenance represents approximately thirteen percent of total operating maintenance costs over the life of a Class 8 truck, cited in the trucking chapter.

https://www.trucking.org/.

American Waterways Operators. Organizational homepage. The national trade association for the U.S. tugboat, towboat, and barge industry, organized in Washington, D.C. in 1944 and representing more than 300 member companies operating on the nation’s inland rivers, the Great Lakes, and the Atlantic, Pacific, and Gulf coasts. Identified in the plaintiff-categories chapter as the trade body that would carry the associational claim under Hunt v. Washington State Apple Advertising Commission for the independent tug and barge operator whose vessel is built around a marine diesel governed by EPA’s Part 1042 standards.

https://www.americanwaterways.com/.

Andrus v. Allard, 444 U.S. 51 (1979). The unanimous Supreme Court decision holding that a federal regulation banning the commercial sale of eagle feathers lawfully obtained before the Eagle Protection Act’s effective date was not a Fifth Amendment taking, because possession and non-commercial transfer were preserved. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the leading adverse precedent for the Takings Clause prong of the book’s argument.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/444/51/.

Anthropic. “Claude Mythos Preview and Project Glasswing.” Announcement, April 7, 2026, as reported in Ravie Lakshmanan, “Anthropic’s Claude Mythos Finds Thousands of Zero-Day Flaws Across Major Systems,” The Hacker News, April 8, 2026. A limited-access frontier model that, with minimal human steering, identified thousands of previously unknown high-severity vulnerabilities across every major operating system and web browser, including a twenty-seven-year-old remote-crash flaw in the TCP stack of OpenBSD — the operating system with the field’s strongest reputation for line-by-line code audit — and a sixteen-year-old flaw in FFmpeg’s H.264 decoder that had survived five million fuzzing runs. Cited in the preface as one of three cases in which a machine found, by reading a system’s rules, a flaw its expert defenders had not.

https://thehackernews.com/2026/04/anthropics-claude-mythos-finds.html.

Aristotle. The Athenian Constitution (Athenaion Politeia). Translated by Frederic G. Kenyon. c. 350 BC. Perseus Digital Library, Tufts University. Reconstructs the procedural mechanisms of classical Athenian deliberative bodies, including the two-urn secret-ballot (kyrios kadiskos and akyros kadiskos) used in the dikasteria and the boule. The mechanical design is the historical antecedent for the secret-ballot procedure used by the Italian parliament for 119 years and proposed in this chapter for Hybrid Voting in the U.S. Congress.

https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/text?doc=Perseus:text:1999.01.0046.

Arkansas Game & Fish Commission v. United States, 568 U.S. 23 (2012).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/568/23/.

Aro Manufacturing Co. v. Convertible Top Replacement Co., 365 U.S. 336 (1961). The Supreme Court’s repair-and-reconstruction decision, arising from replacement tops for Ford and General Motors convertibles. The Court held that replacing a worn, unpatented element of a patented combination is permissible repair, not infringing reconstruction, and that no unpatented element of a combination is entitled to the patent monopoly, however essential to the combination it may be. Cited for the principle that a combination patent reaches only the totality of its claimed elements.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/365/336/.

Aronson v. Quick Point Pencil Co., 440 U.S. 257 (1979). The modern Supreme Court restatement of the Patterson v. Kentucky principle that states may regulate the use of intellectual property in any manner not inconsistent with federal patent law. Cited in the chapter on objections considered to establish the long-standing rule that patents do not confer immunity from generally applicable regulation.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/440/257/.

Asch, Solomon E. “Studies of Independence and Conformity: A Minority of One Against a Unanimous Majority.” Psychological Monographs: General and Applied 70, no. 9 (1956): 1–70.

https://psycnet.apa.org/doiLanding?doi=10.1037%2Fh0093718.

Association for Accessible Medicines. The U.S. Generic & Biosimilar Medicines Savings Report. Annual report. Source for the cumulative consumer-savings figure attributed to the Drug Price Competition and Patent Term Restoration Act of 1984 (the Hatch-Waxman Act), reported in the trillion-dollar range cumulatively since the statute’s passage. Cited in the policy-framework chapter for the Hatch-Waxman precedent.

https://accessiblemeds.org/sites/default/files/2023-09/AAM-2023-Generic-Biosimilar-Medicines-Savings-Report-web.pdf.

Aggregated via OpenSecrets.org. The trade association representing the major appliance manufacturers selling into the United States market reported total federal lobbying expenditures of $1,104,386 in calendar year 2019; comparable annual totals have been reported in adjacent years.

https://www.opensecrets.org/orgs/association-of-home-appliance-mfrs/summary?id=D000024172.

Attia, Zachi I., et al. “Screening for Cardiac Contractile Dysfunction Using an Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Electrocardiogram.” Nature Medicine 25 (2019): 70-74. The derivation study behind the Mayo Clinic electrocardiogram work discussed in the chapter on outsiders and blind doctrine.

Aurora Innovation, Inc. Corporate page and commercial-operations record. Aurora Innovation is the autonomous-freight company that began hauling commercial loads without a driver in the cab on the Dallas–Houston corridor of Interstate 45 in 2025; trade reporting places its Driver-as-a-Service fee near $0.85 per mile. Cited in the autonomous-vehicle chapter for the structure of the autonomous-trucking fee.

Automatic Emergency Braking Rule (FMVSS No. 127). Final Rule, “Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards; Automatic Emergency Braking Systems for Light Vehicles,” 89 Fed. Reg. 39686 (May 9, 2024). National Highway Traffic Safety Administration. Compliance required by September 1, 2029, under the mandate at 49 U.S.C. § 30129 added by the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act of 2021.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/05/09/2024-09462/federal-motor-vehicle-safety-standards-automatic-emergency-braking-systems-for-light-vehicles.

Axios. “The Math Is Improving for Autonomous Trucks.” Axios, May 6, 2026. Trade reporting on Goldman Sachs Research projections for autonomous-versus-human trucking costs, including the projected 2028 cost crossover, early single-route claims of sub-$2.00-per-mile driverless operation, the projected fall in the autonomous hardware premium to roughly $35,000 to $40,000 by 2035, and the American Transportation Research Institute figure of about $2.26 per mile for a human-driven truck. Cited in the autonomous-vehicle chapter on the crossover projection.

https://www.axios.com/2026/05/06/autonomous-trucks-av-freight.

Azoulay, Pierre, Christian Fons-Rosen, and Joshua S. Graff Zivin. “Does Science Advance One Funeral at a Time?” American Economic Review 109, no. 8 (August 2019): 2889-2920. Four hundred fifty-two academic life scientists who died prematurely; publication by scientists who had never collaborated with the deceased rose 8.6 percent on average, and entry was most suppressed where the deceased scientist’s collaborators held gatekeeping positions.

https://doi.org/10.1257/aer.20161574.

Backup Camera Rule (FMVSS No. 111). Final Rule, “Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards; Rear Visibility,” 79 Fed. Reg. 19178 (April 7, 2014). National Highway Traffic Safety Administration.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2014/04/07/2014-07469/federal-motor-vehicle-safety-standards-rear-visibility.

Baker v. Selden, 101 U.S. 99 (1879). Justice Bradley, for the Court, held that the copyright in a book explaining a system of bookkeeping gave its author no exclusive right to the system itself; copyright protects the author’s explanation, and the method or art the book describes is open to the public unless secured by patent. The foundational statement that copyright reaches expression and not function.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/101/99/.

Bandura, Albert. Moral Disengagement: How People Do Harm and Live with Themselves. New York: Worth Publishers, 2016.

https://albertbandura.com/albert-bandura-moral-disengagement.html.

Beck, Andrew H., Ankur R. Sangoi, Samuel Leung, Robert J. Marinelli, Torsten O. Nielsen, Marc J. van de Vijver, Robert B. West, Matt van de Rijn, and Daphne Koller. “Systematic Analysis of Breast Cancer Morphology Uncovers Stromal Features Associated with Survival.” Science Translational Medicine 3, no. 108 (November 9, 2011): 108ra113. The C-Path (Computational Pathologist) study. Breast cancer had been graded on a small set of features of the cancer epithelium under a scheme largely unchanged since the 1920s. The system measured 6,642 features of each slide across both epithelium and stroma, was trained on the Netherlands Cancer Institute cohort (n = 248) and evaluated on the independent Vancouver General Hospital cohort (n = 328), and found three stromal features whose association with overall survival was stronger than that of the epithelial characteristics in the model — identifying stromal morphology as a prognostic determinant not previously recognized. Cited in the preface as the case in which a machine did not solve what specialists could not, but looked where specialists had not thought to look.

https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22072638/

Bellan, Rebecca. “OpenAI Claims It Solved an 80-Year-Old Math Problem — For Real This Time.” TechCrunch, May 20, 2026. Reports OpenAI’s announcement that its reasoning model produced an original mathematical proof disproving Paul Erdős’s 1946 planar-unit-distance conjecture, with companion remarks supporting the disproof from mathematicians Noga Alon, Melanie Wood, and Thomas Bloom. OpenAI characterized the result as “the first time AI has autonomously solved a prominent open problem” in mathematics. Source for the preface’s reference to the moment AI tools began solving problems unsolved by human effort alone.

https://techcrunch.com/2026/05/20/openai-claims-it-solved-an-80-year-old-math-problem-for-real-this-time/.

Bharat NCAP. “AIS 197.” Notification page, Bharat New Car Assessment Programme, Ministry of Road Transport and Highways, Government of India. Bharat NCAP star ratings are awarded on cars that manufacturers voluntarily submit, assessed against the AIS-197 laboratory test protocol by an agency authorized under Rule 126 of the Central Motor Vehicles Rules, 1989. Cited in the international-comparison chapter to distinguish a consumer safety assessment from a statutory type-approval certificate: a Bharat NCAP fact sheet is the former and not the latter, and the chapter’s claim about the Thar ROXX is stated as an independent assessment rather than as a certification.

https://www.bncap.in/notifications/ais-197/.

Bharat NCAP. “Mahindra Thar ROXX.” Vehicle test report, Bharat New Car Assessment Programme, Ministry of Road Transport and Highways, Government of India, November 2024. The official BNCAP page documents the five-star ratings for adult and child occupant protection awarded under the BNCAP testing protocol.

https://www.bncap.in/vehicle/mahindra-thar-roxx/.

Bicycle Blue Book. “2005 Trek Madone 5.2.” Value Guide, Bicycle Blue Book, model-year 2005 listing. Pricing reference for the 2005 Trek Madone 5.2 ($3,400–$3,629 new in 2005) cited in the bicycles section of the modern-bargain chapter as the patented-era counterpart to the 2026 SAVA Warwind V3.

https://www.bicyclebluebook.com/value-guide/2005%20Trek%20Madone%205.2.

Bizzozero, Giulio. “Ueber die schlauchförmigen Drüsen des Magendarmkanals und die Beziehungen ihres Epithels zu dem Oberflächenepithel der Schleimhaut.” Archiv für mikroskopische Anatomie 42 (1893): 82–152. The organisms Bizzozero described were in the stomachs of dogs, not humans; the chapter on outsiders and blind doctrine states this, because the popular retelling does not. Known here through later histories of Helicobacter pylori and not consulted in the original.

Black & Decker portable electric drill, 1916. S. Duncan Black and Alonzo G. Decker devised a pistol grip and trigger switch in 1914 that let one hand control the tool, paired it with a universal motor able to run on alternating or direct current, and began manufacturing their half-inch portable drill in 1916; the patent issued as U.S. Patent No. 1,245,860. Electric drills existed well before then, but as large stationary machines confined to industrial plants. Cited in the preface for the date the portable power tool became available, as against the much later date at which the building trades took it up. National Inventors Hall of Fame, “S. Duncan Black,” inducted 2019.

https://www.invent.org/inductees/s-duncan-black.

Bolling v. Sharpe, 347 U.S. 497 (1954). The federal companion to Brown v. Board of Education, decided the same day, holding that the Fifth Amendment’s Due Process Clause incorporates an equal-protection guarantee against the federal government, since the Fourteenth Amendment binds only states. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the doctrinal foundation that makes any federal-equal-protection framing of the regulatory wall possible at all.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/347/497/.

Bonito Boats, Inc. v. Thunder Craft Boats, Inc., 489 U.S. 141 (1989).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/489/141/.

Boston Consulting Group. “Is There a Future for Service Stations?” July 2019. Scenario analysis of fuel-retail network economics under combined electrification, shared mobility, and vehicle autonomy. States that the refueling or recharging of shared-mobility autonomous vehicles will commonly occur while the vehicles are empty of passengers, at dedicated parking areas outside urban areas, and that the result will be a decline in customer traffic at service stations and lower fuel and convenience-store sales. In its faster environments the report puts station throughput down 30 to 50 percent, gasoline demand for light-duty vehicles down 50 to 70 percent by 2035, and up to 80 percent of an unadapted fuel-retail network unprofitable within about fifteen years. Cited in The Last Human Driver for the effect of hired autonomous fleets on the roadside economy. The report does not break out a separate figure for in-store merchandise or foodservice sales; searches in September 2026 across consultancies, oil majors, trade associations, and transport journals located no published study that does.

https://web-assets.bcg.com/img-src/BCG-Is-There-a-Future-for-Service-Stations-July-2019_tcm9-223783.pdf

Boulware, David R., Matthew F. Pullen, Ananta S. Bangdiwala, et al. “A Randomized Trial of Hydroxychloroquine as Postexposure Prophylaxis for Covid-19.” New England Journal of Medicine 383, no. 6 (August 6, 2020): 517–25. The trial most often cited as establishing that hydroxychloroquine does not work as post-exposure prophylaxis. Cited in the hindered-sector chapter as the exhibit for how a null result can be produced by trial design rather than by the molecule.

https://doi.org/10.1056/NEJMoa2016638. See Endnote Commentary.

Boyle, Willard S., and George E. Smith. “Buried Channel Charge Coupled Devices.” U.S. Patent 3,792,322, filed April 19, 1973, issued February 12, 1974. Assigned to Bell Telephone Laboratories, Inc. Foundational charge-coupled-device imaging patent in the corpus underlying automotive backup-camera image sensors; expired in the 1990s under the pre-1995 seventeen-year-from-issue patent term. Cited in the surveillance chapter as one of the foundational expired imaging patents from which a remanufactured vehicle’s backup camera can be built.

https://patents.google.com/patent/US3792322.

Betts v. Brady, 316 U.S. 455 (1942). The Supreme Court’s holding that due process did not require a State to appoint counsel for an indigent defendant in a noncapital case. Justice Black dissented, joined by Justices Douglas and Murphy. Overruled twenty-one years later in Gideon v. Wainwright (1963).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/316/455/.

Bowers v. Hardwick, 478 U.S. 186 (1986). The Supreme Court’s decision upholding a Georgia sodomy statute, stating that proscriptions against such conduct had ancient roots. Overruled in Lawrence v. Texas (2003) after a brief filed by historians established that the sources had been in print throughout and did not say what the opinion said they said.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/478/186/.

bp p.l.c. “Energy Outlook: Transport.” bp Energy Outlook, annual; transport section consulted September 2026. Models the share of passenger-car vehicle-kilometres powered by electricity that is driven by robotaxis, reaching roughly 40 percent by 2035 in the Rapid and Net Zero scenarios and about 20 percent in the business-as-usual case, with robotaxis used up to nine times as intensively as private cars. Cited in The Last Human Driver for the scale of hired-fleet mileage. bp owns TravelCenters of America; the Outlook models the demand side of this shift and publishes no companion estimate of its effect on the company’s retail business.

https://www.bp.com/en/global/corporate/energy-economics/energy-outlook/demand-by-sector/transport.html

Brulotte v. Thys Co., 379 U.S. 29 (1964). The Supreme Court’s holding that a patentee may not collect royalties for use of a patented invention after the patent has expired, even where the parties agreed to the arrangement by contract. The patents in the case expired on schedule and no term was extended; the Court struck the royalty provision because the practical consequence of expiration had been withheld by another instrument. Reaffirmed on stare decisis grounds in Kimble v. Marvel Entertainment (2015).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/379/29/.

Six years passed before the rules were finalized. The article documents the lawsuit filed by the Specialty Equipment Market Association (SEMA) that forced NHTSA’s January 2020 publication of the proposed rule. The DeLorean Motor Company of Texas, which had championed the legislation and announced plans for spring-2017 production of new DMC-12s, ultimately placed the project on indefinite hold in October 2022 because of supplier-relationship collapse and engine-certification difficulties produced by the regulatory paralysis. City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, Inc., 473 U.S. 432 (1985). The Supreme Court decision applying rational-basis review with unusual rigor to strike down a municipal zoning ordinance that excluded a group home for persons with intellectual disabilities. The Court found that the ordinance’s only conceivable rationale was prejudice against an unpopular group, which is not a legitimate state interest. The case is the cleanest illustration of the “rational basis with bite” doctrine that occasionally allows an equal-protection challenge to economic or social classifications to succeed where animus is identifiable. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the narrow escape route from Williamson v. Lee Optical, the conditions for which the regulatory wall in this book’s argument does not satisfy.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/473/432/.

Code of Federal Regulations. “40 C.F.R. § 1068.120 — Requirements for Rebuilding Engines.” U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. Requires anyone rebuilding an engine to have a reasonable technical basis for knowing that its emission controls perform at least as well as in its certified configuration, and requires an engine that stays in the same equipment to be rebuilt to its original configuration or to a certified configuration of the same or a later model year. Cited in the Patent Clause prong chapter for the point that a rebuilt engine is held to the standards of its own model year, not the current ones.

https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-U/part-1068/subpart-B/section-1068.120.

Code of Federal Regulations. “40 C.F.R. § 1068.240 — Exempting New Replacement Engines.” U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. Lets a certificate holder, and no one else, build a new engine to the configuration of an earlier model year with less stringent standards, solely to replace an engine already placed into service, in equipment no more than forty years old, and only after determining that no engine certified to current standards has the physical or performance characteristics to fit. Cited in the Patent Clause prong chapter as a federal time-shift for engines, confined to a certificate holder and to machines already built.

https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-U/part-1068/subpart-C/section-1068.240. See Endnote Commentary.

The general-compliance regulatory framework that sets EPA’s requirements for rebuilding engines (§ 1068.120) and its exemption for new replacement engines (§ 1068.240), cited in the trucking chapter as a regulatory model for independent engineering certification of patent-expired heavy-duty truck platforms.

Coke, Edward. The Third Part of the Institutes of the Laws of England. 2nd ed. London: M. Flesher for W. Lee and D. Pakeman, 1648. Chapter “Of Monopolies.” Coke defines a monopoly as an allowance by the King, “by his grant, commission, or otherwise,” to “any person or persons” for “the sole buying, selling, making, working, or using of any thing,” whereby others “are sought to be restrained of any freedom or liberty that they had before.” The test turns on the restraint placed on everyone else, not on how many hold the grant. The passage falls at page 181 of the 1669 edition. Cited in the Preface for the definition behind the book’s title, and in the chapter Four Problems, One Remedy, which answers the same objection by back-reference.

https://scholarship.law.wm.edu/british-colonial-antecedents/32/.

Collins, Andrew P. “Ford CEO Jim Farley’s Right To Repair Comment Should Make Every Car Owner Uncomfortable.” The Drive, June 11, 2026. Transcribes the exchange between Ford chief executive Jim Farley and a Detroit Free Press reporter at an event announcing a $5 million Ford and Bloomberg Philanthropies investment in the Detroit Public Schools Community District Foundation’s auto technician program, days after the June 3, 2026 White House meeting between the administration and automobile executives on right-to-repair policy. Asked whether he wanted owners repairing their own vehicles, Farley drew the distinction the foreword turns on: he says he has no difficulty working on a 1973 Bronco, but that a current-model Bronco requires specialty tools and that owner repair of it would put lives at risk. The admission is that the older machine remains serviceable by its owner and the new one does not — a difference of regulatory and electronic architecture, not of mechanical necessity. The foreword by Gord Magill quotes the exchange from this transcription. Road & Track reported the same footage under the headline “Ford CEO Jim Farley Clarifies Blue Oval’s Position on ‘Right to Repair’ Rules After Trump Comments” and renders Farley’s opening sentence differently, so The Drive is the source of record for the wording used. The underlying video, published June 8, 2026, is at

https://www.freep.com/videos/money/cars/ford/2026/06/08/ford-ceo-jim-farley-right-to-repair/90465881007/. This footnote is also for the Foreword. https://www.thedrive.com/news/ford-ceo-jim-farleys-right-to-repair-comment-should-make-every-car-owner-uncomfortable.

Compco Corp. v. Day-Brite Lighting, Inc., 376 U.S. 234 (1964). Companion case to Sears, Roebuck & Co. v. Stiffel Co., decided the same day. Together the two cases established that the federal patent system’s policy of leaving the unpatented public domain free for all to copy is supreme over state unfair-competition laws that would prohibit such copying. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as part of the Supreme Court doctrinal line that supports the book’s structural Patent Clause argument.

Congressional Research Service. Diesel Fuel and Engines: An Analysis of EPA’s New Regulations. CRS Report RL30737. May 1, 2001. Neutral congressional analytical document reviewing the EPA heavy-duty diesel rule signed December 21, 2000 by Administrator Carol Browner and promulgated as final rule at 66 Fed. Reg. 5002 (January 18, 2001). The report explicitly characterizes industry reactions to the rule as “mixed,” documenting concerns expressed by engine manufacturers and refiners about technological feasibility, cost, and the diesel-fuel sulfur-reduction component, all addressed by EPA through its formal Response to Comments document published with the final rule under the notice-and-comment rulemaking process of the Administrative Procedure Act, 5 U.S.C. § 553. Connecticut Humanities. “Albert Augustus Pope, Transportation Pioneer.” ConnecticutHistory.org. Pope began by importing English bicycles in the 1870s and contracting the Weed Sewing Machine Company of Hartford to build them under the Columbia name. By 1892 he was selling a safety bicycle that carried the Starley frame, tangential spoke lacing, the Renold chain, and pneumatic tires — British designs, every one, built in Connecticut. That same year he took majority control of the Hartford Rubber Works, then employing roughly one hundred seventy people and running around the clock, along with a steel company and a nickel-plating works. The strategy was vertical integration, and it worked because the underlying designs cost him nothing. A firm that had to license the frame, the chain, and the tire could not have absorbed its suppliers; it would have been paying rent on the machine itself.

https://connecticuthistory.org/albert-augustus-pope-1843-1909/.

Conrad, Kylie, and John D. Graham. “The Benefits and Costs of Automotive Regulations for Low-Income Americans.” Journal of Benefit-Cost Analysis 12, no. 3 (Fall 2021): 518–549. Aggregates per-vehicle regulatory-cost data from agency Regulatory Impact Assessments compiled in the Office of Management and Budget’s annual reports to Congress, covering the period 1967–2015. Table 5 (p. 535) itemizes the per-vehicle cumulative cost addition in 2016 dollars: $2,708.23 for safety regulations, $1,049.19 for emissions regulations, and $2,439.14 for fuel-economy regulations, summing to $6,196.56. The authors describe the total burden as “in the vicinity of $6,000 to $7,000 per vehicle” and note that, against an average new-vehicle transaction price “approaching $40,000,” cumulative federal regulation accounts for 15 to 20 percent of the price the consumer pays. The cars-and-trucks chapter uses the Conrad and Graham (2021) figure as the post-1970 cost magnitude and Sperling et al. (2004) as the proportional-attribution evidence that essentially all of that cumulative burden was added after 1970.

https://doi.org/10.1017/bca.2021.12.

Consumer Product Safety Act of 1972, Pub. L. No. 92-573, 86 Stat. 1207. Codified as amended at 15 U.S.C. §§ 2051 et seq. Establishes the Consumer Product Safety Commission and authorizes the issuance of mandatory product-safety standards and recalls under section 15.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/chapter-47.

Consumer Product Safety Commission. “Requirements for Bicycles.” 16 C.F.R. Part 1512. First promulgated at 43 Fed. Reg. 60034 (December 22, 1978), with amendments in 1980, 1981, 1995, and 2003. Bicycles were among the first product categories the Commission regulated after Congress created it in 1972. The date carries the weight here. Every American bicycle discussed in this chapter’s historical section — the Columbia safeties of the 1890s, the Indianapolis chain that went to Kitty Hawk, the balloon-tire machines of the 1930s, the middleweights and lightweights of the 1950s — was designed, built, and sold before any federal design standard for bicycles existed. Secondary accounts disagree about whether the operative date is 1975, 1976, or 1978, and the Commission’s own documents are not consistent; the Federal Register citation above is the one to rely on.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2011/05/13/2011-11742/requirements-for-bicycles.

Continental Paper Bag Co. v. Eastern Paper Bag Co., 210 U.S. 405 (1908). Decided June 1, 1908. Held that a patentee who has never used its invention may still enjoin others from using it: the right to exclude is “the very essence of the right conferred by the patent,” and nonuse alone is no ground to deny an injunction. Justice McKenna wrote for the Court; Justice Harlan dissented. Cited in the Preface, and in the chapter Four Problems, One Remedy, as the third authority for the book’s title: a monopoly lies in the power to exclude, which needs no production, and the patent’s power to exclude is what outlives the term.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/210/405/.

Corner Post, Inc. v. Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, 603 U.S. 799 (2024). Decided July 1, 2024, three days after Loper Bright and in the same term. Held, 6–3, that a claim under the Administrative Procedure Act accrues when the plaintiff is injured by an agency rule rather than when the rule was issued, so the six-year limitations period of 28 U.S.C. § 2401(a) runs from injury and not from promulgation. Justice Barrett wrote for the Court; Justice Kavanaugh concurred; Justice Jackson dissented, joined by Justices Sotomayor and Kagan. Cited in the filable-case chapter as the companion rule to the end of Chevron deference: the regulatory wall was built between 1970 and 1987, and under the older reading of the limitations period most of it was beyond challenge by anyone not already in court decades ago.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/603/22-1008/.

Corporate Average Fuel Economy Standards for Passenger Cars and Light Trucks for Model Years 2027–2031. Final Rule, 89 Fed. Reg. 52396 (June 24, 2024). National Highway Traffic Safety Administration.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/06/24/2024-13243/corporate-average-fuel-economy-standards-for-passenger-cars-and-light-trucks-for-model-years.

Coudray, Nicolas, et al. “Classification and Mutation Prediction from Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer Histopathology Images Using Deep Learning.” Nature Medicine 24, no. 10 (2018): 1559-1567. Six of the ten most commonly mutated genes in lung adenocarcinoma predicted from stained tissue, with areas under the curve between 0.733 and 0.856.

Declaratory Judgment Act of 1934, 28 U.S.C. §§ 2201–2202. Authorizes federal courts to declare the rights of any party in a case of actual controversy without requiring anyone to first violate the law they seek to challenge. Originally enacted as Pub. L. No. 73-343, 48 Stat. 955 (June 14, 1934).

Diamond Alternative Energy, LLC v. Environmental Protection Agency, 606 U.S. 100 (2025). Seven-to-two decision, June 20, 2025, holding that fuel producers had Article III standing to challenge EPA’s approval of California regulations requiring automakers to build more electric vehicles, although the producers were not the regulated parties and their injury ran through the automakers’ responses. Justice Kavanaugh’s opinion for the Court held that commonsense economic principles, supported by the record, establish causation and redressability where the plaintiff shows a predictable chain of events, and stated that “the government generally may not target a business or industry through stringent and allegedly unlawful regulation, and then evade the resulting lawsuits by claiming that the targets of its regulation should be locked out of court as unaffected bystanders.” Justices Sotomayor and Jackson dissented. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the answer to the objection that a buyer’s injury depends on the independent choices of remanufacturers not before the court.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/606/24-7/.

Diesel Engine Consent Decrees, 1998. United States v. Caterpillar Inc., Cummins Engine Company, Detroit Diesel Corporation, Mack Trucks Inc., Navistar International Transportation Corporation, Renault Véhicules Industriels, and Volvo Truck Corporation. Settlements lodged October 22, 1998, in the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia (Judge Henry H. Kennedy Jr.), entered as final judgments July 1, 1999. The decrees resolved Clean Air Act claims that the seven manufacturers had installed undisclosed defeat-device software (altering fuel injection timing) in heavy-duty diesel engines from the late 1980s through 1998 that allowed engines to pass EPA emissions tests while emitting up to two to three times the NOx standard during normal highway operation. The combined civil penalty was $83.4 million — the largest civil penalty in environmental enforcement history at the time — with twenty-five percent of each company’s share directed to the California Air Resources Board in parallel settlements. Including injunctive relief (accelerated compliance with stricter NOx-plus-NMHC standards by October 2002, new not-to-exceed and emission surface limits, supplemental testing, recalls and fixes for in-use engines, and offset projects), the total settlement cost the manufacturers well over one billion dollars. Source for the consent-decree reference in the trucking chapter and the assertion that the 1998 settlements stripped heavy-duty engine manufacturers of credibility to resist tougher standards.

https://www.epa.gov/enforcement/heavy-duty-diesel-engine-settlements-1998. Companion primary source: U.S. Department of Justice Press Release No. 499 (October 22, 1998), https://www.justice.gov/archive/opa/pr/1998/October/499_enr.htm.

DieselNet. “United States: Heavy-Duty Onroad Engines.” Emission standards reference compiled by Ecopoint Inc. The authoritative public summary of U.S. EPA particulate matter, NOx, and hydrocarbon standards for heavy-duty highway diesel engines from 1988 through the present, cross-referenced against 40 C.F.R. Part 86 and the underlying Federal Register notices. Documents the PM standard progression: 0.60 g/bhp-hr (model years 1988–1990), 0.25 g/bhp-hr (1991–1993), 0.10 g/bhp-hr (1994–2006), and 0.01 g/bhp-hr (2007–onward); and the in-use unregulated baseline of approximately 1.0 g/bhp-hr for pre-1988 engines. Source for Table 16.1 in the trucking chapter and for the chapter’s reconciliation of the eighty-three-percent reduction from the 1988 federal baseline with the approximately ninety-percent reduction from the unregulated pre-1988 in-use level.

https://dieselnet.com/standards/us/hd.php.

DOE, “The History of the Light Bulb.” U.S. Department of Energy. Documents that the first spiral compact fluorescent lamp was created by engineer Edward E. Hammer at General Electric in 1976, in response to the 1973 oil crisis, and that early compact fluorescents reached the consumer market in the 1980s (the first successful integral product being the Philips Model SL, 1980). General Electric patented Hammer’s spiral work; those foundational compact-fluorescent patents, and the integral-lamp, ballast, and phosphor patents underlying the ordinary screw-base compact fluorescent, have long since expired — the latest clearly relevant foundational U.S. patent identified in the author’s review reaching expiration in 2015, and most expiring well before that; that expiration-date synthesis is this argument’s own, drawn from the underlying U.S. patent records rather than from the DOE history. The compact fluorescent was therefore public-domain technology when the April 2024 general service lamp rule raised the efficiency threshold beyond its reach. Cited in the healthy-lighting chapter for the recurrence of the withdrawal pattern in the technology that replaced the incandescent.

https://www.energy.gov/articles/history-light-bulb

DOE Energy Conservation Standards for Consumer Products and Industrial Equipment, 10 C.F.R. Parts 430 and 431. Department of Energy regulations implementing the energy-efficiency provisions of the Energy Policy and Conservation Act of 1975 and its amendments, including the National Appliance Energy Conservation Act of 1987. Cover refrigerators, freezers, room and central air conditioners, water heaters, furnaces, boilers, dishwashers, ovens and ranges, and a long list of other consumer durables.

https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-10/chapter-II/subchapter-D.

DOE General Service Lamps — Backstop Final Rule (2022). Energy Conservation Program: Energy Conservation Standards for General Service Lamps. 87 Fed. Reg. 27439 (May 9, 2022). U.S. Department of Energy (RIN: 1904-AF09). Final rule effective July 25, 2022; full enforcement on manufacture and import beginning January 2023 and on retail and distribution beginning July 2023. Codified the 45 lumens-per-watt minimum efficacy backstop standard for general service lamps under 42 U.S.C. § 6295(i)(6)(A)(v). This is the operative rule that pulled the ordinary incandescent light bulb from the United States residential market and is the principal subject of the healthy-lighting chapter.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2022/05/09/2022-09477/energy-conservation-program-energy-conservation-standards-for-general-service-lamps.

DOE General Service Lamps — Definitions Final Rule (2022). Energy Conservation Program: Definitions for General Service Lamps. 87 Fed. Reg. 27461 (May 9, 2022). U.S. Department of Energy (RIN: 1904-AF22). Final rule effective July 8, 2022. Reinstated the Obama-era GSL and GSIL definitions DOE had withdrawn in September 2019, expanding the regulatory scope to include LED, OLED, decorative candle and globe lamps, and reflector lamps that had been exempt from federal coverage.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2022/05/09/2022-09480/energy-conservation-program-definitions-for-general-service-lamps.

DOE General Service Lamps — 120 Lumens-per-Watt Final Rule (2024). Energy Conservation Program: Energy Conservation Standards for General Service Lamps. 89 Fed. Reg. 28859 (April 19, 2024). U.S. Department of Energy. Final rule effective July 3, 2024; compliance required on and after July 25, 2028. Raises the minimum efficacy threshold from 45 to more than 120 lumens-per-watt, effectively eliminating compact fluorescent lamps as compliant alternatives alongside the ordinary incandescent already eliminated by the 2022 rules.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/04/19/2024-07831/energy-conservation-program-energy-conservation-standards-for-general-service-lamps.

DOE Lighting Market Characterization 2020. U.S. Department of Energy, Office of Energy Efficiency and Renewable Energy, Solid-State Lighting Program, published April 2024 (baseline year 2020). The most recent comprehensive federal characterization of the U.S. lighting market by lamp type, sector, technology, and installed-stock distribution. Documents approximately 819 million incandescent lamps installed in the U.S. residential sector at the 2020 baseline (approximately 12.6 percent of the residential installed lighting stock, comprising approximately 161 million A-shape general service incandescent lamps, 294 million decorative lamps, 334 million reflector lamps, and miscellaneous types). Companion to the 2015 Lighting Market Characterization report. Source for the healthy-lighting chapter’s installed-stock figure documenting the public-right being exercised at the moment the 2022 backstop rule withdrew the manufacturing future of those lamps.

https://www.energy.gov/sites/default/files/2024-08/ssl-lmc2020_apr24.pdf.

DOE Process Rule Notice of Proposed Rulemaking (2026). U.S. Department of Energy, Office of Critical Minerals and Energy Innovation. Notice of Proposed Rulemaking issued July 2, 2026, announced by Secretary of Energy Chris Wright. The Department’s public announcement described the action as moving to end home-appliance and equipment efficiency mandates; the proposed rule itself amends the Department’s internal “Process Rule” governing how future energy-conservation standards are evaluated and does not repeal any existing standard, including the general service lamp standards. Trade-press and legal analysis noted that any subsequent attempt to repeal an existing standard would implicate the anti-backsliding provision at 42 U.S.C. § 6295(o)(1) and would likely be challenged in litigation. Cited in the healthy-lighting chapter as the second test, after Executive Order 14154, of the executive path’s closure.

https://www.energy.gov/documents/process-rule-nopr

Doenges, J. L. Autopsy series reporting spiral organisms in forty-three percent of 242 human stomach specimens, 1938. Cited in the chapter on outsiders and blind doctrine for the fact that the organisms had been counted in a published human series and connected to no disease. Known here through later reviews of Helicobacter pylori; the full citation has not been confirmed against the original.

Dolan v. City of Tigard, 512 U.S. 374 (1994). The Supreme Court’s holding that regulatory conditions imposed on the use of property must be “roughly proportional” to the impact of the proposed use, building on Nollan v. California Coastal Commission and supplying the second prong of the modern proportionality test reaffirmed in Sheetz v. County of El Dorado (2024).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/512/374/.

Drais, Karl von. The running machine Karl von Drais first rode in 1817 and protected by a ten-year Baden privilege in 1818. Baden had no patent law at the time, so what Drais received on 12 January 1818 was a Großherzogliches Privileg — a grand-ducal privilege running ten years and effective only within Baden, which the German Patent and Trade Mark Office describes as the equivalent of a patent today. His first public ride on the Laufmaschine was at Mannheim on 12 June 1817, and a French brevet followed the Baden grant. Cited in the chapters on pre-1900 American prosperity and on the sectors where the bargain still works for the proto-bicycle, or “dandy horse,” that opens the historical lineage running forward through the safety bicycle of the 1880s and 1890s; the design was widely copied in the United States and Britain once the brief and territorially limited term ended. Deutsches Patent- und Markenamt, “World Day of the Bicycle,” last updated 16 February 2026.

https://www.dpma.de/english/our_office/publications/background/sports-leisure-ip/worlddayofthebicycle/index.html.

Drug Price Competition and Patent Term Restoration Act of 1984 (the Hatch-Waxman Act), Pub. L. No. 98-417, 98 Stat. 1585. Codified in part at 21 U.S.C. § 355(j). Created the Abbreviated New Drug Application pathway for generic drug manufacturers, requiring proof of bioequivalence rather than full safety and efficacy review, and restored some patent term consumed by the FDA pre-market approval process for originators. Co-sponsored by Senator Orrin Hatch (R-Utah) and Representative Henry Waxman (D-California). Cited in the policy-framework chapter as the American precedent for opening a blocked patent commons through statutory action; the chapter contrasts the statutory mechanism Hatch-Waxman required with the constitutional remedy this book argues SCOTUS can deliver without congressional action.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/21/355.

Dun & Bradstreet. “Fuel Dealers in the United States — Business Directory.” D&B Business Directory. https://www.dnb.com/business-directory/company-information.fuel_dealers.us.html.

Duniho, Fergus, Jean-Louis Cazaux, and Hans Bodlaender. “Janggi — 장기 — Korean Chess.” The Chess Variant Pages. The principal English-language scholarly reference page on Janggi maintained by the chess-variants research community since the late 1990s; current version mostly rewritten by Duniho, based on earlier text by Cazaux and Bodlaender, and ultimately on Chapter LXXIV (“Tjyang-Keui—Chess”) of Stewart Culin’s Korean Games (1895). The page acknowledges, on its face, that “certain parts of Cazaux’s text were taken from Roleigh Martin and from David B. Pritchard” — placing Martin’s 1995 web page alongside Pritchard’s Encyclopedia of Chess Variants (1994) as a canonical English-language source for the rules of the game. The page further notes that “an earlier version had lifted large portions of text from Roleigh Martin’s page describing Korean Chess, and the rule in question was the interpretation of Martin.” The site’s persons index separately credits Martin as the author of “Korean Chess” (an extensive description) and “Chinese and Korean Chess Links,” with reference to his ShareDebate International. Cited in the preface as the independent third-party corroboration of the author’s claim to have published the first comprehensive English-language webpage on Korean Chess.

https://www.chessvariants.com/oriental.dir/koreanchess.html.

Dunlop, John Boyd. Pneumatic tire. U.S. Patent 435,995, granted 1890. The specification describes an expansible rubber tube inside a protective cover reinforced with cloth or canvas. Dunlop had patented the design in Great Britain in December 1888, and British courts invalidated that patent in 1890 on Robert William Thomson’s prior art — Thomson having patented the same principle in France in 1846 and the United States in 1847. The American patent is the one that matters for this chapter’s argument. A Scottish veterinary surgeon living in Belfast, holding no American rights by virtue of his British filing, came to the United States Patent Office and accepted the same terms the Constitution offers every inventor. Seventeen years from grant, the design belonged to the American public. https://patents.google.com/patent/US435995A/en.

Eastern Railroad Presidents Conference v. Noerr Motor Freight, Inc., 365 U.S. 127 (1961), and United Mine Workers v. Pennington, 381 U.S. 657 (1965). Together the source of the Noerr-Pennington doctrine, under which concerted efforts to petition the government for favorable legislation or regulation are immune from antitrust liability, even where the petitioner’s purpose is to disadvantage a competitor. Cited in Objections Considered as the doctrine that would defeat an antitrust theory built on the lobbying activity described in the chapter, Why Nobody Noticed.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/365/127/.

Eldred v. Ashcroft, 537 U.S. 186 (2003). Seven-to-two decision upholding the 1998 Sonny Bono Copyright Term Extension Act against a “limited Times” challenge under Article I, Section 8, Clause 8. Justice Ginsburg’s majority opinion held that an extension is permissible as long as the resulting term remains “limited, not perpetual.” Justices Stevens and Breyer dissented; Breyer’s dissent argued that the extension produced a term worth 99.8 percent of the value of a perpetual copyright. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the leading adverse precedent for the Patent Clause structural prong, and distinguished there as a copyright case in a calendar-adjustment posture rather than a patent case in a structural-ratchet posture.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/537/186/.

Electronic Code of Federal Regulations. “49 CFR Part 562 — Lighting and Marking on Agricultural Equipment.” National Highway Traffic Safety Administration. Codifies the lighting and marking standards adopted by NHTSA in 2016 under the Moving Ahead for Progress in the 21st Century Act (MAP-21) and discussed in the farm-equipment chapter as the third of the three regulatory layers that captured the American tractor.
