Edited volume of essays by political theorists on the effects of voting transparency in deliberative bodies. Includes Daniela Giannetti’s chapter documenting the use of voto segreto in the post-war Italian parliament as a strategic tool of legislators against external coercive pressures, the scholarly basis for the Hybrid Voting chapter’s reference to the parliament’s 119-year use of the practice.

https://www.amazon.com/dp/1107084334. See Endnote Commentary.

Emissions standards, 2010 versus current. A vehicle certified around 2010 met the federal Tier 2 program, whose fleet-average standard was 0.07 grams per mile NOx (Bin 5); California’s LEV II program reached full phase-in the same year, placing much of the fleet at ULEV levels. The current Tier 3 program sets a fleet-average NMOG-plus-NOx target of 0.030 grams per mile, reached in model year 2025, and lowered gasoline sulfur from 30 to 10 parts per million beginning in 2017, while extending required emission-control durability from 120,000 to 150,000 miles. A remanufactured vehicle built on a roughly 2010-era patent-expired platform is therefore about one regulatory generation behind a new vehicle certified to the current standard, and is no dirtier than the vehicles of that generation already lawful on the road.

https://www.epa.gov/regulations-emissions-vehicles-and-engines/final-rule-control-air-pollution-motor-vehicles-tier-3.

Encyclopedia of Indianapolis. “Diamond Chain Company.” Indianapolis: Indiana Historical Society and IU Indianapolis. Hans Renold’s roller chain, developed in Manchester, made the chain-driven safety bicycle possible, and imported chain stayed beyond the reach of most buyers. On December 24, 1890, Arthur C. Newby, Edward C. Fletcher, and Glenn Howe invested five thousand dollars to begin manufacturing bicycle chain in Indianapolis, starting with four machines and four operators in rented rooms above a tinner’s shop. The encyclopedia states the consequence plainly: manufacturing the drive chains in Indianapolis made bicycles affordable to the masses in the 1890s. By 1904 the firm produced two-thirds of the bicycle chain used in America, and it grew to supply roughly sixty percent of all bicycles sold in the country. Wilbur and Orville Wright, running a bicycle shop in Dayton, were agents for the company; seven specially designed chains drove the propellers of their flying machine at Kitty Hawk in 1903. The company was later renamed Diamond Chain. No license was sought and none was needed.

https://indyencyclopedia.org/diamond-chain-company/.

Energy Independence and Security Act of 2007, Pub. L. No. 110-140, 121 Stat. 1492. Codified as amended at scattered sections of 42 U.S.C., including the lighting-efficiency provisions amending the Energy Policy and Conservation Act at 42 U.S.C. §§ 6291 et seq.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/chapter-77.

Energy Information Administration. “Electric Power Monthly, Table 5.6.A: Average Price of Electricity to Ultimate Customers by End-Use Sector.” U.S. Department of Energy. Monthly publication of average retail electricity prices to U.S. ultimate customers by end-use sector and state. Source for the healthy-lighting chapter’s residential-electricity-rate progression: annual averages of 15.04 cents per kilowatt-hour (2022), 16.00 cents per kilowatt-hour (2023), 16.48 cents per kilowatt-hour (2024), and 17.30 cents per kilowatt-hour (2025) — a fifteen-percent increase across the period in which DOE’s general-service-lamp rule savings were projected to accrue.

https://www.eia.gov/electricity/monthly/epm_table_grapher.php?t=epmt_5_6_a.

Energy Policy and Conservation Act of 1975, Pub. L. No. 94-163, 89 Stat. 871. Codified as amended at various sections of 42 U.S.C. and 49 U.S.C., including the Corporate Average Fuel Economy program at 49 U.S.C. § 32902.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/49/32902.

Environmental Protection Agency. “Heavy-Duty Engine and Vehicle Standards and Highway Diesel Fuel Sulfur Control Requirements: Regulatory Impact Analysis.” EPA420-R-00-026. December 2000. The Regulatory Impact Analysis accompanying the heavy-duty diesel rule signed December 21, 2000 by Administrator Carol Browner and promulgated as final rule at 66 Fed. Reg. 5002 (January 18, 2001). Chapter VII (Benefit-Cost Analysis), Section D, Table VII-19 documents the primary estimate of 8,300 PM₂.₅-related avoided premature deaths annually at full implementation in 2030 from the combined engine/vehicle standards and 15 ppm diesel fuel sulfur requirements. Premature mortality comprises approximately eighty-nine percent of the rule’s approximately $70.4 billion in total monetized annual benefits. Alternative concentration-response functions in the RIA produce ranges from approximately 9,400 to 24,200; 8,300 is the central-case primary estimate. Source for the trucking chapter’s reference to EPA’s projected 8,300 premature deaths prevented annually.

https://nepis.epa.gov/Exe/ZyPDF.cgi/P100K576.PDF?Dockey=P100K576.pdf.

Environmental Protection Agency. “Civil Monetary Penalty Inflation Adjustment.” Final Rule, 90 Fed. Reg. 1375 (January 8, 2025). The annual rulemaking that updates the table in 40 C.F.R. § 19.4 with the inflation-adjusted civil penalty maxima EPA uses across its statutes, including the Clean Air Act tampering provisions. The 2025 adjusted figures (the most recent currently in effect: under Office of Management and Budget Memorandum M-26-11, no further cost-of-living adjustment was issued for 2026) set the Clean Air Act § 203 tampering maximum at $5,911 per violation for an individual and $59,114 per violation for a manufacturer or dealer. Source for the current per-violation tampering penalty figures cited in the trucking chapter.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/01/08/2025-00206/civil-monetary-penalty-inflation-adjustment.

Environmental Protection Agency. Our Nation’s Air: Trends Through 2024. Annual report on national criteria-pollutant trends. U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, 2025.

https://gispub.epa.gov/air/trendsreport/.

Environmental Protection Agency. “Administrator Zeldin Takes Additional Measures to Address Diesel Exhaust Fluid (DEF) Issues for American Farmers and Truckers.” Press release, U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, February 3, 2026. The follow-up to the August 2025 guidance reversing five-mile-per-hour DEF derates. Announces a Section 208(a) information-collection demand against the top fourteen on-road and nonroad diesel manufacturers and previews rulemaking to permanently eliminate DEF derates from new and existing trucks. Source for the trucking chapter’s account of the EPA-path actions through February 2026.

https://www.epa.gov/newsreleases/administrator-zeldin-takes-additional-measures-address-diesel-exhaust-fluid-def-issues.

Environmental Protection Agency. The 2026 EPA Automotive Trends Report. EPA-420-R-26-001. Washington, DC: U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, February 2026. Annual EPA report documenting trends in light-duty vehicle fuel economy, emissions, and technology adoption from model year 1975 forward. Source for the manual-transmission share trajectory cited in the surveillance chapter: 34.6 percent of new American light-duty vehicles in 1980; 22.2 percent in 1990; 9.7 percent in 2000; 3.8 percent in 2010; 1.1 percent in 2020; 0.9 percent in 2024.

https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2026-02/420r26001.pdf.

Environmental Protection Agency. Battery Collection Best Practices Report to Congress. Washington, DC: U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, May 2026. The report Congress required under Section 70401(b) of the 2021 Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act. It finds no widely accepted U.S. recycling rate for lithium-ion batteries, notes that the commonly cited five-percent figure is European and nearly a decade old, and states that used electric-vehicle batteries are only beginning to enter the waste stream with no collection system of their own yet in place — against a 99 percent industry-reported recycling rate for the lead-acid battery in a conventional vehicle. Cited in the environmental chapter for the point that the technology the regulatory wall favors carries lifecycle costs no regulation prices.

https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2026-05/battery-collection-best-practices-report-to-congress_508.pdf.

Environmental Protection Agency. “Regulations for Emissions from Heavy Equipment with Compression-Ignition Engines.” U.S. EPA portal for the nonroad compression-ignition (diesel) emissions program, codifying Tier 1 (1994), Tiers 2 and 3 (1998), and Tier 4 (2004) standards expressly covering farm tractors and other nonroad agricultural equipment. Source for the second of the three regulatory layers discussed in the farm-equipment chapter.

https://www.epa.gov/regulations-emissions-vehicles-and-engines/regulations-emissions-heavy-equipment-compression.

Environmental Protection Agency. “Regulations for Emissions from Marine Compression-Ignition (Diesel) Engines.” Codified at 40 C.F.R. Part 1042. U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. The marine compression-ignition (diesel) emissions program governing engines installed in commercial vessels such as tugs, towboats, and barges; the program’s near-term Tier 3 and longer-term Tier 4 aftertreatment-based standards for engines under thirty liters per cylinder were finalized in the June 30, 2008 final rule (73 Fed. Reg. 37243). Cited in the plaintiff-categories chapter as the federal hook for the independent tug and barge operator, distinct from the Part 1039 nonroad rule that governs land-based heavy equipment.

https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-U/part-1042.

EPA Final Rule for Control of Emissions of Air Pollution From Highway Heavy-Duty Engines. 62 Fed. Reg. 54694 (October 21, 1997). U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. The 1997 final rule that established the 2.4 g/bhp-hr NMHC+NOx standard (or the alternative 2.5 g/bhp-hr with a 0.5 g NMHC cap) for heavy-duty diesel engines beginning with the 2004 model year — a fifty-percent reduction from the 1998 NOx standard of 4.0 g/bhp-hr. The October 1998 Consent Decrees pulled the 2004 standard forward by fifteen months to October 2002, the implementation point that pushed every signatory toward cooled exhaust gas recirculation. Source for the trucking chapter’s account of the first wave of post-1999 regulatory tightening that ended the mechanical-engine era of heavy-duty diesel and defines the 1999 model year as the dream-truck cutoff.

https://www.epa.gov/regulations-emissions-vehicles-and-engines/final-rule-control-emissions-air-pollution-highway-heavy.

EPA Heavy-Duty Highway Engine and Vehicle Standards and Highway Diesel Fuel Sulfur Control Requirements. Final Rule, 66 Fed. Reg. 5002 (January 18, 2001). U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. The 2001 final rule (commonly referenced as the 2007 rule for its principal compliance date) established the 0.01 grams per brake-horsepower-hour particulate-matter standard that made diesel particulate filters and the full aftertreatment stack effectively mandatory on every Class 8 truck sold after 2007. Source for the trucking chapter’s account of the regulatory transition from engine-design cleanup to mandatory aftertreatment.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2001/01/18/01-2/control-of-air-pollution-from-new-motor-vehicles-heavy-duty-engine-and-vehicle-standards-and-highway.

EPA Standards for Small Nonroad Spark-Ignition Engines and Equipment. Phase 1 final rule, 60 Fed. Reg. 34581 (July 3, 1995). Phase 3 final rule, 73 Fed. Reg. 59259 (October 8, 2008). Codified at 40 C.F.R. Part 1054. U.S. Environmental Protection Agency. Standards apply to engines used in lawn and garden equipment, snow blowers, and other small nonroad applications.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2008/10/08/E8-21093/control-of-emissions-from-nonroad-spark-ignition-engines-and-equipment.

Epstein, Richard A. Takings: Private Property and the Power of Eminent Domain. Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 1985. The leading libertarian-property-rights statement of regulatory-takings doctrine; counter-authority to Joseph Sax’s narrower police-power analysis. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as part of the doctrinal landscape against which the Takings Clause prong of the book’s argument is built.

https://www.hup.harvard.edu/books/9780674867307.

Equal Access to Justice Act, 28 U.S.C. § 2412; 5 U.S.C. § 504. The statute waiving federal sovereign immunity for attorney-fee awards to parties who prevail in non-tort civil litigation against the United States. Recoverable fees are capped at $125 per hour, subject to increase for cost of living or a special factor; recovery is limited to individuals with a net worth of $2 million or less and to businesses worth $7 million or less with no more than 500 employees, while organizations exempt under Internal Revenue Code § 501(c)(3) are not subject to those caps. No award is available if the government shows its litigating position was substantially justified, and fee shifting runs one way only. Cited in Objections Considered for the proposition that the fee structure, rather than the merits, explains why no firm has brought the Patent Clause case since the doctrinal obstacle to it was removed in 2024.

https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/IF11246.

Erlich, Mark. With Our Hands: The Story of Carpenters in Massachusetts. Philadelphia: Temple University Press, 1986. Oral history of the Massachusetts building trades, built from interviews with carpenters whose working lives ran from the 1910s through the 1980s. Chapter 14, “New Tools, New Materials, New Methods,” is the source for the preface’s account of how the trade received the portable circular saw: that hand labor dominated on-site construction until after the Second World War; that the early models carried only marginal safety features and often proved dangerous in the hands of a first-time user; that one carpenter carried his Skilsaw to the roof of a building and threw it off, saying the saw was too fast and he would cut by hand; and that as early as 1932 the Springfield District Council proposed fining union members twenty-five dollars for furnishing electric hand-driven machinery to a contractor. Read free in the open-access edition published by Temple University Press and North Broad Press.

https://temple.manifoldapp.org/read/with-our-hands-the-story-of-carpenters-in-massachusetts/section/5a3bd679-a298-470e-8e0e-702802bd49e9. See Endnote Commentary.

Erman, Sam. Almost Citizens: Puerto Rico, the U.S. Constitution, and Empire. Cambridge University Press, 2018; Studies in Legal History; LCCN 2018035531; ISBN 9781108415491. The paperback of 2019 is widely cited as the first edition and is not. The nearest work in the literature to the argument that individuals without formal legal training make a difference at law, advanced there about the constitutional absorption of empire.

European Parliament and Council. Regulation (EU) 2018/858 of 30 May 2018 on the approval and market surveillance of motor vehicles and their trailers, and of systems, components and separate technical units intended for such vehicles. Official Journal of the European Union L 151, 14 June 2018, pp. 1–218. The Whole Vehicle Type Approval framework that governs motor vehicle market access across the twenty-seven EU member states.

https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2018/858/oj/eng.

Evans, Oliver. U.S. Patent for the high-pressure steam engine, granted 1804 (term expired 1818 under the fourteen-year rule of the Patent Act of 1790). The Evans engine made high-pressure steam economical for the first time and is the steam-engine entry in the chapter’s eight-product table. Background and patent history at the Linda Hall Library’s history-of-science collection.

https://www.lindahall.org/about/news/scientist-of-the-day/oliver-evans.

Event Data Recorder Rule. 49 C.F.R. § 563 (Event Data Recorders). National Highway Traffic Safety Administration. Original final rule promulgated 2006; multiple amendments since. Required for vehicles voluntarily equipped with event data recorders, with prescribed data fields and survivability standards.

https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-49/subtitle-B/chapter-V/part-563.

Executive Office of the President. “Memorandum on Delivering Emergency Price Relief for American Families and Defeating the Cost-of-Living Crisis.” Presidential Memorandum of January 20, 2025. 90 Fed. Reg. 8245 (January 28, 2025) (Federal Register Doc. 2025-01904). Directs the heads of all executive departments and agencies to pursue actions reducing housing costs, eliminating administrative expenses and rent-seeking practices that raise health-care costs, and removing “counterproductive requirements that raise the costs of home appliances.” This document is a Presidential Memorandum, not Executive Order 14148; the chapter prose mis-cites it as an Executive Order and should be corrected at edit time.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/01/28/2025-01904/delivering-emergency-price-relief-for-american-families-and-defeating-the-cost-of-living-crisis.

Executive Order 14154 of January 20, 2025. “Unleashing American Energy.” 90 Fed. Reg. 8353 (January 29, 2025) (Federal Register Doc. 2025-01956). Directs federal agencies to review and, where appropriate, withdraw, rescind, or postpone regulations and orders that impede the development of domestic energy resources, including consumer-appliance and equipment energy-efficiency mandates. The Trump administration’s February 14, 2025 Department of Energy announcement postponing seven Biden-administration appliance-efficiency mandates was issued under the authority of this Executive Order and the related Presidential Memorandum on Delivering Emergency Price Relief.

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/01/29/2025-01956/unleashing-american-energy.

Farm Action. Organizational homepage. Farmer-led watchdog organization focused on corporate consolidation and antitrust enforcement in agriculture; policy work has been developed into twenty pieces of congressional legislation. Identified in the farm-equipment chapter as one of five candidate plaintiff organizations under associational standing established in Hunt v. Washington State Apple Advertising Commission (1977).

https://farmaction.us/.

Farm and Ranch Freedom Alliance (FARFA). Organizational homepage. Coalition-building organization founded in 2006 representing independent small-scale farmers, ranchers, and value-added producers; works with fifty or more farm organizations on regulatory and antitrust questions. Identified in the farm-equipment chapter as one of five candidate plaintiff organizations under associational standing.

https://farmandranchfreedom.org/.

Farrand, Max, ed. The Records of the Federal Convention of 1787. Vol. 2. New Haven: Yale University Press, 1911. Entries for August 18, 1787, recording Madison’s proposal to secure to literary authors their copyrights “for a limited time” and the separate proposal offered the same day to grant patents for useful inventions, and for September 5, 1787, recording the Committee of Eleven’s report of the merged clause securing “for limited Times to Authors and Inventors the exclusive Right to their respective Writings and Discoveries.” Cited in the chapter on objections considered for the drafting history of the plural “Times.”

https://memory.loc.gov/ammem/amlaw/lwfr.html.

Fawzi, Alhussein, et al. “Discovering Faster Matrix Multiplication Algorithms with Reinforcement Learning.” Nature 610 (2022): 47-53. The forty-seven multiplication result for four-by-four matrices holds in arithmetic modulo two and not over the real numbers, where two-level Strassen’s forty-nine still stands.

See Endnote Commentary.

Federal Aviation Administration, Aircraft Type Certification. 49 U.S.C. § 44704; 14 C.F.R. Parts 21, 23, 25, 27, and 29. Statutory and regulatory framework requiring type certificates for new aircraft designs, production certificates for manufacturing facilities, and airworthiness certificates for individual aircraft.

https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-14/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-21.

Federal Aviation Administration. Type Certificate Data Sheet 3A12 (Cessna 172 series), issued November 4, 1955, on an application of July 11, 1955, and amended since; and Advisory Circular 21.101-1B, Establishing the Certification Basis of Changed Aeronautical Products. The data sheet shows that the 172R (amendment of June 21, 1996) and 172S (amendment of May 1, 1998) were added to the 1955 certificate rather than certified afresh, and that the certificate passed to Textron Aviation on July 29, 2015. The advisory circular, applying 14 C.F.R. § 21.101, states that a change to a type-certificated product is evaluated against its existing certification basis and that earlier regulations are acceptable where the latest amendments are not appropriate. Cited in The Patent Clause Prong as the clearest instance of a federal power securing a patent-expired original design to one private party without an end date; and in How a Remake Is Built and Certified for the point that a design’s own certification history can still govern (newer Part 23 amendments apply to the changed and affected areas of a design change, while earlier requirements are retained where the latest amendments are not appropriate) and as the working precedent for platform-year certification, together with the General Aviation Revitalization Act of 1994, Public Law 103-298, which restored Cessna’s production through liability reform and waived no airworthiness standard. The continuity runs with the certificate, which is transferable property; a third party gets it only by acquiring the certificate or licensing it from the holder under 14 C.F.R. § 21.55, and the 172S carries a model-specific basis at several Part 23 amendment levels rather than the 1955 rules alone.

https://drs.faa.gov/browse/excelExternalWindow/DRSDOCID153801600120250627164731.0001; https://www.faa.gov/documentLibrary/media/Advisory_Circular/AC_21.101-1B.pdf; https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/14/part-21/subpart-B

Federal Aviation Administration. Supplemental Type Certificate framework, 14 C.F.R. Part 21 Subpart E. The FAA's standing regulatory architecture under which modifications to a previously certified aircraft type design are approved through an independent engineering certification process distinct from the original type certificate held by the airframe manufacturer. Cited in the trucking chapter as the regulatory model for independent engineering certification of authentic remanufactured patent-expired heavy-duty truck platforms.

https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-14/chapter-I/subchapter-C/part-21/subpart-E.

Federal Motor Carrier Safety Administration. “Electronic Logging Devices and Hours of Service Supporting Documents.” Final Rule, 80 Fed. Reg. 78292 (December 16, 2015). Codified at 49 C.F.R. Parts 385, 386, 390, and 395. Requires most commercial motor vehicles engaged in interstate commerce to use a federally certified electronic logging device to record drivers’ hours of service, location, engine status, and motion. Compliance was required by December 18, 2017 for carriers previously using paper logs. Cited in the trucking-section discussion of federally mandated continuous driver surveillance in the surveillance chapter. This footnote is also for the Foreword.

Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards: date-of-manufacture applicability. NHTSA, “Importation and Certification FAQs.” Each vehicle’s certification label states that it conforms to all applicable FMVSS in effect on its date of manufacture, under 49 C.F.R. Part 567; the standards bind by manufacture date, and a vehicle lawfully built to the standards then in force is not required to retrofit to later standards for continued operation or resale.

Federal Reserve Economic Data (FRED). Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Database hosting more than 800,000 economic time series, including BEA Industry Economic Accounts series used in the cumulative wealth-transfer calculation discussed in Chapter 8.

https://fred.stlouisfed.org/.

Federal Rules of Civil Procedure (especially Rule 10(a), caption and names of parties); Federal Rules of Appellate Procedure (especially Rule 3, notice of appeal, and Rule 12, docketing the appeal); Rules of the Supreme Court of the United States (especially Rule 12, review on writ of certiorari). The three federal rule sets that establish the party-label conventions used at each level of the federal courts — plaintiff and defendant at the trial court, appellant and appellee at the United States Court of Appeals, petitioner and respondent at the Supreme Court. The Federal Rules of Civil Procedure and the Federal Rules of Appellate Procedure are promulgated by the Supreme Court under the Rules Enabling Act, 28 U.S.C. § 2072; the Rules of the Supreme Court are promulgated by the Court itself. Cited in the Endnote Commentary as the authority for the procedural-terminology shifts a reader encounters when following the same litigant from the district court to the Supreme Court.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frcp; https://www.law.cornell.edu/rules/frap; https://www.supremecourt.gov/filingandrules/2023RulesOfTheCourt.pdf. See Endnote Commentary.

Proposed order banning General Motors and OnStar from disclosing consumers’ precise geolocation and driver behavior data to consumer reporting agencies for a period of five years; order finalized January 2026. Source for the FTC enforcement action documented in the surveillance chapter.

Fisher, Robert S., Jay N. Acharya, Fiona M. Baumer, et al. “Visually sensitive seizures: An updated review by the Epilepsy Foundation.” Epilepsia 63, no. 4 (2022): 739–768. The current peer-reviewed Epilepsy Foundation review of visually triggered seizures. Documents that approximately three percent of people with epilepsy experience photoparoxysmal responses to flashing lights or patterns; that the most provocative flicker frequency range is approximately three to sixty hertz (peak around fifteen to twenty hertz) at intensities above approximately twenty candelas per square meter, occupying ten to twenty-five percent of the visual field; and that modern LED light sources with elevated luminance can trigger photosensitive seizures in susceptible individuals. Source for the healthy-lighting chapter’s clinical anchor on LED flicker and photosensitive epilepsy.

https://doi.org/10.1111/epi.17175.

Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, 527 U.S. 627 (1999).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/527/627/.

Food and Drug Administration v. Alliance for Hippocratic Medicine, 602 U.S. 367 (2024). Unanimous decision holding that doctors and medical associations who neither prescribed nor used mifepristone lacked standing to challenge the Food and Drug Administration’s relaxation of its conditions of use. Justice Kavanaugh’s opinion for the Court held that an unregulated plaintiff challenging the government’s regulation of others must show a predictable causal link rather than a speculative one. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the government’s strongest recent authority against a buyer’s standing.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/602/23-235/.

Ford Motor Company. “Advertisement for the 1925 Ford Model T, ‘Within the Means of Millions.’” The Henry Ford Museum, Artifact #355535. Created by Ford Motor Company in 1925. The advertisement, held in The Henry Ford Museum’s digital collection, depicts factory workers leaving a plant in their personal Ford Model T cars; the five-word tagline “Within the / means of / millions” is inscribed in the lower-left corner, and a secondary safety slogan “MAKE SAFETY YOUR RESPONSIBILITY” appears at the bottom. The cars-and-trucks chapter quotes the tagline once as a one-sentence framing of the affordability the 1925 patent-expired commons in personal transportation actually delivered.

https://www.thehenryford.org/collections-and-research/digital-collections/artifact/355535/.

Created an eighteen-year statute of repose for product-liability suits against manufacturers of general-aviation aircraft and their parts. Gilbert, Ruth, Georgia Salanti, et al. “Infant Sleeping Position and the Sudden Infant Death Syndrome: Systematic Review of Observational Studies and Historical Review of Recommendations from 1940 to 2002.” International Journal of Epidemiology 34 (2005). Pooled odds ratio for front against back sleeping reached 2.93 (95 percent confidence interval 1.15 to 7.47) by 1970, and 3.00 (1.69 to 5.31) across five studies by 1986. The authors estimate that a systematic review conducted in 1970 might have prevented more than ten thousand infant deaths in the United Kingdom. See also their reply to Guntheroth and Spiers, same volume, page 1166, on the greater cost of introducing the advice as against the delay in withdrawing it.

Gideon v. Wainwright, 372 U.S. 335 (1963). The Supreme Court’s unanimous holding, written by Justice Black, that the Sixth Amendment right to counsel binds the States through the Fourteenth Amendment. Clarence Earl Gideon wrote his own petition in pencil; the Court appointed Abe Fortas to argue the case, and the winning argument was his.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/372/335/.

Goldberger, Joseph. “The Etiology of Pellagra: The Significance of Certain Epidemiological Observations with Respect Thereto.” Public Health Reports 29, no. 26 (June 26, 1914): 1683-1686.

Golan v. Holder, 565 U.S. 302 (2012). Six-to-two decision, Justice Kagan taking no part, upholding Section 514 of the Uruguay Round Agreements Act, which restored United States copyright to foreign works that had already passed into the American public domain. Justice Ginsburg’s majority opinion rejected the premise that the Constitution renders the public domain largely untouchable by Congress and read the “limited Times” language as no bar to protecting works already in it; the opinion refers to the provision as the Copyright and Patent Clause. Justice Breyer, joined by Justice Alito, dissented. Cited in the chapter on objections considered, alongside Eldred v. Ashcroft, as adverse authority on the reach of the public domain, and distinguished there as a copyright decision reviewing an act of Congress rather than a Patent Clause challenge to an agency-built certification barrier.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/565/302/.

Golden v. United States, 955 F.3d 981 (Fed. Cir. 2020). The Federal Circuit’s reaffirmation of Celgene Corp. v. Peter, rejecting a takings claim arising from inter partes review; the opinion repeats that Oil States Energy Services v. Greene’s Energy Group did not suggest that patents are not property for purposes of the Due Process or Takings Clauses, and declines to decide the broader status of patents under the Takings Clause because Celgene controlled the result. Cited in the chapter on objections considered, with Celgene, as the Federal Circuit’s application of the question Oil States reserved; neither decision reaches the public’s right to build a patent-expired design.

https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/cafc/19-2134/19-2134-2020-04-10.html.

Goldman Sachs. “Robotaxis Are Forecast to Become a $400 Billion Market in 2035.” Goldman Sachs Research, 2026. Reports the research team’s projection that the all-in operating cost of an autonomous Class 8 truck, before corporate overhead, was approximately $8.56 per mile in 2025, falling toward $2.03 per mile by 2035; that autonomous and human-driven trucking costs are projected to cross around 2028; that the autonomous sensor and computing stack adds $125,000 to $150,000 per truck; that the comparable per-mile cost of a human-driven truck is projected to rise from $2.55 to $2.84 over the same period on expected driver-wage increases; that the autonomous stack is projected to fall to $35,000 to $40,000 per truck by 2035 as hardware improves and production scales; that vehicle depreciation falls from roughly $0.35 per mile in 2025 to about $0.14 in 2035 as the ratio of vehicles per remote supervisor improves from 6-to-1 today to 26-to-1 in 2035; that autonomous trucks are not subject to the 11-hour daily driving limit and mandatory breaks that bind human drivers, and so can cover more miles; and that the per-mile cost of owning a personal vehicle ($0.77 to $1.00) remains below the more-than-$2.00 cost of the average ride-hail trip. Cited throughout the autonomous-vehicle chapter.

https://www.goldmansachs.com/insights/articles/robotaxis-to-become-a-400-billion-dollar-market-in-2035.

Google. Big Sleep discovery of CVE-2025-6965 in SQLite, July 15, 2025, as reported in “Google AI ‘Big Sleep’ Stops Exploitation of Critical SQLite Vulnerability Before Hackers Act,” The Hacker News, July 16, 2025. Big Sleep, an AI agent built by Google DeepMind and Project Zero, identified a memory-corruption flaw in the SQLite database engine after Google’s threat-intelligence group saw indications that an attacker was staging an exploit but could not isolate the vulnerability; Kent Walker of Google called it the first time an AI agent had been used to directly foil an exploit in the wild. Cited in the preface as one of three cases in which a machine found, by reading a system’s rules, a flaw its expert defenders had not.

https://thehackernews.com/2025/07/google-ai-big-sleep-stops-exploitation.html.

Google LLC v. Oracle America, Inc., 593 U.S. 1 (2021). Six-to-two decision, Justice Barrett taking no part, holding that Google’s reimplementation of the declaring code of the Java SE application programming interface, to let programmers use familiar calls in a new platform, was fair use. The Court assumed copyrightability without deciding it. Cited for the room the fair-use doctrine leaves a rewriter who needs an interface to work with existing code.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/593/18-956/.

Gosker, Robert, and J. F. Degenhart. “Pack-Saddle.” U.S. Patent No. 39,285. Issued July 21, 1863. Pack-saddle improvement designed for long-distance, mixed-cargo overland travel in the western territories.

https://patents.google.com/patent/US39285A.

Graham v. John Deere Co., 383 U.S. 1 (1966).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/383/1/.

Griffin, G. Edward. World Without Cancer: The Story of Vitamin B17. Westlake Village, CA: American Media, 1974. Multiple revised editions through 2019.

https://www.amazon.com/World-Without-Cancer-Story-Vitamin/dp/0912986190.

Haartsen, Jacobus Cornelis. “Contemporaneous Connectivity to Multiple Piconets.” U.S. Patent 6,026,297, filed September 17, 1997, issued February 15, 2000. Assigned to Telefonaktiebolaget LM Ericsson AB. Foundational Bluetooth patent covering simultaneous connectivity to multiple piconets; expired 2017–2018. Cited in the surveillance chapter as one of the foundational expired patents from which a remanufactured vehicle’s Bluetooth radio can be built.

https://patents.google.com/patent/US6026297A/en.

Haartsen, Jacobus Cornelis. “Frequency Hopping Piconets in an Uncoordinated Wireless Multi-User System.” U.S. Patent 6,590,928, filed September 17, 1997, issued July 8, 2003. Assigned to Telefonaktiebolaget LM Ericsson AB. Foundational Bluetooth patent on the frequency-hopping piconet architecture; expired August 2018.

https://patents.google.com/patent/US6590928B1/en.

Haley, Daniel. Politics in Healing: The Suppression and Manipulation of American Medicine. Washington, DC: Potomac Valley Press, 2000.

https://www.amazon.com/Politics-Healing-Suppression-Manipulation-American/dp/0972751416.

Hamblin, Michael R. “Mechanisms and applications of the anti-inflammatory effects of photobiomodulation.” AIMS Biophysics 4, no. 3 (2017): 337–361. Peer-reviewed review of the photobiomodulation literature. Documents that red and near-infrared light — wavelengths abundantly emitted by incandescent sources and substantially reduced or absent from blue-shifted LED output — are absorbed by cytochrome c oxidase in mitochondria, dissociating inhibitory nitric oxide, increasing ATP production, reducing oxidative stress, and producing measurable anti-inflammatory effects at the cellular level. Situates the work of Glen Jeffery and others in a substantial peer-reviewed body of research on the systemic and ocular consequences of long-wavelength light exposure.

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5523874/.

Hamil, Robert C. “Improvement in Saddles.” U.S. Patent No. 139,668. Issued June 10, 1873. Riding-saddle improvement specifying a design with two girths passing round the body, addressing the slip problem of the Stickney single-girth design on long ascents.

https://patents.google.com/patent/US139668A.

Handmer, Casey. “Reading Ancient Scrolls.” Personal blog, 5 August 2023. https://caseyhandmer.wordpress.com/2023/08/05/reading-ancient-scrolls/. The first substantial evidence of ink inside an unopened Herculaneum scroll, found by examining publicly released X-ray scan segments and identifying a cracked-mud surface texture in the shape of Greek letters. The Vesuvius Challenge awarded the First Ink Prize of ten thousand dollars on 12 October 2023.

Hardin, Garrett. “The Tragedy of the Commons.” Science 162, no. 3859 (December 13, 1968): 1243–1248.

https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.162.3859.1243.

Heckler v. Chaney, 470 U.S. 821 (1985). An agency’s decision not to undertake an enforcement action is presumptively committed to the agency’s discretion and unreviewable under the Administrative Procedure Act. Cited in the anti-backsliding chapter to distinguish enforcement discretion, the power not to prosecute a violation, from the power to repeal a standard, which the executive does not possess.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/470/821/.

Hill, Kashmir. “Automakers Are Sharing Consumers’ Driving Behavior with Insurance Companies.” New York Times, March 11, 2024. First article in Hill’s 2024 investigation documenting that General Motors, Honda, Hyundai, Kia, Subaru, and Mitsubishi were transmitting driving-behavior data from their connected vehicles to LexisNexis Risk Solutions and Verisk, which compiled the data into individual driver-risk reports and sold them to insurance companies.

https://www.nytimes.com/2024/03/11/technology/carmakers-driver-tracking-insurance.html.

Hill, Kashmir. “Florida Man Sues G.M. and LexisNexis Over Sale of His Cadillac Data.” New York Times, March 14, 2024. Reports the first private civil action filed against General Motors and LexisNexis arising from the data-sharing arrangement Hill’s reporting documented.

https://www.nytimes.com/2024/03/14/technology/gm-lexis-nexis-driving-data.html.

Hill, Kashmir. “General Motors Quits Sharing Driving Behavior with Data Brokers.” New York Times, March 22, 2024. Reports GM’s announcement that it would discontinue the data-sharing arrangements with LexisNexis Risk Solutions and Verisk; precursor to the April 24, 2024 formal discontinuation of the OnStar Smart Driver program.

https://www.nytimes.com/2024/03/22/technology/gm-onstar-driver-data.html.

Hill, Kashmir. “How G.M. Tricked Millions of Drivers into Being Spied On (Including Me).” New York Times, April 23, 2024. First-person investigative account of how the OnStar Smart Driver enrollment consent flow was structured to capture consent at vehicle delivery; documents the granular data fields transmitted, including hard-braking events, rapid accelerations, and speeding events.

https://www.nytimes.com/2024/04/23/technology/general-motors-spying-driver-data-consent.html.

Hill, Kashmir. “Automakers Sold Driver Data for Pennies, Senators Say.” New York Times, July 26, 2024. Reports on the Wyden–Markey letter to the Federal Trade Commission and the underlying oversight responses obtained directly from the automakers, including per-vehicle broker payments on the order of twenty-six cents from Honda and sixty-one cents from Hyundai.

https://www.nytimes.com/2024/07/26/technology/driver-data-sold-for-pennies.html.

https://www.gov.uk/hmrc-internal-manuals/corporate-intangibles-research-and-development-manual/cird123000.

Podcast episode discussing the peer-reviewed research of Dr. Glen Jeffery at University College London on the mitochondrial and retinal benefits of long-wavelength incandescent and natural-sunlight exposure, and the corresponding harms from blue-shifted LED exposure. Hunt v. Washington State Apple Advertising Commission, 432 U.S. 333 (1977).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/432/333/. The associational-standing decision establishing the three-prong test under which a trade or membership association may sue on behalf of its members: (1) at least one member must have standing to sue individually; (2) the interests the lawsuit protects must be germane to the association’s purpose; and (3) neither the claim nor the relief may require individual member participation. Cited in the farm-equipment chapter as the doctrinal basis for adding farm associations as a fifth category of plaintiff with standing to challenge the regulatory ratchet.

Imported Vehicle Safety Compliance Act of 1988; the twenty-five-year exemption. Codified in the importation framework at 49 C.F.R. Part 591 and implemented through 49 C.F.R. Part 571, the rule exempts motor vehicles twenty-five years old or older from the FMVSS that would otherwise bar importation, allowing an older design to lawfully enter and remain in U.S. commerce without meeting the current year’s standards.

India. Finance Act 2016, amending Section 35(2AB) of the Income-tax Act, 1961. Reduced the weighted deduction for approved in-house scientific research from 200 percent of qualifying expenditure to 150 percent effective April 1, 2017, and to 100 percent thereafter. Section 35 applied different weighted rates to different categories of research expenditure, making it the clearest documented instance of a single statute tuning the multiplier by category. Documented in KPMG India, “Department of Scientific and Industrial Research Amends Guidelines for Approval of In-House R&D Centres.”

https://www.in.kpmg.com/taxflashnews/KPMG-Flash-News-Department-of-Scientific-and-Industrial-Research-3.pdf.

Cited in the international-comparison chapter for the proposition that Indian compliance obligations attach to the date a vehicle is manufactured. No legacy-platform, grandfathering, or platform-year provision was located in the retrieved text of this standard or of the Central Motor Vehicles Rules; that absence is the argument’s own finding and is identified as such in the chapter.

Inland Revenue Authority of Singapore. “Enterprise Innovation Scheme (EIS).” Establishes a total deduction of 400 percent on the first S$400,000 of qualifying research and development expenditure, comprising the 100 percent base deduction under Section 14C of the Income Tax Act 1947, the additional 150 percent deduction under Section 14D(1), and a further 150 percent enhanced deduction under the scheme, for Years of Assessment 2024 through 2028. Lighting trade-press confirmation that Executive Order 14154 of January 20, 2025 (Unleashing American Energy) does not and cannot override the federal efficiency standards governing light bulbs, because the standards rest on statutory authority under the Energy Policy and Conservation Act and its anti-backsliding provision at 42 U.S.C. § 6295(o)(1). Institute for Justice. Pro bono constitutional litigation organization founded in 1991, based in Arlington, Virginia. Cited in the trucking chapter as one of three public-interest legal foundations that carry constitutional cases of the kind the Patent Clause argument requires at no cost to the plaintiff.

Insurance Institute for Highway Safety. “Fatality Facts 2023: Bicyclists.” IIHS-HLDI, posted July 2025. Annual summary of bicyclist fatality data compiled from the U.S. Department of Transportation’s Fatality Analysis Reporting System (FARS). Source for the bicycles section figures on bicyclist deaths under age 20 (786 in 1975 falling to approximately 93 in 2023, an 88 percent decline), adult bicyclist deaths (212 in 1975 rising to 1,041 in 2023, a near-fivefold increase), and total bicyclist deaths (1,155 in 2023, the highest annual figure ever recorded). The same IIHS page documents the crossover year (1989) when adult bicyclist deaths first exceeded child bicyclist deaths in the United States.

Internal Revenue Code § 170(e)(3). Added by the Tax Reform Act of 1976 for contributions made after October 4, 1976. Allows a corporation contributing inventory for the care of the ill, the needy, or infants to deduct the lesser of basis plus one-half of the appreciation or twice the basis of the contributed property. The twice-basis ceiling is the longest-standing above-cost deduction in the United States Code.

https://irc.bloombergtax.com/public/uscode/doc/irc/section_170.

Lyon, France: World Health Organization, 2014. The 2012 IARC working-group reclassification of diesel engine exhaust from Group 2A (probably carcinogenic to humans) to Group 1 (carcinogenic to humans), based on sufficient evidence of an association with lung cancer. Isaacs, Davida H. “Not All Property Is Created Equal: Why Modern Courts Resist Applying the Takings Clause to Patents, and Why They Are Right to Do So.” George Mason Law Review 15, no. 1 (2007): 1. Argues that a patent’s status as property is a relevant but incomplete answer to the constitutional question, because the Supreme Court has held that some interests — federal benefits in particular — receive Due Process Clause protection without receiving Takings Clause protection, and that patents are federal entitlements of that kind. Cited in the objections chapter as the strongest scholarly statement of the objection to the Takings Clause prong.

Jeffery, Glen, et al. Peer-reviewed clinical study on long-wavelength red light exposure and mitochondrial function in human visual decline. Scientific Reports (Nature), 2021. University College London research documenting measurable improvements in retinal function following brief exposure to deep-red (670 nm) light, with implications for the harms imposed by blue-shifted LED-only residential lighting environments. Anchored in the healthy-lighting chapter through the corresponding Huberman Lab podcast treatment.

https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-021-02311-1.

Jeffery, Glen, et al. Peer-reviewed follow-up research on long-wavelength light therapy and cellular function. Scientific Reports (Nature), 2025. University College London research extending the 2021 findings on the systemic and ocular benefits of deep-red light exposure and the corresponding harms from blue-shifted LED-only lighting environments.

https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-025-09785-3.

Provides the pre-1971 versus post-1970 federal-regulatory cost split per vehicle cited in the cars-and-trucks chapter: $250–$400 in 2025 dollars for the pre-1971 layer (Motor Vehicle Safety Act of 1966 and Air Quality Act of 1967) against $6,600–$8,600 in 2025 dollars for the post-1970 layer (Clean Air Act Amendments of 1970 and Energy Policy and Conservation Act of 1975). Article author and exact publication date pending verification at edit time.

https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-benefit-cost-analysis.

J. W. Hampton, Jr., & Co. v. United States, 276 U.S. 394 (1928). The Supreme Court upheld a delegation of rulemaking authority to the executive, holding that Congress may delegate such authority so long as it lays down an intelligible principle to guide the body exercising it. Cited in the chapter on the Patent Clause prong as the source of the modern non-delegation rule, and for the limit that rule carries: an agency may exercise only authority Congress possessed and could lawfully delegate, and Congress possessed no authority to cancel the public’s ownership of an expired invention.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/276/394/.

Kaplan, Sonia, Malika Nurullaeva, and John Paul Helveston. “Comparing the Costs of Autonomous and Conventional Taxi Services.” Transport Policy 159 (December 2024): 108–119. DOI: 10.1016/j.tranpol.2024.10.010. Component-level cost model grounded in deployed U.S. robotaxi operations: an autonomous taxi at about $1.99 per mile against $2.74 for a conventional taxi, with labor still $1.02 per mile because remote support, cleaning, and depot work remain human. Assumes 65,000 miles per vehicle-year at 50 percent utilization. Cited in The Last Human Driver as the peer-reviewed cost work that follows the Massachusetts Institute of Technology study without overturning it.

Kauers, Manuel, and Jakob Moosbauer. “Flip Graphs for Matrix Multiplication.” In Proceedings of the 2023 International Symposium on Symbolic and Algebraic Computation (ISSAC ’23), Tromsø, Norway, 381–388. Association for Computing Machinery, 2023. doi:10.1145/3597066.3597120. Preprint arXiv:2212.01175. The authors state that in their quick response to Fawzi and colleagues they announced further four-by-four schemes at forty-seven multiplications in characteristic two and a reduction of the five-by-five case to ninety-five, against AlphaTensor’s ninety-six; they also reduced a rectangular format from sixty-three to sixty-two. The flip-graph search method is their own, ran on a small computer in seconds, and used AlphaTensor’s output as a starting point. Cited in the chapter on outsiders and blind doctrine for the proposition that the machine result dislodged a settled belief that the question was closed, after which mathematicians who had held the capability all along went further than the machine had.

https://www.kbb.com/ford/f-150/2025/.

https://www.coxautoinc.com/market-insights/kbb-atp-june-2023/.

Kahle v. Gonzales, 487 F.3d 697 (9th Cir. 2007). The Ninth Circuit’s affirmance of the dismissal of a challenge to the shift from an opt-in to an opt-out copyright system, holding that the plaintiffs made essentially the argument the Supreme Court had rejected in Eldred v. Ashcroft. The opinion nonetheless states that Eldred did not purport to directly address whether the phrase “limited Times” places substantive limits on duration, citing Eldred’s own footnote that the outer-boundary question was not before the Court. Judge Farris wrote for a unanimous panel.

https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/487/697/622521/. See Endnote Commentary.

Kellogg Co. v. National Biscuit Co., 305 U.S. 111 (1938). Justice Brandeis, for the Court, held that on expiration of the Perky shredded-wheat patents there passed to the public “not only the right to make the article as it was made during the patent period, but also the right to apply thereto the name by which it had become known,” and that upon expiration “the form, as well as the name, was dedicated to the public.” The only obligation resting on the newcomer was to identify its own product so that buyers were not deceived as to source. Cited in the chapter on the Patent Clause prong for the Court’s plainest statement that the form of the article, and not merely its underlying idea, enters the public domain when the patent expires, and in the chapter on objections considered for the source-identification duty that answers the trademark objection to remanufacturing. The suit arose in diversity as a common-law unfair-competition claim; the case is cited for what it holds about post-expiration rights, not for a constitutional proposition. It completes the line running from Singer Manufacturing Co. v. June Manufacturing Co. (1896) through Scott Paper Co. v. Marcalus Manufacturing Co. (1945) to Sears, Roebuck & Co. v. Stiffel Co. and Compco Corp. v. Day-Brite Lighting, Inc. (1964).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/305/111/.

Kempe, C. Henry, Frederic N. Silverman, Brandt F. Steele, William Droegemueller, and Henry K. Silver. “The Battered-Child Syndrome.” Journal of the American Medical Association 181, no. 1 (1962): 17-24. The first state mandatory reporting statutes followed in 1963; all fifty states had enacted one by 1967.

Kennedy, Robert F., Jr. The Real Anthony Fauci: Bill Gates, Big Pharma, and the Global War on Democracy and Public Health. New York: Skyhorse, 2021.

https://www.skyhorsepublishing.com/9781510766808/the-real-anthony-fauci/.

Kelo v. City of New London, 545 U.S. 469 (2005). The Supreme Court’s holding that transferring private property to a private developer for economic development satisfies the public-use requirement of the Takings Clause. Justices O’Connor and Thomas dissented. The development was never built and the anchor tenant left New London in 2009; the decision produced eminent-domain reform legislation in a majority of States.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/545/469/.

Kewanee Oil Co. v. Bicron Corp., 416 U.S. 470 (1974). Six-to-two decision, Justice Powell taking no part, holding that Ohio trade-secret law is not preempted by the federal patent laws. Chief Justice Burger, for the Court, reasoned that trade-secret protection is weaker than a patent, withdraws nothing from the public domain, and poses no real risk of deterring inventors from seeking patents. Justice Marshall concurred in the result; Justice Douglas, joined by Justice Brennan, dissented. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as adverse authority for the proposition that federal patent policy does not displace every other legal regime bearing on an invention, and distinguished there on the ground that trade-secret law leaves the public free to reverse-engineer and copy what it learns, while the certification barrier forbids the lawful sale of the copy itself.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/416/470/.

Kimble v. Marvel Entertainment, LLC, 576 U.S. 446 (2015).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/576/446/.

Korematsu v. United States, 323 U.S. 214 (1944). The Supreme Court’s decision upholding the wartime exclusion of Japanese Americans from the West Coast, with Justices Roberts, Murphy, and Jackson dissenting. The claim of military necessity was made while contrary evidence sat in the government’s files; a federal court vacated Fred Korematsu’s conviction on coram nobis in 1983 after the record surfaced.

Largey, Matt. “The ‘Bad Grade’ That Changed The U.S. Constitution.” All Things Considered, National Public Radio, May 5, 2017. Originally produced for Pop-Up Magazine in collaboration with KUT-FM Austin, Texas. The principal popular source for Chapter 31’s account of Gregory Watson’s 1982–1992 campaign to ratify the Twenty-Seventh Amendment, featuring interviews with Watson and with his University of Texas professor Sharon Waite, whose 2017 reconsideration of the original C grade led to the formal university grade change from C to A+ on March 1, 2017. Largey’s reporting also documents the chronology of state ratifications during the 1982–1992 campaign. The 1792 Kentucky ratification, rediscovered after Michigan’s May 7, 1992 vote — and which makes Alabama (May 5, 1992) the technical thirty-eighth ratifying state — is documented across multiple state-archive sources collected in the U.S. National Archives Twenty-Seventh Amendment file.

https://www.npr.org/2017/05/05/526900818/the-bad-grade-that-changed-the-u-s-constitution.

Larsson, Fredrik, Petra Andersson, Per Blomqvist, and Bengt-Erik Mellander. “Toxic Fluoride Gas Emissions from Lithium-Ion Battery Fires.” Scientific Reports 7 (2017): 10018. Fire tests on seven types of commercial lithium-ion batteries measured hydrogen fluoride emissions of 20 to 200 milligrams per watt-hour of nominal battery capacity, with phosphoryl fluoride detected in some tests; the authors extrapolate a release of 400 to 1,200 grams of hydrogen fluoride from a 10 kWh automotive pack in a fire. Cited in the environmental chapter for the point that the technology the regulatory wall favors carries lifecycle costs no regulation prices.

https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-017-09784-z.

Last Emperor, The. Directed by Bernardo Bertolucci. Screenplay by Mark Peploe and Bernardo Bertolucci. Columbia Pictures, 1987. Winner of nine Academy Awards (60th Academy Awards, 1988), including Best Picture and Best Director. The Hybrid Voting chapter cites the tutor-scene dialogue in which Reginald Johnston (Peter O’Toole) tells the fifteen-year-old Pu Yi (Wu Tao), in response to the Emperor’s question why precise words matter: “If you cannot say what you mean, Your Majesty, you will never mean what you say. And a gentleman should always mean what he says.” The exchange arises from Johnston’s correction that Scotsmen wear kilts, not skirts — “a matter of words, perhaps, but words are important.” The line is original to the Peploe-Bertolucci screenplay and does not appear in Pu Yi’s memoir From Emperor to Citizen (1964) or in Reginald Johnston’s memoir Twilight in the Forbidden City (1934). Quote attribution verified against the film’s audio and against the Internet Movie Database’s quotes page.

https://www.imdb.com/title/tt0093389/quotes/.

Lawrence v. Texas, 539 U.S. 558 (2003). The Supreme Court’s holding that a Texas statute criminalizing same-sex intimate conduct violates the Due Process Clause, overruling Bowers v. Hardwick. Justice O’Connor concurred in the judgment on equal-protection grounds. The corrected historical record came from a brief filed by historians outside the legal profession.

Levy, Karen. Data Driven: Truckers, Technology, and the New Workplace Surveillance. Princeton: Princeton University Press, 2022. Sociologist’s ethnographic study of the electronic-logging-device mandate and its downstream consequences for American long-haul truck drivers, documenting how federal regulation supplied the legal floor while commercial vendors built the surveillance stack above it, including the Omnitracs Driver Retention Model that analyzes hours-of-service data to predict which drivers are at risk of voluntary termination. Cited in the trucking-section of the surveillance chapter for the proposition that federal mandate, vendor product, employer surveillance, and behavioral prediction move in one direction only.

https://press.princeton.edu/books/hardcover/9780691175300/data-driven.

Li, David H. The Genealogy of Chess. Bethesda, MD: Premier Publishing Co., 1998. The minority-view book in the chess-origin debate, advancing the thesis that an early form of chess was invented in China around 203–204 BC by General Han Xin during the Chu-Han Contention, and that the Indian chaturanga tradition derives from a westward transmission of the Chinese game rather than the reverse. Named Book of the Year 1998 by the editors of GAMES Magazine. Cited in the preface as one side of the historians’ debate over whether chess migrated from India to China or from China to India.

https://www.amazon.com/genealogy-chess-David-H-Li/dp/0963785222.

Lieberman, Ben. “Home Appliance Regulations Are Out of Control.” City Journal, March 13, 2025. Senior fellow Lieberman (Competitive Enterprise Institute) surveys the Department of Energy efficiency-standards regime applied to washing machines, refrigerators, furnaces, dishwashers, central air conditioners, light bulbs, and water heaters; documents DOE-estimated cost increases such as the $953 added cost on the latest electric water heater standard; and reports the post-standards performance degradation that has produced two-hour dishwasher cycles. The article does not contain the Kansas-family dishwasher anecdote attributed to it in some derivative writing; the chapter prose attribution should be corrected at edit time.

https://www.city-journal.org/article/home-appliance-regulations-department-of-energy-efficiency-standards.

Liu, Wenzhe. The Chinese School of Chess. Translated by Yasser Seirawan and Ken Smith. London: Batsford, 2002. Liu, a leading Chinese international master and theoretician, takes a pro-China view of the origins of the chess family and traces, from inside the modern game, the distinctively Chinese contribution to opening theory and strategic outlook. Cited in the preface together with Li (1998) and Murray (1913) as a representative voice in the historians’ debate over whether chess migrated from India to China or from China to India.

https://www.chess.com/article/view/quotchinese-school-of-chessquot-by-liu-wenzhe.

Low Volume Motor Vehicle Manufacturers Act of 2015. Enacted as Section 24405 of the Fixing America’s Surface Transportation Act (FAST Act), Pub. L. No. 114-94, 129 Stat. 1312 (December 4, 2015). Codified at 49 U.S.C. § 30114(b). Authorizes specialty manufacturers to produce replica cars of designs at least twenty-five years old without full current-year federal motor vehicle safety standards compliance, subject to a ceiling of three hundred twenty-five replica vehicles per manufacturer per year across the entire United States and the requirement that the replicas meet current-year EPA emissions standards. The Act’s amendment to Clean Air Act § 206 deems a replica emissions-compliant only if it is equipped with an engine, including all emissions-control equipment, already covered by an EPA certificate of conformity for a current model year — so the statute that reopens the old body to production requires, in its own text, that the original powertrain be replaced with a modern certified one. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the empirical test in which Congress waived the safety standards for old designs yet left the emissions wall standing.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/49/30114.

Lucas v. South Carolina Coastal Council, 505 U.S. 1003 (1992).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/505/1003/.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/504/555/.

Manufacturers of Emission Controls Association (MECA). Trade association of the emission-controls component-manufacturers serving the automotive, heavy-duty truck, off-road, and stationary engine markets. Membership includes Corning Inc. (the principal manufacturer of ceramic substrates used in diesel particulate filters), Johnson Matthey, BASF Catalysts, Umicore, and other component suppliers. Manufacturers of Emission Controls Association (MECA). “Technology Feasibility for Heavy-Duty Diesel Trucks in Achieving 90% Lower NOx Standards in 2027.” White paper, 2020. Documents that the MECA-member aftertreatment-hardware share (diesel oxidation catalyst, diesel particulate filter substrate, selective catalytic reduction catalyst) on a current Class 8 line-haul tractor with a twelve- to thirteen-liter engine, certified to the EPA 2010 heavy-duty standards, runs approximately $3,500 to $4,600 in 2019 dollars, based on a MECA member survey. Source for the trucking chapter’s figure that every Class 8 truck sold after 2007 carries roughly three to five thousand dollars in MECA-member components. The total aftertreatment-system cost paid by the buyer (including original-equipment-manufacturer integration, sensors, tanks, dosing equipment, on-board diagnostics, and dealer markup) is materially higher; the MECA-member component share isolated by this white paper is the figure the chapter cites.

The author’s original proposal of the multi-value tax deduction — a deduction of two or three dollars for each dollar spent on a favored activity, cheaper to the Treasury than a tax credit and more inducing than an ordinary deduction. Martin, Roleigh. “The Forgotten Half of the Patent Clause: What was Promised When Patents Expired.” Published April 10, 2026. The published companion methodology article for the three-tier breakdown of the expired-patent economy. Its section “A note on the methodology beneath the two charts” sets out the derivation: the major categories of expired-patent commerce were weighted by their share of U.S. manufacturing output and household consumer spending, drawing on the Bureau of Labor Statistics Consumer Expenditure Survey, the Bureau of Economic Analysis Industry Economic Accounts, and the Census Bureau Annual Survey of Manufactures. Each sub-category was then assigned to a tier by degree of regulatory interference: blocked where post-1970 federal regulation makes domestic manufacture of expired-patent designs effectively impossible, hindered where FDA and medical-licensing capture limits but does not fully block use of expired patents, and flowing where the public domain is at least partially honored. A second chart splits the flowing tier between imports and domestic production using Census Bureau International Trade data. The article states plainly that these are informed estimates, order-of-magnitude accurate but not rigorously calculated, because the counterfactual they represent — what American commerce would look like if regulation did not block use of the expired-patent public domain — is inherently difficult to specify with precision. Readers wanting harder numbers for any single sub-category are directed there to the Census Bureau Annual Survey of Manufactures and the International Trade Administration’s trade data, which together supply the authoritative federal baseline. X article: https://x.com/RoleighMartin/status/2042726307931287738. Substack: https://postpandemicchanges.substack.com/p/the-forgotten-half-of-the-patent-e04.

Metzl, Jamie. “Why I Co-Authored a Book with ChatGPT.” The Free Press, April 21, 2026. Metzl’s defense of naming GPT-5 as named co-author of his book The AI Ten Commandments: A New Moral Code for Humanity (Transcend Books, 2026) — the first major work of nonfiction to do so. Metzl describes a workflow in which the human serves as sole conceptualizer, framer, and editorial controller while the AI serves as drafting and synthesis partner across thousands of iterative cycles. Cited in this book’s note on AI-assisted authorship as a contemporary publishing precedent that arrived alongside this book; the methodologies closely parallel each other, the principal difference being that Metzl’s book presents an AI synthesis at its core while this book presents a human-discovered constitutional argument that the AI helped execute.

Ministry of Finance and State Taxation Administration of the People’s Republic of China. Announcement on Further Improving the Pre-tax Additional Deduction Policy for Research and Development Expenses, Announcement No. 7 of 2023. Issued March 26, 2023, effective retroactively from January 1, 2023, with no expiry date. Permits an additional deduction of 100 percent of qualifying research expenditure, for a total pre-tax deduction of 200 percent. The rate was raised to 100 percent for manufacturing enterprises in 2021 before being extended to all eligible enterprises, an instance of a multiplier tuned by sector before being generalized. Documented in Dezan Shira & Associates, “China R&D Expenses Pre-Tax Super Deduction.”
