Motion Picture Patents Co. v. Universal Film Manufacturing Co., 243 U.S. 502 (1917). Held (6—3) that a patentee may not, by notice attached to a patented machine, extend the monopoly to unpatented supplies used with it; an unconditional sale exhausts the patent right. In the course of that holding the Court stated, as a rule settled since Pennock v. Dialogue (1829), that the primary purpose of the patent laws is not the creation of private fortunes for patent owners but the promotion of the progress of science and the useful arts. Cited in the Patent Clause prong chapter as the decision elevating that reasoning toward settled rule.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/243/502/.

Muller v. Oregon, 208 U.S. 412 (1908). Justice Brewer, for a unanimous Court, upheld an Oregon statute limiting women in factories and laundries to ten hours a day. The decision is cited in this book for the brief rather than the holding; see the entry under Brandeis and Goldmark for the filing that gave the Brandeis brief its name.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/208/412/.

Murray, H. J. R. A History of Chess. Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1913. The classical and exhaustive scholarly reference on the history of the chess family, supporting the long-standing mainstream view that the game originated as Indian chaturanga around the sixth century CE, spread westward via Persian shatranj, and that Chinese Xiangqi and Korean Janggi developed as eastward derivatives or parallels of the same ancestor. Cited in the preface as the principal representative of the mainstream Indian-origin position against which Li (1998) argues. Public-domain digital edition via the Internet Archive.

https://archive.org/details/historyofchess0000hjrm.

Natanson, Hannah, Jeff Stein, Dan Diamond, and Rachel Siegel. “DOGE Builds AI Tool to Cut 50 Percent of Federal Regulations.” The Washington Post, July 26, 2025. Reports, from a July 1 PowerPoint and four officials, that the “DOGE AI Deregulation Decision Tool” was built to analyze roughly 200,000 federal regulations for those no longer required by law, had made decisions on 1,083 regulatory sections at the Department of Housing and Urban Development, and had written the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau’s deregulations; HUD employees said it misread the language of the law on several occasions. Cited in the preface as the nearest existing use of AI on the regulatory state — asking whether a statute still requires a rule, not whether the statute and rule together do what the Constitution forbade.

https://www.washingtonpost.com/business/2025/07/26/doge-ai-tool-cut-regulations-trump/.

National Aeronautics and Space Administration. “Artificial Intelligence, NASA Data Used to Discover Eighth Planet Circling Distant Star.” News release, December 14, 2017. Reports the discovery of Kepler-90i by a neural network built by Christopher Shallue, a software engineer at Google, trained on fifteen thousand previously vetted Kepler signals and applied to 670 star systems already examined by professional astronomers. Shallue originated the approach and was not an astronomer; the astronomy was supplied by Andrew Vanderburg, then a NASA Sagan postdoctoral fellow at the University of Texas at Austin, who worked the problem with him and co-authored the resulting paper. The finding tied Kepler-90 with the solar system for the most planets known to orbit a single star. Cited in the preface as an instance in which an outsider supplied the method and specialists inside the field supplied the domain judgment.

https://www.nasa.gov/news-release/artificial-intelligence-nasa-data-used-to-discover-eighth-planet-circling-distant-star/

National Appliance Energy Conservation Act of 1987, Pub. L. No. 100-12, 101 Stat. 103 (March 17, 1987). Codified primarily at 42 U.S.C. §§ 6291–6309, with the anti-backsliding provision at § 6295(o)(1). Senate Roll Call 28, February 17, 1987 (89–6); House voice vote, March 3, 1987.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/chapter-77.

National Association of Home Builders (NAHB). Study of Life Expectancy of Home Components. Washington, D.C.: NAHB and Bank of America Home Equity, February 2007. Industry-standard reference for residential appliance and component lifespans, compiled from a 2006 survey of manufacturers, trade associations, and researchers. The figures represent industry-wide averages assuming normal usage and adherence to maintenance schedules; the study notes that actual service life depends substantially on installation quality, usage frequency, and climate conditions, and that appliances are often replaced for reasons of technological advancement or changing consumer preferences well before they cease to function. Cited in the cars-and-trucks chapter for the appliance replacement-cycle figures, including the refrigerator (13 years), dishwasher (9 years), clothes dryer (13 years), microwave oven (9 years), gas range (15 years), and electric range (13 years). The chapter’s per-appliance figures are conservative midpoints calibrated against the NAHB single-point estimates and the InterNACHI ranges.

https://hcmuddox.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/10/nahb20study20of20life20expectancy20of20home20components.pdf.

National Association of Landscape Professionals (NALP). Organizational homepage. The national trade association for the landscape, lawn care, irrigation, and tree care trades, headquartered in Fairfax, Virginia. Formed January 1, 2005 by the merger of the Associated Landscape Contractors of America (founded 1961) and the Professional Lawn Care Association of America as the Professional Landcare Network, and rebranded to its present name April 1, 2015. Identified in the plaintiff-categories chapter as the trade body for the landscaping and grounds-maintenance operator, whose governing hook is EPA Part 1054, the standard for small spark-ignition engines. The association has actively opposed restrictive small-engine emissions rules and state-level gasoline-equipment bans. Membership figures appear in two forms and should not be conflated: roughly 100,000 landscape industry professionals in membership, within an industry the association describes as approaching one million professionals.

https://www.landscapeprofessionals.org/.

National Automobile Chamber of Commerce. Automobile Facts and Figures, 1926 edition. Industry trade-association annual compilation of U.S. motor-vehicle production, sales, and registration data. Source for the 1925 U.S. motor-vehicle production figures cited in the cars-and-trucks chapter: 3,735,171 passenger cars and 4,265,830 total motor vehicles (passenger cars plus 530,659 trucks). The NACC was the primary industry body collecting and publishing automotive production statistics in the 1920s; comprehensive retail-sales data was not compiled and made public until after World War II, so the chapter’s reference to four million new cars sold in 1925 represents factory production, which the industry of the period used interchangeably with factory sales. Archived by the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis, FRASER digital library, alongside related commercial-car registration series.

https://fred.stlouisfed.org/series/M0146DUSM543NNBR.

National Electrical Manufacturers Association v. United States Department of Energy, No. 17-1341 (4th Cir.). Petition for review filed by NEMA in 2017 challenging the U.S. Department of Energy’s January 2017 expanded definitions of general service lamp and general service incandescent lamp. Settled by agreement on July 11, 2017; the settlement led directly to the Trump-administration September 2019 rollback of the expanded definitions (84 Fed. Reg. 46661, September 5, 2019). Cited in the healthy-lighting chapter’s bootleggers-and-Baptists section as documentary evidence that NEMA’s posture across the 2007–2024 arc included active litigation against DOE on the scope of the rule, complicating any straight-line account of industry support for the regulatory architecture.

https://www.law.nyu.edu/sites/default/files/settlement-agreement.pdf.

National Family Farm Coalition (NFFC). Organizational homepage. Coalition representing family farmers across dozens of state-level farmer organizations; multi-state structure provides the geographic and circuit diversity that strengthens a constitutional filing. Identified in the farm-equipment chapter as one of five candidate plaintiff organizations under associational standing.

https://nffc.net/.

National Farmers Union (NFU). Organizational homepage. Founded 1902; represents more than 200,000 family farms and ranches across 33 state divisions through a grassroots policy process. President Rob Larew, Vice President Jeff Kippley. The NFU 124th Anniversary Convention (March 7–9, 2026, New Orleans) highlighted the 46 percent year-over-year increase in farm bankruptcy filings reported by the American Farm Bureau Federation. Identified in the farm-equipment chapter as the strongest single candidate plaintiff among the five farm associations developed under associational standing.

https://nfu.org/.

National Highway Traffic Safety Administration. “Bicycle Safety.” U.S. Department of Transportation. Official federal portal for bicycle safety statistics, educational materials, and crash data drawn from the agency’s Fatality Analysis Reporting System (FARS). Cited in the bicycles section of the modern-bargain chapter as the federal-data anchor for cyclist fatality trends and as the agency-level confirmation that no federal product safety regulation operates on the bicycle itself.

https://www.nhtsa.gov/road-safety/bicycle-safety.

National Institute of Mental Health. “Seasonal Affective Disorder.” NIMH Health Publications, U.S. Department of Health and Human Services. The current authoritative federal mental-health publication on Seasonal Affective Disorder (SAD), a recurring depressive disorder linked to seasonal variation in light exposure. NIMH states that millions of Americans experience SAD, with prevalence rising at higher latitudes and higher rates in women. Treated with controlled-spectrum light therapy that requires careful filtering against the blue-shifted output of standard residential LED lighting. Cited in the healthy-lighting chapter’s coalition section as the federal-source anchor for the SAD-affected population.

https://www.nimh.nih.gov/health/publications/seasonal-affective-disorder.

National Psoriasis Foundation. “Phototherapy.” NPF patient-resource page. Authoritative patient-organization summary of phototherapy as an approved treatment modality for psoriasis. Documents that ultraviolet B (UVB) light, present in natural sunlight and delivered via therapeutic lamps, is an effective treatment for psoriasis because it penetrates the skin and slows the excessive growth of affected skin cells. Psoriasis affects approximately 7.5 to 8 million American adults per the NPF’s prevalence data, of whom a non-trivial fraction depends on UVB phototherapy delivered through specialized incandescent and halogen lamp architectures. Cited in the healthy-lighting chapter’s coalition section.

https://www.psoriasis.org/phototherapy/.

National Sustainable Agriculture Coalition (NSAC). Organizational homepage. Coalition of grassroots organizations advocating for sustainable and organic farmers; published analyses document that four firms control 45 percent of global farm equipment sales. Identified in the farm-equipment chapter as one of five candidate plaintiff organizations under associational standing.

https://sustainableagriculture.net/.

National Technology Transfer and Advancement Act of 1995, Pub. L. No. 104-113, 110 Stat. 775. Directs federal agencies to use technical standards developed by private-sector voluntary consensus standards bodies (such as SAE, ASTM, UL, and ANSI) where practicable, in lieu of developing federal standards from scratch. Implemented through OMB Circular A-119.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/272.

Neri, Cassio. “Shorter and Faster than Sort3AlphaDev.” arXiv:2307.14503 (2023). Supplies counterexamples to the claim that no shorter three-element sorting program exists.

New Civil Liberties Alliance (NCLA). Pro bono litigation organization founded in 2017, headquartered in Washington, DC. Focused on constitutional challenges to administrative-state overreach. NCLA was lead counsel on Relentless, Inc. v. Department of Commerce, which the Supreme Court consolidated with Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo — the 2024 decision that ended Chevron deference. Cited in the trucking chapter as one of three public-interest legal foundations carrying constitutional cases of the kind the Patent Clause argument requires at no cost to the plaintiff.

https://nclalegal.org/.

Newbold, Charles. U.S. Patent for the cast-iron plow, granted 1797 (term expired 1811 under the fourteen-year rule of the Patent Act of 1790). The single-piece cast-iron moldboard plow that, after expiration, became the dominant American farming implement of the early-to-mid nineteenth century. The cast-iron plow entry in the chapter’s eight-product table.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Newbold.

Nichia Corporation and the LED-to-laser lighting succession. The efficient blue gallium-nitride light-emitting diode was developed by Shuji Nakamura at Nichia Corporation of Japan in the early 1990s; Nichia paired a blue chip with a yellow phosphor to produce the first commercial white LED in 1996, the architecture in most household LED bulbs today. Nakamura shared the 2014 Nobel Prize in Physics for the blue LED. Nichia held the core white-LED patents, earned a reported fortune from them, and litigated to defend them; those patents, filed in the 1990s, have only recently begun to expire. As the LED portfolio lapses, the same inventor and the same company have moved to laser-based white light: Nakamura’s post-LED work through SoraaLaser (SLD Laser) and Nichia’s GigaWhite blue-laser-plus-phosphor module, developed for automotive, specialty, and industrial lighting, with Nichia in 2016 publicly prioritizing laser diodes as the next lighting source. Cited in the healthy-lighting chapter for the observation that the regulatory withdrawal pattern recurs one technology at a time, each expired-patent design displaced as its patented successor arrives.

https://led-ld.nichia.co.jp/en/product/ld_gigawhite.html. Nobel Prize background: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shuji_Nakamura

Nixon v. United States, 506 U.S. 224 (1993). The Supreme Court’s leading modern application of the political-question doctrine to internal Congressional procedure: the Senate’s procedure for trying an impeachment was held nonjusticiable because the Constitution textually commits the matter to the Senate and the courts lack judicially manageable standards for second-guessing the chamber’s procedural choices. Cited in the Hybrid Voting chapter as the doctrinal anchor for the proposition that federal courts have shown strong reluctance to police the chamber’s internal voting mechanics, including the interpretive scope of the Yeas-and-Nays override in Article I, Section 5, Clause 3.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/506/224/.

Nollan v. California Coastal Commission, 483 U.S. 825 (1987). The Supreme Court’s holding that regulatory conditions imposed on the use of property must bear an “essential nexus” to the harm the regulation claims to address, supplying the first prong of the proportionality test later completed in Dolan v. City of Tigard (1994) and reaffirmed in Sheetz v. County of El Dorado (2024).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/483/825/.

Nordlinger v. Hahn, 505 U.S. 1 (1992). The Supreme Court’s decision upholding California’s acquisition-value property-tax scheme under Proposition 13, which assessed newly purchased property at its purchase price while capping annual increases on long-held property at two percent. Justice Blackmun wrote for the Court, which found the classification rationally related to legitimate state interests and confined Allegheny Pittsburgh Coal Co. v. County Commission of Webster County (1989) to the rare case in which the facts precluded any plausible inference that an acquisition-value policy explained the disparity. Justice Thomas concurred in part while stating that Allegheny Pittsburgh could not be distinguished; Justice Stevens dissented. The decision is the strongest authority against pleading this book’s equality argument as a standalone equal-protection claim, and it is taken up in that posture in the objections chapter.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/505/1/.

Northeast States for Coordinated Air Use Management (NESCAUM). “Heavy-Duty Omnibus Low-NOx Rule: Frequently Asked Questions.” September 2024. The current authoritative public summary of state adoption of the California Air Resources Board Heavy-Duty Omnibus (Low-NOx) regulation under Clean Air Act § 177. Documents the nine states that have specifically adopted the CARB Heavy-Duty Omnibus standards as of 2024–2025: Colorado (model year 2027), Massachusetts (MY 2025), New Jersey (MY 2027), New Mexico (MY 2027), New York (MY 2026), Oregon (MY 2025), Rhode Island (MY 2027), Vermont (MY 2026), and Washington (MY 2026). The broader seventeen-state Section 177 group that adopted CARB light-duty (LEV/ZEV/ACC II) or general vehicle standards is distinct from the nine-state Heavy-Duty Omnibus group. Source for the trucking chapter’s reference to the nine states whose Heavy-Duty Omnibus adoption layered the federal mandate with a parallel state regime.

https://www.nescaum.org/documents/HD-Low-NOx-Omnibus-FAQ_website-version_clean_09-23-24.pdf.

Novak Conversions. The Novak Guide to the Spicer Model 18 Transfer Case. Reference history of the Jeep transfer case. The civilian Jeep’s core drivetrain was supplied and patented by independent component makers rather than by Willys–Overland or its successors: the Borg-Warner T-90 three-speed transmission and the Spicer (Dana) Model 18 transfer case and Dana axles. The underlying utility patents on these mid-century components expired under the seventeen-year-from-grant term then in force, placing the CJ drivetrain in the public domain by the late 1980s.

https://www.novak-adapt.com/knowledge/transfer-cases/model-18/.

Nunes, Ashley, and Kristen D. Hernandez. “Autonomous Vehicles and Public Health: High Cost or High Opportunity Cost?” Massachusetts Institute of Technology, 2019. A full-cost study finding that a conventional human-driven vehicle costs about $0.72 per mile to operate, while a robotaxi—once licensing, insurance, cleaning, remote oversight, and the low utilization of the taxi fleet are counted—costs between $1.58 and $6.01 per mile. Cited in the autonomous-vehicle chapter on robotaxi economics.

https://www.researchgate.net/publication/332743767_Autonomous_Vehicles_and_Public_Health_High_Cost_or_High_Opportunity_Cost.

Occupational Safety and Health Act of 1970, Pub. L. No. 91-596, 84 Stat. 1590. Codified as amended at 29 U.S.C. §§ 651 et seq. Establishes federal workplace safety standards through the Occupational Safety and Health Administration; the General Duty Clause is at 29 U.S.C. § 654.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/29/chapter-15.

Occupational Safety and Health Administration. “29 CFR 1928.51 — Roll-Over Protective Structures for Agricultural Tractors.” U.S. Department of Labor. Source for the first of the three regulatory layers discussed in the farm-equipment chapter: the 1976 OSHA rollover rule requiring rollover protective structures and seatbelts on tractors manufactured after October 25, 1976, when operated by employees.

https://www.osha.gov/laws-regs/regulations/standardnumber/1928/1928.51.

Oil States Energy Services, LLC v. Greene’s Energy Group, LLC, 584 U.S. 325 (2018). Seven-to-two decision holding that inter partes review, the Patent Office proceeding for reconsidering the validity of an issued patent, violates neither Article III nor the Seventh Amendment. Justice Thomas’s majority opinion characterized a patent as a public franchise and patent validity as a matter of public rights that Congress may assign to an executive agency; it described the holding as narrow, declined to decide whether inter partes review may be applied to patents issued before the Leahy-Smith America Invents Act, and stated that the decision should not be read as holding that patents are not property for purposes of the Due Process Clause or the Takings Clause. In the same passage the majority repeated, from its own older cases, that the grant of a patent is a matter between the public, who are the grantors, and the patentee, and that in issuing a patent the Patent Office takes from the public rights of immense value and bestows them upon the patentee. Justice Breyer, joined by Justices Ginsburg and Sotomayor, concurred. Justice Gorsuch, joined by Chief Justice Roberts, dissented on the ground that an issued patent is private property revocable only by an Article III court. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as adverse authority on the government’s power over the patent grant, and distinguished there as a decision about the grant side of the Clause that says nothing about the invention after its patent expires.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/584/16-712/case.pdf.

Old Tacoma Marine. “Fairbanks-Morse.” Marine engine history page. States that after the expiration of Rudolf Diesel’s American licence in 1912, Fairbanks-Morse entered the large-engine business, its Model Y semi-diesel becoming a standard workhorse in sugar, rice, timber, and mine mills. The page’s 1912 date refers to Diesel’s American licence; the underlying patent, U.S. Patent No. 608,845, ran to 1915 (see that entry). Cited in the trucking chapter for Fairbanks-Morse as an American firm that entered the diesel-engine business after Diesel’s American patent rights ran out.

https://www.oldtacomamarine.com/engines/fairbanks/index.html.

Olson, Mancur. The Logic of Collective Action: Public Goods and the Theory of Groups. Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 1965.

https://www.hup.harvard.edu/books/9780674537514.

Ostrom, Elinor. Governing the Commons: The Evolution of Institutions for Collective Action. Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1990.

Owner-Operator Independent Drivers Ass’n, Inc. v. United States Department of Transportation, No. 15-3756 (7th Cir. Oct. 31, 2016), cert. denied, 137 S. Ct. 2246 (2017). Seventh Circuit decision rejecting OOIDA’s Fourth Amendment challenge to the FMCSA Electronic Logging Device rule. The court held that the trucking industry’s status as a “pervasively regulated industry” under New York v. Burger, 482 U.S. 691 (1987), permits the warrantless inspections the ELD rule effectively requires. Cited in the trucking-section of the surveillance chapter as the doctrinal anchor for the proposition that the Fourth Amendment frame has lost on the question of continuous federally-mandated driver surveillance — and as the open door for the Patent Clause and Takings Clause prongs this book develops.

https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-7th-circuit/1752477.html.

The current authoritative source for OOIDA’s self-described membership: approximately 150,000 members in all fifty states, collectively owning and operating more than 240,000 individual heavy-duty trucks. Pacific Legal Foundation. Pro bono constitutional litigation organization founded in 1973, based in Sacramento, California. Palmer, E. D. Histological study of gastric biopsies from a thousand subjects concluding that spiral organisms were contaminants introduced from the mouth, 1954. Cited in the chapter on outsiders and blind doctrine as the study that closed the question for thirty years. Known here through later reviews; the full citation has not been confirmed against the original.

Pardee, Joseph T. “Unusual Currents in Glacial Lake Missoula, Montana.” Geological Society of America Bulletin 53, no. 11 (1942): 1569-1600. Identified the water source that resolved the Scablands dispute in Bretz’s favour.

Patent Act of 1790, ch. 7, 1 Stat. 109 (April 10, 1790). Federal Reserve Archival System for Economic Research (FRASER), Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. The First Congress did not merely permit an exclusive right; it deployed national authority to secure one. Section 1 directed that letters patent issue “in the name of the United States,” signed by the President. Section 4 armed the right with a federal cause of action — damages assessed by a jury and forfeiture of the infringing articles to the person aggrieved, recoverable in the circuit courts of the United States. This is the affirmative, coercive sense of the Constitution’s verb “securing.” Note one correction often made in secondary summaries: the familiar treble-damages remedy (three times the price the patentee usually charged) is not in the 1790 Act; it belongs to the Patent Act of 1793, § 5, 1 Stat. 318.

https://fraser.stlouisfed.org/title/patent-act-1790-5734/fulltext.

Patent Act of 1861, ch. 88, 12 Stat. 246 (March 2, 1861). Extended the U.S. utility patent term from fourteen years to seventeen years from the date of grant. The seventeen-year rule remained in force until 1995, when the Uruguay Round Agreements Act harmonized U.S. patent terms with TRIPS at twenty years from the date of filing.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Term_of_patent_in_the_United_States.

Patent Clause Letter Campaign. Hub page for the campaign’s free, browser-based letter and brochure generators, which produce mailing packages addressed to county sheriffs, governors and state attorneys general, and tribal nations; readers can consult the hub to see whether a generator has been added for a given plaintiff category.

https://marti124.github.io/Patent_Letters/.

Patterson v. Kentucky, 97 U.S. 501 (1878). Foundational Supreme Court decision holding that the existence of a federal patent does not exempt the tangible product embodying the invention from state police-power regulation aimed at the safety of the public. The Kentucky statute at issue banned the sale of a patented “improved burning oil” that the state inspector had condemned as unsafe; the patentee’s argument that his federal patent placed the oil beyond state regulatory reach was rejected unanimously. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the principal authority for the proposition (which the book accepts) that patents do not confer immunity from regulation.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/97/501/.

Peace Corps, Establishment of. Executive Order 10924, “Establishment and Administration of the Peace Corps in the Department of State,” March 1, 1961, signed by President John F. Kennedy; authorized by Congress in the Peace Corps Act, Pub. L. No. 87-293, 75 Stat. 612, approved September 22, 1961. Cited in the remedy chapter as the verified historical instance of a President standing up a federal institution by executive order, later ratified by statute. National Archives, “Executive Order 10924: Establishment of the Peace Corps.”

https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/executive-order-10924.

Penn State Extension. “A Summary of Laws and Regulations Related to Agricultural Safety and Machinery.” Synthesizing source for the federal regulatory landscape surrounding agricultural equipment manufacture and operation, drawn upon in the farm-equipment chapter’s diagnostic of the three regulatory layers (OSHA, EPA, NHTSA).

https://extension.psu.edu/a-summary-of-laws-and-regulations-related-to-agricultural-safety-and-machinery.

Pennock v. Dialogue, 27 U.S. (2 Pet.) 1 (1829). Held that an inventor who permits public use or sale of his invention before applying for a patent forfeits the right to one (an ancestor of the on-sale bar). In reasoning the Court has treated as settled ever since, Justice Story explained that the main object of the patent system is to promote the progress of science and useful arts, best served by giving the public at large the right to make, construct, use, and vend the invention at as early a period as possible, with due regard to the rights of the inventor. The opinion construes the Patent Act of 1793. Cited in the Patent Clause prong chapter for the public’s half of the bargain, stated early and by a Madison appointee.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/27/1/.

Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon, 260 U.S. 393 (1922).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/260/393/.

Perkins, Jacob. U.S. Patent for a nail-making machine, granted 1795 (term expired 1809 under the fourteen-year rule of the Patent Act of 1790). The Perkins machine and its post-expiration competitors collapsed the American price of nails by roughly seventy percent across the nineteenth century. The nail-making entry in the chapter’s eight-product table.

U.S. Department of Transportation. Source for the distinct federal hazardous-materials regime governing fuel-tanker operators: bulk packaging above 119 gallons triggers hazmat and tanker-endorsement requirements, the cargo tank itself must meet the DOT-406 construction specification, and the driver must hold a hazardous-materials endorsement and clear a Transportation Security Administration background check. Cited in the trucker chapter’s fuel-hauler subsection.

Age, sex, smoking status, systolic blood pressure and major adverse cardiac events predicted from the photograph alone.

Railway Labor Executives’ Association v. Gibbons, 455 U.S. 457 (1982). The Supreme Court’s holding that Congress may not use the Commerce Clause to enact a non-uniform bankruptcy law, because permitting it would eradicate from the Constitution the uniformity limitation the Bankruptcy Clause places on the bankruptcy power. The specific-governs-the-general anchor for this book’s argument that a limitation written into one clause of Article I, Section 8 is not escaped by legislating under another clause of the same section. Gibbons concerned the Bankruptcy Clause; carrying its reasoning to the Patent Clause’s “limited Times” limitation is this book’s step and not the Court’s.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/455/457/.

Self-certification and certification by simulation. The United States does not pre-approve motor vehicles before sale; the National Traffic and Motor Vehicle Safety Act establishes a self-certification process under which manufacturers certify that their vehicles comply with all applicable Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards. NHTSA has confirmed that a manufacturer exercising reasonable care may certify compliance through simulation or engineering analysis, not only through the agency’s physical test procedures. Independent engineering and homologation firms perform such certification work today, at both the whole-vehicle and component level. NHTSA, Notice Regarding the Applicability of NHTSA FMVSS Test Procedures to Certifying Manufacturers, 85 Fed. Reg. 83,502 (Dec. 21, 2020).

https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2020/12/21/2020-28107/notice-regarding-the-applicability-of-nhtsa-fmvss-test-procedures-to-certifying-manufacturers.

Semmelweis, Ignaz. Die Ätiologie, der Begriff und die Prophylaxis des Kindbettfiebers. Pest, Vienna and Leipzig: C. A. Hartleben, 1861. The dates for the division of the Vienna General Hospital’s obstetric clinics and for the introduction of chlorine washing are taken from Epidemiology and Infection 136, no. 1 (7 June 2007): 1–9, doi 10.1017/S0950268807008746, whose figure legend gives them directly; the authors of that article have not been confirmed. Sources differ on whether the clinics divided by gender in 1839 or on 19 October 1840.

See Endnote Commentary.

Shallue, Christopher J., and Andrew Vanderburg. “Identifying Exoplanets with Deep Learning: A Five-Planet Resonant Chain around Kepler-80 and an Eighth Planet around Kepler-90.” Astronomical Journal 155, no. 2 (February 2018): 94. doi:10.3847/1538-3881/aa9e09. A neural network trained on fifteen thousand previously vetted Kepler signals, classifying a held-out test set correctly ninety-six percent of the time, then directed at 670 star systems already known to hold multiple planets. Kepler-90i is listed in the NASA Exoplanet Archive.

Shoshone Tribe v. United States (1937; covered in the Takings Clause prong chapter). The federal government had settled another tribe on land guaranteed to the Shoshone by treaty, diminishing their interest. The Supreme Court held the loss compensable. Justice Cardozo’s opinion supplied the phrase this book relies on — “Spoliation is not management” — and the principle it carries: a collective, non-individually-titled interest is property the government must pay to take. The book cites the case for that principle, not for any holding about the patent commons.

Sierra Saddlery School (Las Cruces, New Mexico). “Some History of the Western Saddle.” Posted October 18, 2012. A capable narrative summary of the trans-Atlantic genealogy of the American Western saddle — from Moorish horsemen of the Dark Ages, to the Spanish War Saddle brought to the New World, to the post-conquest evolution of the saddle into a working tool for the Mexican vaquero, to the Texican-and-Californio split that produced today’s swell-fork (square-skirted, double-rigged) and slick-fork (round-skirted, center-fire) Western styles.

https://saddleschool.wordpress.com/2012/10/18/some-history-of-the-western-saddle/. See Endnote Commentary.

Signify N.V. Annual Report 2025. Signify N.V. (formerly Philips Lighting), Eindhoven, Netherlands. The current annual report of the lighting company that emerged from the May 2016 separation of Philips’ Lighting division. Documents that Signify holds more than twenty thousand patents (with the cumulative portfolio reflecting decades of Philips Lighting investment in light-emitting-diode and earlier lighting technologies; the foundational compact-fluorescent patents dating to the 1980 Philips SL lamp have long since expired, while the in-force portfolio is concentrated in solid-state lighting); that annual research and development spending is reported at approximately 250 to 271 million euros, the great majority of it on solid-state lighting and adjacent technologies. Cited in the healthy-lighting chapter’s bootleggers-and-Baptists section as the primary-source anchor for the patent-protected business model that benefits structurally from the displacement of the unpatentable incandescent.

https://www.signify.com/static/2025/signify-annual-report-2025.pdf.

Holding that indigent plaintiffs lacked standing to challenge an Internal Revenue Service ruling that relaxed the charity-care condition for hospitals’ tax exemption, because whether the hospitals would serve them depended on the independent decisions of hospitals not before the court. Skilsaw and the portable circular saw, 1924. Edmond Michel, a French immigrant living in New Orleans, built the first electric handsaw in 1923 by mounting a six-inch blade on a wooden frame and driving it with a motor taken from a malted-milk mixer. Joseph W. Sullivan read a newspaper account of the invention, sought Michel out, and the two founded the Michel Electric Handsaw Company in Chicago in 1924; they named the saw the Skilsaw. The firm was renamed Skilsaw Incorporated in 1926 after Michel left it, and the die-cast aluminum Model E followed in 1928. Cited in the preface for the date the portable circular saw reached the market, which the trade did not adopt in quantity until after the Second World War. Skil, “History” (corporate timeline).

https://www.skileurope.com/uk/history. Companion trade-press account: Industrial Equipment News, “IEN 75th Anniversary Perspective & Timeline: Skil Power Tools,” December 2, 2008, https://www.ien.com/operations/news/21098125/ien-75th-anniversary-perspective-timeline-skil-power-tools (metered access).

Cited in Chapter 2, with Tesla and Rivian, as a new maker entering the market with a new design built to current standards.

Southworth, Michael, and Eran Ben-Joseph. “Street Standards and the Shaping of Suburbia.” Journal of the American Planning Association 61, no. 1 (1995): 65–81. DOI: 10.1080/01944369508975620. The foundational scholarly paper on the historical evolution of American suburban residential street standards. Documents the role of the Federal Housing Administration — described by the authors as “the most ambitious suburbanization plan in United States history” — in establishing the 50-foot minimum right-of-way for federally-backed developments, the preference for cul-de-sacs, and the standards that discouraged through traffic. Hosted on Ben-Joseph’s MIT Department of Urban Studies and Planning page. https://web.mit.edu/ebj/www/doc/JAPAv61n1.pdf.

Sperling, Daniel, Ethan C. Abeles, David S. Bunch, Andrew Burke, Belinda S. Chen, Kenneth S. Kurani, and Thomas S. Turrentine. “The Price of Regulation.” ACCESS Magazine, no. 25 (Fall 2004): 9–18. University of California Transportation Center, summarizing research prepared for the California Air Resources Board under contract 02-310. The article estimates total federal regulatory cost (safety, emissions, and fuel-economy compliance) on a 2001 passenger vehicle at $2,500–$4,020 in then-current dollars, against approximately $11 for the same regulatory categories on a 1967 vehicle. The proportional finding — that more than 99 percent of the cumulative federal regulatory cost on a new vehicle by 2001 was added after 1970 — is the source the cars-and-trucks chapter relies on for the post-1970 attribution. The authors’ preferred estimate is the lower figure ($2,500, of which approximately $1,000 is emissions compliance and less than $1,500 is safety compliance); the higher figure ($4,020) is the BEA/BLS estimate compiled in Ward’s Automotive Yearbook.

https://www.accessmagazine.org/fall-2004/price-regulation/.

Standard Oil Co. of New Jersey v. United States, 221 U.S. 1 (1911). The antitrust decision dissolving the Standard Oil trust, cited in the chapter on why nobody noticed as the legal endpoint of the public pressure that Ida Tarbell’s McClure’s series had built.

Star Athletica, L.L.C. v. Varsity Brands, Inc., 580 U.S. 405 (2017). Establishes the governing separability test under 17 U.S.C. § 101: a feature incorporated into the design of a useful article is eligible for copyright protection only if it can be perceived as a two- or three-dimensional work of art separate from the useful article and would qualify as a protectable pictorial, graphic, or sculptural work on its own or in another medium if imagined separately from the article. The Court noted that the test does not render the underlying useful article itself eligible for copyright. Cited in the chapter Objections Considered for the limit it places on copyright claims over vehicles: a separable badge or graphic may qualify, the vehicle may not.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/580/15-866/.

State-of-the-art defense, products liability. The “state-of-the-art” defense varies by jurisdiction: some states codify it as a full defense to a design-defect claim (e.g., Iowa Code § 668.12), some as a rebuttable presumption that a conforming product is not defective (e.g., Colo. Rev. Stat. § 13-21-403), some as one factor for the jury to weigh (e.g., S.D. Codified Laws § 20-9-10.1), and some (e.g., Illinois) recognize no affirmative state-of-the-art defense, admitting era-relative evidence only within the risk-utility analysis. Federal motor-vehicle safety standards generally do not preempt state tort law; see 49 U.S.C. § 30103(e) and Williamson v. Mazda Motor of America, Inc., 562 U.S. 323 (2011). Cited in the chapter on how a remake is built and certified.

https://www.bloomberglaw.com/external/document/X8S1SDG000000/litigation-overview-state-of-the-art-tort-defense.

Statute of Monopolies, 21 Jac. I, c. 3 (1624). The National Archives (UK), legislation.gov.uk.

https://www.legislation.gov.uk/aep/Ja1/21/3.

Statuto Albertino. 1848. Constitution granted by King Charles Albert of Sardinia, March 4, 1848; extended to the unified Kingdom of Italy upon its proclamation on March 17, 1861; officially abolished when the Constitution of the Italian Republic took effect on January 1, 1948. Article 63 provided, in English translation: Votes shall be taken by rising, by division, and by secret ballot. The latter method, however, shall always be employed for the final vote on a law and in all cases of a personal nature. Article 63 is the constitutional foundation for the Italian parliament’s voto segreto practice from 1848 to 1922 under direct constitutional mandate — the first span within the 119 years the Hybrid Voting chapter refers to. The post-war Republican Constitution’s Article 64 transferred chamber voting procedure to each chamber’s own rules of procedure, under which voto segreto continued in practice from 1948 until the October 1988 chamber-rules reform under the De Mita government.

https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Statuto_Albertino. See Endnote Commentary.

Steffens, Lincoln. The Shame of the Cities. New York: McClure, Phillips & Co., 1904. The collected McClure’s exposé of municipal political corruption, cited in the chapter on why nobody noticed as the third of the four Progressive-Era cases in which exposure alone drove reform.

https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/4904.

Townsend, Harry Pond. Driving and braking mechanism for cycles. U.S. Patent 850,077, filed October 10, 1898 (Serial No. 693,117), granted April 9, 1907; assigned to the New Departure Manufacturing Company of Bristol, Connecticut. Expired 1924. Contemporary trade accounts state that the Townsend patent contained the broad and controlling claims for the coaster brake. Attribution here is contested and worth stating plainly. Daniel Stover and William Hance of Freeport, Illinois are widely credited with an 1889 coaster brake, and they did receive an 1889 patent — but the specification describes a lever pivoted above the bottom bracket bearing on the rim of the wheel, which is a back-pedal spoon brake, not a coaster brake. It has no freewheel, it sits outside the hub, and it was obsolete once pneumatic tires arrived, since a friction pad rubbing a pneumatic tire destroys it. The coaster brake proper — drive, coast, and brake combined inside the hub — arrives with the 1898 filings. Townsend, James S. Copeland of Pope Manufacturing, and William Robinson of Brooklyn all filed within roughly a year of one another, and Alexander P. Morrow of Elmira, New York followed with U.S. Patent 773,333, filed August 1, 1902 and granted October 25, 1904. New Departure lacked the machinery to build Townsend’s design and contracted P. & F. Corbin of New Britain for the first five thousand brakes; within a decade the coaster brake was standard equipment on American-made bicycles.

https://patents.google.com/patent/US850077A/en.

TrafFix Devices, Inc. v. Marketing Displays, Inc., 532 U.S. 23 (2001). Holds that a functional product feature cannot receive trade dress protection under the Lanham Act, and that an expired utility patent is strong evidence that the features it claimed are functional. Where the expired patent claimed the feature in question, the party seeking trade dress protection carries a heavy burden of showing that the feature is merely ornamental, incidental, or arbitrary. Cited in the chapter Objections Considered as the doctrinal bar that prevents trade dress from being used to recapture a functional design after the patent on it has expired — the trademark-side counterpart to the patent-side rule of Bonito Boats, Inc. v. Thunder Craft Boats, Inc. and Sears, Roebuck & Co. v. Stiffel Co..

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/532/23/.

TrueCar. “2025 Ford F-150 Lariat 4×4 SuperCrew 5.5′ Box Listings.” TrueCar dealer-inventory price aggregator. Secondary verification source for the chapter title figure of $72,495: real-world Lariat 4×4 SuperCrew PowerBoost Hybrid configurations are listed at MSRP-with-destination totals of $72,480, $72,580, and $72,690 — converging on the $72,495 figure from a second direction (base Lariat out-the-door total against hybrid Lariat MSRP-with-destination).

https://www.truecar.com/prices-new/ford/f-150-pricing/.

Tucker Act, 28 U.S.C. § 1491; limitations period at 28 U.S.C. § 2501. Confers jurisdiction on the United States Court of Federal Claims over non-tort monetary claims against the United States founded on the Constitution, including claims for just compensation under the Fifth Amendment, subject to a six-year limitations period running from the accrual of each claim. Because the Administrative Procedure Act’s waiver of sovereign immunity at 5 U.S.C. § 702 extends only to relief other than money damages, a plaintiff seeking compensation rather than declaratory or injunctive relief must proceed here instead. Cited in Objections Considered for the six-year ceiling on any damages theory built on the regulatory wall.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/28/1491.

Trump v. Hawaii, 585 U.S. 667 (2018). The Supreme Court’s decision upholding the entry restrictions of Presidential Proclamation 9645. Cited here for one sentence: the Court stated that Korematsu had been gravely wrong the day it was decided and has no place in law under the Constitution — seventy-four years after the fact.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/585/17-965/.

United Food and Commercial Workers Union Local 751 v. Brown Group, Inc., 517 U.S. 544 (1996). Construing the third prong of the associational-standing test of Hunt v. Washington State Apple Advertising Commission. The Court explained that individual participation by members is not normally necessary when an association seeks prospective or injunctive relief on their behalf, but would be required in an action for damages, so that an association’s damages action running solely to its members is barred for want of standing. Cited in the citizen’s-playbook chapter and in Objections Considered for the proposition that a membership association is available as plaintiff for the remedy this book seeks and unavailable for a damages claim.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/95-340.ZO.html.

United States Code. “17 U.S.C. § 101 — Definitions (‘useful article’ and ‘pictorial, graphic, and sculptural works’).” Copyright Act of 1976, as amended. Defines a useful article as one having an intrinsic utilitarian function that is not merely to portray the appearance of the article or to convey information, and provides that the design of a useful article is protected only to the extent it incorporates features that can be identified separately from, and are capable of existing independently of, the utilitarian aspects of the article. Cited in the chapter Objections Considered.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/17/101.

United States Code. “17 U.S.C. § 302(c) — Duration of Copyright: Anonymous Works, Pseudonymous Works, and Works Made for Hire.” Provides that copyright in a work made for hire endures for ninety-five years from the year of first publication or one hundred twenty years from the year of creation, whichever expires first. Cited in the chapter Objections Considered as the source of the ninety-five-year figure a correspondent attributed to whole vehicles; the term is correctly stated but does not attach to a useful article.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/17/302.

United States Code. “42 U.S.C. § 6295(o)(1) — Energy Conservation Program for Consumer Products: Anti-Backsliding Provision.” Cornell University Legal Information Institute.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/6295.

United States Code. “42 U.S.C. § 7521(a)(1) — Emission Standards for New Motor Vehicles or New Motor Vehicle Engines.” Cornell University Legal Information Institute. The Clean Air Act provision granting EPA authority to prescribe emission standards “applicable to the emission of any air pollutant from any class or classes of new motor vehicles or new motor vehicle engines.” Cited in the trucking chapter as the statutory grandfathering principle: EPA’s authority reaches engines at the time of manufacture, fixing each model year’s emission certification as the perpetual standard for the life of that engine. A 1999 model-year heavy-duty diesel certified to the 1998 standards (4.0 g/bhp-hr NOx, 0.10 g/bhp-hr PM) remains subject to those standards, and only those standards, for its operational life.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/42/7521.

United States Code. “49 U.S.C. § 30112(a) — Prohibitions on Manufacturing, Selling, and Importing Noncomplying Motor Vehicles” and “49 U.S.C. § 30115 — Certification of Compliance.” Cornell University Legal Information Institute. Section 30112(a) forbids any person to manufacture for sale, sell, offer for sale, introduce or deliver for introduction into interstate commerce, or import a motor vehicle manufactured on or after the date an applicable Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standard takes effect unless the vehicle complies with the standard and is covered by a § 30115 certification. Cited in the chapter on objections considered to establish that a faithful new copy of a patent-expired vehicle, built to its original platform-year design, cannot be lawfully manufactured for sale or sold today — the standards binding by date of manufacture, so a vehicle built now must meet the standards in force now.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/49/30112 and https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/49/30115.

United States Code. “49 U.S.C. § 30112(b)(9) — Nonapplication of Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards to Vehicles at Least Twenty-Five Years Old.” Cornell University Legal Information Institute. The provision exempting a motor vehicle that is at least twenty-five years old from the Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards whose noncompliance would otherwise bar its manufacture, sale, or importation. Cited in the policy-framework chapter as standing proof that federal law already grandfathers older vehicle designs to the standards of an earlier era; the chapter’s grandfather-exemption leg extends that established principle, replacing the statute’s rolling twenty-five-year age threshold with the fixed platform year the remanufactured design is certified to.

https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/49/30112.

United States Copyright Office. “Useful Articles.” Registration guidance, Visual Arts. States that copyright in a work portraying a useful article extends only to the artistic expression of the author of the pictorial, graphic, or sculptural work and does not extend to the design of the article portrayed; a drawing or photograph of an automobile may be copyrighted, but that does not give the artist or photographer the exclusive right to make automobiles of the same design. Cited in the chapter Objections Considered.

https://www.copyright.gov/register/va-useful.html.

United States Department of Agriculture v. Moreno, 413 U.S. 528 (1973).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/413/528/.

United States International Trade Commission. Remanufactured Goods: An Overview of the U.S. and Global Industries, Markets, and Trade. Investigation No. 332-525, USITC Publication 4356. Washington, DC: USITC, October 2012. Source for the figure cited in the chapter on the wall that the United States is the world’s largest producer of remanufactured goods, valued at no less than $43 billion in annual production over 2009–2011 and supporting some 180,000 full-time jobs, with motor-vehicle parts among the three largest sectors. The chapter cites the report to distinguish the thriving trade in remanufactured components below the certification gate from the assembled patent-expired products the wall blocks at the gate.

https://www.usitc.gov/publications/332/pub4356.pdf.

United States Patent and Trademark Office. “2701 Patent Term [R-10.2019].” Manual of Patent Examining Procedure (MPEP). The authoritative reference for the U.S. patent term: seventeen years from grant for utility patents filed before June 8, 1995, with the post-1995 transitional rule giving the greater of seventeen years from grant or twenty years from filing. Under either rule, all utility patents on the saddles, plows, gins, engines, and clocks discussed in this chapter expired no later than the late 1980s. For applications filed on or after June 8, 1995, the term is twenty years from filing; Chapter 3 relies on that rule in dating tire-pressure-monitor patents filed before 2009 as expired by the end of 2028, subject to any term adjustment on an individual patent. Accessed May 9, 2026.

https://www.uspto.gov/web/offices/pac/mpep/s2701.html.

United States v. 50 Acres of Land, 469 U.S. 24 (1984).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/469/24/.

United States v. Ballin, 144 U.S. 1 (1892). The closest Supreme Court case to address the Journal Clause of Article I, Section 5, Clause 3. In Ballin the Court held the House Journal to be conclusive evidence of what occurred during a contested quorum vote, but did not interpret whether the Yeas-and-Nays override (“at the Desire of one fifth of those Present, be entered on the Journal”) constitutionally requires individual named records or only an aggregate tally. The case is cited in the Hybrid Voting chapter as the doctrinal anchor for the proposition that no Supreme Court holding has squarely decided the interpretive scope of the Yeas-and-Nays override, leaving the question one of first impression.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/144/1/.

United States v. Lopez, 514 U.S. 549 (1995). Five-to-four decision holding the Gun-Free School Zones Act of 1990 beyond the commerce power. Cited for the shape of an enumerated-powers question: the Court asked whether the regulated activity fell within the power, not whether Congress could rationally have thought the statute wise.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/549/.

United States v. Moghadam, 175 F.3d 1269 (11th Cir. 1999), cert. denied, 529 U.S. 1036 (2000). The Eleventh Circuit’s decision upholding the federal anti-bootlegging statute under the Commerce Clause while assuming that the Copyright Clause could not sustain it, live performances being unfixed and so outside the term “Writings.” The court held that the commerce power may not be used where the legislation is fundamentally inconsistent with the limitations of Article I, Section 8, Clause 8, and its footnote identified the statute’s lack of any express time limit as the likely site of that inconsistency. The court did not decide the point; the appellant had not preserved it.

https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/175/1269/637322/.

United States v. Sioux Nation of Indians, 448 U.S. 371 (1980).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/448/371/.

Vargo, Jason, Benjamin G. Gerhardstein, Geoffrey P. Whitfield, and Arthur Wendel. “Bicyclist Deaths Associated with Motor Vehicle Traffic — United States, 1975–2012.” Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report 64, no. 31 (August 14, 2015): 837. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Analyzes FARS bicycle fatality data for the period 1975–2012 and reports cyclist mortality on a per-population rate basis (a methodology that addresses denominator concerns inherent in absolute-count comparisons). Cyclist mortality rate fell from 0.41 to 0.23 deaths per 100,000 across the study period — a 44 percent decline. The steepest decline occurred among children aged under 15; age-specific mortality rates for adults aged 35–74 increased over the same period. The bicycles section of the modern-bargain chapter cites the Vargo et al. rate figure as the population-adjusted counterpart to the chapter’s absolute-count IIHS figure, making possible an apples-to-apples comparison against the per-vehicle-mile fatality rate the chapter cites for the automobile.

https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/preview/mmwrhtml/mm6431a1.htm.

Vaughn, Mark. “FCA Wins Legal Battle Against Mahindra and Roxor.” Autoweek, June 12, 2020.

https://www.autoweek.com/news/industry-news/a32851849/fca-wins-legal-battle-against-mahindra-and-roxor/. The dispute, before the U.S. International Trade Commission, turned on Jeep’s trade dress—the vehicle’s ornamental appearance, including its boxy body, flat vertical sides, and round headlights—and not on the vehicle’s mechanical design or on Mahindra’s underlying license. The Commission found the 2018–2019 Roxor infringed that trade dress while violating none of FCA’s registered trademarks. Mahindra restyled the front end, and in December 2020 the Commission cleared the redesigned vehicle, which resumed sale in the United States mechanically unchanged.

Trade-press report on General Motors CEO Mary Barra’s October 2025 confirmation that the company will phase out support for Apple CarPlay and Android Auto across its entire vehicle lineup — internal-combustion and electric — in favor of a proprietary infotainment system built on Google’s Android Automotive operating system, with full transition expected by the 2028 model year. October 22, 2025.

https://www.theverge.com/transportation/804562/gm-apple-carplay-android-auto-gas-cars-mary-barra.

Vesuvius Challenge. Project site and results archive. Documents the open competition to read the carbonized Herculaneum papyri without unrolling them. In August 2023 Casey Handmer, a former Jet Propulsion Laboratory physicist, identified a faint “crackle” texture in the scan data as ink — the first ink found inside an unopened scroll — and took the First Ink Prize for it. Building on Handmer’s discovery, Luke Farritor, then a twenty-one-year-old undergraduate in computer science with no training in papyrology or classical Greek, trained a model on the crackle shapes and in October 2023 recovered the Greek word for purple from inside an unopened scroll, winning the First Letters Prize; papyrologists confirmed the reading. Farritor subsequently shared the competition’s $700,000 Grand Prize with Youssef Nader and Julian Schilliger for recovering roughly fifteen columns of a previously unread philosophical text. Cited in the preface as an instance in which an outsider supplied the method and specialists inside the field supplied the domain judgment.

https://scrollprize.org/grandprize See Endnote Commentary.

Vesuvius Challenge. “First Word Discovered in Unopened Herculaneum Scroll by 21yo Computer Science Student.” 12 October 2023. https://scrollprize.org/firstletters. Records the First Letters Prize of forty thousand dollars to Luke Farritor for more than ten characters recovered from roughly four square centimetres of scroll, the word πορφύρας, the second-place award to Youssef Nader, and the First Ink Prize to Casey Handmer whose crackle pattern supplied Farritor’s training data. The seven-hundred-thousand-dollar grand prize followed in December 2023 to Farritor, Nader and Julian Schilliger for about two thousand characters. Scans produced by Brent Seales and EduceLab at the University of Kentucky; readings confirmed by papyrologists.

Village of Arlington Heights v. Metropolitan Housing Development Corp., 429 U.S. 252 (1977). Holding that a nonprofit developer that had contracted for a specific site and planned a specific low- and moderate-income housing project there had standing to challenge the village’s refusal to rezone the site, although the project still depended on financing and other contingencies, and that a prospective resident who sought housing near his job had standing as well; on the merits the Court found no proof of discriminatory purpose. Justice Powell wrote for the Court; Justice Marshall, joined by Justice Brennan, concurred in part and dissented in part; Justice White dissented; Justice Stevens took no part. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the authority for the standing of a would-be builder with a definite project blocked by law.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/429/252/.

von Neumann, John, and Oskar Morgenstern. Theory of Games and Economic Behavior. Princeton: Princeton University Press, 1944.

https://press.princeton.edu/books/paperback/9780691130613/theory-of-games-and-economic-behavior.

Walk-Behind Power Lawn Mower Safety Standard. Consumer Product Safety Commission, 16 C.F.R. Part 1205. Final Rule, 47 Fed. Reg. 1880 (January 14, 1982). Mandatory product-safety standard issued under the Consumer Product Safety Act, 15 U.S.C. §§ 2051 et seq.

https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-16/chapter-II/subchapter-B/part-1205.

Walterscheid, Edward C. The Nature of the Intellectual Property Clause: A Study in Historical Perspective. Buffalo, NY: William S. Hein, 2002.

https://www.amazon.com/Nature-Intellectual-Property-Clause-Perspective/dp/1575887096.

Warth v. Seldin, 422 U.S. 490 (1975). Five-to-four decision holding that low- and moderate-income residents of the Rochester, New York, area and allied organizations lacked standing to challenge the zoning ordinance of the suburb of Penfield as excluding them, because they had not shown that any specific project would have been built absent the ordinance or that relief would let them live there. Justice Powell wrote for the Court; Justice Douglas dissented, and Justice Brennan, joined by Justices White and Marshall, dissented. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the government’s closest authority against a buyer whose relief depends on a producer’s decision to build.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/422/490/.

Washington Examiner. “Industry, Not Environmentalists, Killed Incandescent Bulbs.” Washington Examiner opinion analysis. Documents the role of patent-protected lighting incumbents — through the National Electrical Manufacturers Association, representing GE Lighting, Signify (formerly Philips), Osram, Cree, and Acuity — in lobbying for the federal efficiency standards that displaced their unpatentable incandescent competitor. Cited in the bootleggers-and-Baptists section of the healthy-lighting chapter.

https://www.washingtonexaminer.com/opinion/2372297/industry-not-environmentalists-killed-incandescent-bulbs/.

Webber v. Virginia, 103 U.S. 344 (1880). Supreme Court decision reaffirming the Patterson v. Kentucky principle that letters patent granted by the United States do not exclude from state tax or license law the tangible property in which the invention or discovery is embodied. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as part of the Supreme Court line establishing that patents and regulation coexist.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/103/344/.

West Virginia v. Environmental Protection Agency, 597 U.S. 697 (2022).

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/597/20-1530/.

Westerman, Wayne, and John G. Elias. “Method and Apparatus for Integrating Manual Input.” U.S. Patent 6,323,846, filed January 25, 1999, issued November 27, 2001. Assigned to the University of Delaware. Foundational multi-touch capacitive touchscreen patent; expired 2019. Wheaton v. Peters, 33 U.S. (8 Pet.) 591 (1834). Foundational Supreme Court decision holding that there is no common-law copyright after publication; intellectual-property rights exist only as Congress grants them under the Patent and Copyright Clause. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the holding that establishes the Patent Clause as the sole constitutional source of intellectual-property rights, foreclosing the defense that a patent-equivalent commercial protection without an expiration date is something other than a patent right.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/33/591/.

Whitney, Eli. U.S. Patent for the cotton gin, granted 1794 (term expired 1808 under the fourteen-year rule of the Patent Act of 1790). The cotton gin entry in the chapter’s eight-product table. Whitney’s during-patent commercial model was service-based at a rate equivalent to roughly nineteen hundred dollars per gin per year; after expiration, mass-produced gins were selling for about twelve hundred dollars by the 1830s and roughly five hundred dollars by the 1880s, a fifty-six percent real-terms drop.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_gin.

Whittemore, Amos. U.S. Patent for the cotton carding machine, granted 1797 (term expired 1811 under the fourteen-year rule of the Patent Act of 1790). The cotton carding entry in the chapter’s eight-product table. The Whittemore design fell from a during-patent price of about thirty cents per pound of carded cotton to ten cents by the 1880s, a fifty percent real-terms drop.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amos_Whittemore.

Wickard v. Filburn, 317 U.S. 111 (1942). The Supreme Court held that Congress may, under the Commerce Clause, regulate even wheat a farmer grew for consumption on his own farm, on the ground that the aggregate effect of such activity on interstate commerce brought it within the commerce power. The decision marks the modern outer reach of that power. Cited in the chapter on the Patent Clause prong as the authority the regulatory wall’s defenders rely on for the breadth of federal regulatory jurisdiction, and as the case the prong answers: the commerce power, however broad, does not extend to nullifying what the Patent Clause commands at the end of the patent term.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/317/111/.

Wilkinson, David. U.S. Patent for a screw-cutting machine, granted 1798 (term expired 1812 under the fourteen-year rule of the Patent Act of 1790). The screw-cutting entry in the chapter’s eight-product table. The Wilkinson design fell from a during-patent price of about four dollars per pound of cut screws to roughly sixty cents per pound by the 1880s, a sixty-four percent real-terms drop, as automated threading entered competing American shops.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Wilkinson_(mechanic).

Williamson v. Lee Optical of Oklahoma, Inc., 348 U.S. 483 (1955). The unanimous Supreme Court decision establishing the modern rational-basis floor for federal economic classifications: a court asks only whether the classification is rationally related to some conceivable legitimate government interest, not whether the stated rationale is the real one. The decision upheld an Oklahoma statute requiring eyeglass replacement lenses to be made only from prescriptions written by licensed optometrists or ophthalmologists, against the challenge that the statute was economic protectionism dressed as health regulation. Cited in the chapter on objections considered as the doctrinal wall that almost every Fifth Amendment equal-protection challenge to federal economic regulation runs into and breaks on, and the principal reason equality under the law cannot serve as a standalone constitutional prong of the book’s argument.

https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/348/483/.

World Trade Organization. Agreement on Trade-Related Aspects of Intellectual Property Rights (TRIPS). Annex 1C of the Marrakesh Agreement Establishing the World Trade Organization, signed at Marrakesh on 15 April 1994. Article 33 establishes the minimum patent term as twenty years from the filing date. The Agreement binds all 166 WTO member nations.

https://www.wto.org/english/docs_e/legal_e/27-trips_04c_e.htm.

Wyden, Ron, and Edward J. Markey. Letter to FTC Chair Lina M. Khan. July 26, 2024. United States Senate. Joint senatorial inquiry to the Federal Trade Commission urging investigation into automaker disclosures of connected-vehicle driving-behavior data to consumer-reporting brokers; draws on oversight responses obtained directly from General Motors, Honda, and Hyundai documenting per-vehicle broker payments on the order of twenty-six cents (Honda) and sixty-one cents (Hyundai). Source for the surveillance chapter’s documentation of the broker-payment magnitudes.

https://www.wyden.senate.gov/imo/media/doc/wyden-markey_auto_privacy_letter_to_ftc.pdf.

First non-human to top HackerOne’s United States leaderboard, June 2025, as reported in “How an AI-Based ‘Pen Tester’ Became a Top Bug Hunter on HackerOne,” Dark Reading, August 2025; HackerOne co-founder Michiel Prins confirmed the ranking to Bloomberg. Roughly 1,060 vulnerability reports submitted over a few months, including a previously unknown flaw in Palo Alto Networks’ GlobalProtect affecting more than 2,000 hosts. Cited in the preface.

Yao, Xiaoxi, et al. “Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Electrocardiograms for Identification of Patients with Low Ejection Fraction: A Pragmatic, Randomized Clinical Trial.” Nature Medicine 27, no. 5 (2021): 815-819. One hundred twenty primary care teams, 22,641 adults; new diagnoses rose from 1.6 to 2.1 percent, an odds ratio of 1.32.

Zoltek Corp. v. United States, 442 F.3d 1345 (Fed. Cir. 2006) (per curiam), reh’g en banc denied, 464 F.3d 1335 (Fed. Cir. 2006), cert. denied, vacated in part, 672 F.3d 1309 (Fed. Cir. 2012) (en banc). The panel held that a patent holder whose invention the United States uses may not bring a Fifth Amendment takings claim, and that the statutory remedy Congress supplied is the only one available. Judge Plager dissented from the panel opinion; Judge Newman dissented from the denial of rehearing en banc. In 2012 the court sitting en banc vacated the 2006 opinion, giving as its grounds the panel’s construction of that statute. Commentators divide on what the vacatur did to the constitutional holding: some treat the takings analysis as vacated with the rest, others report that the reasoning was never repudiated and that later filings continue to cite the case for it. A Federal Circuit decision, not a Supreme Court decision. Cited in the objections chapter.

https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/442/1345/642048/.

Zwicky, Fritz. “Die Rotverschiebung von extragalaktischen Nebeln.” Helvetica Physica Acta 6 (1933): 110-127. Applied the virial theorem to the Coma cluster and named the discrepancy dunkle Materie.

See Endnote Commentary.

ZX Auto (Hebei Zhongxing Automobile Co., Ltd.). “GRANDTIGER.” ZX Auto Global Official Site. Manufacturer product page for the ZX Grand Tiger pickup truck. Specifications include 2.4L gasoline and 2.8T diesel engine options, 5-speed manual transmission, 2WD/4WD, double cab, and a 5310×1812×1800mm body. The chapter on patent-expired international vehicles cites the Grand Tiger as a working pickup truck derived from the 1997-to-2004 Toyota Hilux platform whose core mechanical patents have been in the public domain for more than a decade. Export pricing runs roughly $13,000 to $18,000 in the buyer markets the chapter discusses. ZX Auto, founded in 1949, is described on its own page as China’s first independent pickup brand.

https://en.zxauto.com.cn/GRANDTIGER.html.

Endnote Commentary

(Ordered to match the Endnotes section, keyed to the corresponding entry above. Substantive notes only; not every Endnote has a Commentary entry.)

The Chicago Manual of Style. 17th ed. The book follows the 17th edition (2017) because it is the most widely used scholarly standard for U.S. nonfiction trade and academic publishing, and because most U.S. literary agents and academic-trade publishers expect endnote apparatus formatted according to its conventions. The 18th edition, published in 2024, refines a number of digital-citation conventions but does not alter the core bibliographic forms the book’s apparatus uses. Where this book’s conventions diverge from strict Chicago style, the divergence is intentional and noted. The principal divergence is that every Endnote entry concludes with a working URL pointing to the source itself — the publisher’s canonical page for periodicals, books, and reports; Justia for U.S. Supreme Court cases; the Cornell University Legal Information Institute for federal statutes; Founders Online for Founding-era documents — rather than to a citation database or aggregator. The aim is to put the reader in front of the authoritative location of each source. Some publisher canonical pages sit behind paywalls; the book does not provide workarounds for those paywalls, but it does name the canonical location so that any reader with institutional access, or any reader who chooses to purchase, can verify the citation directly at the source. Where the publisher’s canonical URL is unavailable, the URL hierarchy falls back through DOI or scholarly database, with Amazon as a last resort for older works without stable publisher URLs. Court cases are linked to Justia. Federal statutes are linked to the Cornell University Legal Information Institute. Founding-era documents are linked to Founders Online at the National Archives.

See Endnote for full citation.

Boulware, David R., et al. “A Randomized Trial of Hydroxychloroquine as Postexposure Prophylaxis for Covid-19.” The Minnesota trial is the most-cited randomized evidence against hydroxychloroquine, and the chapter offers it as an exhibit rather than as a result. The critique that follows is this book’s own and is not the journal’s: that the dose administered exceeded the range the early-treatment protocols specify, that zinc was not co-administered although the protocols treat it as necessary, and that participants were enrolled past the window in which early treatment is said to work. On the last point the published record supplies a mechanism: the drug was shipped to participants, and the shipping interval meant a portion of the treatment arm did not begin dosing inside the four-day post-exposure window the trial reports. Readers should weigh the trial and the critique together. The point the chapter draws is narrower than either — that when a molecule is off patent, no sponsor has a commercial interest in designing the trial that would vindicate it.

See Endnote for full citation.

Class 8 tractor, remanufactured: the $80,000 should-cost figure. The book prices a faithfully remanufactured pre-2000 Class 8 highway tractor — a Peterbilt 379 or Kenworth W900 class chassis around a Caterpillar 3406E, first-year C-15, Cummins N14, or Detroit Series 60 certified to 1999 standards — at $80,000 in 2025 dollars, against $172,900 for a 2025 Kenworth T680 sleeper (see the Kenworth Truck Company endnote). The figure is a projection, and the chapters that use it say so; no such market exists while the wall stands. It is built from three components. The floor is the bare platform: a new Sinotruk HOWO 6×4 tractor of comparable horsepower sells for $28,000 to $35,000 FOB China with no aftertreatment (see the Sinotruk endnote), which is what a public-domain heavy-truck platform costs to build where no patent royalty, no diesel particulate filter, no selective catalytic reduction system, and no post-2000 electronics are carried. The remainder is American: domestic labor and materials, the independent engineering certification the remedy requires, and a domestic margin. The result sits at less than half the new-truck price rather than at one-fifth, because the book prices an American-built truck at American wages, not an import. The chapters that carry the figure — the opening chapter, the environmental chapter, the trucking chapter, the autonomous-vehicle chapter, the plaintiff chapter, the plaintiffs’ recovery chapter (which compares against the $161,850 T680 day cab), and the Epilogue — all use $80,000, and the per-mile arithmetic in the autonomous-vehicle chapter (about eleven cents of capital cost over 750,000 miles) is derived from it. The methodology chapter states the book’s base year: unless a year is given, prices are in 2025 dollars.

Code of Federal Regulations. “40 C.F.R. § 1068.240 — Exempting New Replacement Engines.” The replacement-engine exemption is the nearest thing outside aviation to the time-shift this book proposes, and its limits are the point. Under it a newly manufactured engine may be built to the standards of an earlier model year. Four conditions confine it. Only a certificate holder may build the engine. The engine may only replace one already placed into service. The equipment may be no more than forty years old. And the maker must first determine that no engine certified to current standards will fit; where an engine of an intermediate tier fits, that one must be used. Section 1068.120 does the parallel work for the rebuilder, who must hold a rebuilt engine to the certified configuration of its own model year or a cleaner one, not to today’s standards. So the EPA already accepts that an engine of an earlier year’s design may lawfully be made new and run. What it does not accept is that engine in a newly built machine, or a new engine of that kind made by anyone but a certificate holder. Compare the Cessna record (see the Federal Aviation Administration, Type Certificate Data Sheet 3A12 endnote): there the time-shift reached the whole airplane, but again only the certificate’s holder and those it licensed. In neither field does the time-shift reach the public. The comparison is this book’s own; no court has drawn it.

See Endnote for full citation.

Coin Laundry Association (CLA). The laundromat is the cleanest statutory hook in the American economy, and the reason is that no gap exists between the regulated equipment and the business model. A federal efficiency standard naming clothes washers as a covered product governs the only capital asset the business owns, so damages trace to the regulation at essentially one hundred percent — a posture no other category in the plaintiff chapter can claim. The arithmetic is unglamorous and that is its strength. A commercial washer-extractor runs from roughly three thousand dollars for a mid-size unit to well above twenty thousand for a high-capacity machine, and a working store is a floor of twenty, thirty, or more such units, so a full equipment retool is a six-figure capital event spent entirely on machines a federal standard governs. The patent-expiration anchor is equally uncontroversial: the foundational washing-machine patents date to the early twentieth century and expired generations ago. One caution on the trade body. It is a smaller organization than the restaurant or hotel associations and carries less Washington presence, and laundromat ownership is a small-business field, frequently immigrant-owned and pragmatically cost-driven, which makes it genuine but resource-limited as a litigation partner.

See Endnote for full citation.

Competitive-discount floor of fifteen percent. Two different fifteen-percent figures appear in the book, and they describe two different things. In the methodology chapter, the retrospective calculation of the $15.73 trillion floor applied a competitive-pressure discount in a range of eight to fifteen percent — component two of that calculation, the price reduction felt by the buyers who still purchase a new item rather than a remanufactured one. That range is a minor adjustment riding atop the dominant component, the direct savings of roughly forty to sixty percent realized by the buyers who purchase remanufactured goods, conservatively drawn from an observed international spread of fifty to eighty-eight percent. The fifteen-percent figure used in the recovery chapters is a forward-looking estimate, not a restatement of that historical range: it is the conservative floor of the competitive discount a reopened commons would deliver after a favorable ruling. Three independent reasons make fifteen percent conservative rather than optimistic. First, in a post-ruling economy no durable good sits atop a fully patented bill of materials; somewhere in every supply chain remanufacturing introduces discounting, so the effect compounds through the chain rather than capping at the final assembly. Second, the roughly ninety countries where patent-expired remanufacturing is already lawful show real-dollar prices well below a fifteen-percent discount off the American equivalent — often a half or a third of the U.S. price — so fifteen percent understates what those markets actually achieve.
