General Description: The federal government had settled another tribe on land guaranteed to the Shoshone by treaty, diminishing their interest. The Supreme Court held the loss compensable.

Justice Cardozo’s opinion supplied the phrase this book relies on — “Spoliation is not management” — and the principle it carries: a collective, non-individually-titled interest is property the government must pay to take. The book cites the case for that principle, not for any holding about the patent commons.

Why Case is Relevant: The Takings Clause Prong — Cardozo's “Spoliation is not management” — cited for the principle that a collective, non-individually-titled interest is still protected property, fitting the public's post-expiry right.

17

Aetna Life Insurance Co. v. Haworth (1937)

300 U.S. 227 (1937)

Score: 9x0

General Description: Aetna had issued disability-income policies to Edwin Haworth and had been paying benefits for several years when it concluded that Haworth was no longer disabled within the meaning of the contracts;

Haworth disagreed but had not yet sued for past or future benefits. Aetna sought a federal-court declaration that the policies were void and no further benefits were owed. The Supreme Court held that the federal Declaratory Judgment Act of 1934 allowed Aetna to file: where a controversy is real and immediate, a federal court may declare the parties’ legal rights without waiting for one of them to be sued or prosecuted. The case is the foundation of the constitutional-litigation path this book describes — the path on which the patent-bargain case will be filed before any plaintiff has been arrested, fined, or enjoined.

Why Case is Relevant: You No Longer Need to Break a Law to Challenge One — The leading early declaratory-judgment case: upheld the Declaratory Judgment Act against an Article III challenge, confirming a real controversy can be adjudicated before enforcement. The Filable Case — Cited for the same point in the filability chapter — a pre-enforcement dispute can be a genuine case or controversy.

18

Kellogg Co. v. National Biscuit Co. (1938)

305 U.S. 111 (1938)

Score: 6x2

General Description: National Biscuit claimed the exclusive right to the name “shredded wheat” and to the pillow shape of the biscuit. Both had been made under the Perky patents, which expired in 1912. Kellogg began making the same product in the same shape in 1922.

Justice Brandeis, for the Court, held that on expiration of the patents there passed to the public “not only the right to make the article as it was made during the patent period, but also the right to apply thereto the name by which it had become known.” The pillow shape went with it: upon expiration of the patents “the form, as well as the name, was dedicated to the public.” The sole surviving obligation on the newcomer was to identify its own product so that buyers were not deceived as to source. The suit arose in diversity as a common-law unfair-competition claim, and the case is cited for what it holds about post-expiration rights, not for a constitutional proposition. Justices McReynolds and Butler would have affirmed the court below, stating that Kellogg was fraudulently seeking to appropriate a goodwill built up at great cost by the respondent. Eight Justices sat; Cardozo had died in July 1938 and Frankfurter was not yet confirmed.

At 120 the Court rested the rule on Singer, at 185: “on the termination of the patent there passes to the public the right to make the machine in the form in which it was constructed during the patent.” The Court then gave a second and independent reason the shape had to remain available. At 122 it found the pillow shape functional: the evidence was persuasive that the cost of the biscuit would be increased and its high quality lessened if some other form were substituted. Requiring a different form would therefore have imposed a penalty on the newcomer — an observation this argument draws from the Court’s reasoning, not a holding the Court announced.

Why Case is Relevant: The Patent Clause Prong — The Court’s plainest statement that the form of the article, and not merely its underlying idea, enters the public domain when the patent expires. The faithful remake is the right the Court described, and Kellogg completes the line that runs from Singer (1896) through Scott Paper (1945) to Sears and Compco (1964). Objections Considered — The source-identification duty is the answer to the trademark objection: the remanufacturer must mark its own goods as its own, and that is all it must do. The functionality finding is the closer analogue still: the Court would not force a competitor into a different form when the original form was the one that worked.

19

Wickard v. Filburn (1942)

317 U.S. 111 (1942)

Score: 9x0

General Description: Roscoe Filburn grew wheat on his Ohio farm in excess of his federal allotment, intending the surplus for his own use, not for sale.

The Supreme Court upheld the penalty against him, reasoning that even purely local activity may be regulated under the Commerce Clause when its aggregate effect on interstate commerce is substantial. The case is the high-water mark of the modern commerce power and the authority a defender of the regulatory wall will cite for the proposition that Congress may regulate the manufacture of vehicles in commerce. The book does not dispute that power. The Patent Clause prong answers it on a different ground: a power broad enough to reach the wheat on Filburn’s farm is still not a power to cancel the public’s ownership of an expired invention, because no grant of federal power authorizes Congress to nullify what another clause of the Constitution commands.

Why Case is Relevant: The Patent Clause Prong — Cited to concede the breadth of the Commerce Clause power — the book does not dispute Congress's authority to regulate; its argument is about the Patent Clause limitation.

20

Betts v. Brady (1942)

316 U.S. 455 (1942)

Score: 6x3

General Description: An indigent defendant tried for robbery in Maryland asked the court to appoint counsel and was refused. Justice Roberts, for a six-Justice majority, held that due process did not require a State to furnish counsel in a noncapital case. Justice Black dissented, joined by Justices Douglas and Murphy.

The case stood for twenty-one years and was overruled in Gideon v. Wainwright (1963), where Justice Black wrote for a unanimous Court. The book cites it not for either holding but for the interval between them, and for the fact that the Court had been looking for a vehicle in which to reconsider Betts before Gideon supplied one.

Why Case is Relevant: When Outsiders Advance Constitutional Law and Other Disciplines — Cited to show that the case a court needs and the theory that decides it rarely come from the same person. Gideon supplied the case; Abe Fortas supplied the argument.

21

Skidmore v. Swift & Co. (1944)

323 U.S. 134 (1944)

Score: 9x0

General Description: Held that an agency interpretation is entitled to judicial respect proportional to its power to persuade — the “Skidmore deference” standard.

Why Case is Relevant: You No Longer Need to Break a Law to Challenge One — Cited for “Skidmore weight” — after Loper Bright, agency interpretations earn respect only to the extent persuasive, not deference — which matters to the patent-bargain case. The Filable Case — Same point in the filability chapter: the agency's view earns respect only if persuasive; “the thumb came off the scale.”

22

Korematsu v. United States (1944)

323 U.S. 214 (1944)

Score: 6x3

General Description: The Court upheld the wartime exclusion of Japanese Americans from the West Coast. Justice Black wrote for the majority; Justices Roberts, Murphy, and Jackson each dissented.

The claim of military necessity was made to the Court while contrary evidence sat in the government’s own files. In 1983 a federal court in San Francisco vacated Fred Korematsu’s conviction on a writ of coram nobis after that record surfaced, and in Trump v. Hawaii (2018) the Court stated that Korematsu had been gravely wrong the day it was decided.

Why Case is Relevant: When Outsiders Advance Constitutional Law and Other Disciplines — The most damning instance of a question nobody asked. The evidence was not merely available somewhere; it was in the government’s files, put there by the government, for forty years.

23

Scott Paper Co. v. Marcalus Manufacturing Co. (1945)

326 U.S. 249 (1945)

Score: 6x2

In Exam

General Description: Marcalus had assigned a patent to Scott Paper, then later built a product using a design covered by a different patent that had already expired.

Scott Paper invoked a contract doctrine — assignor estoppel — to stop its former assignor from competing. The Supreme Court refused. The public’s right to use an invention whose patent has expired, the Court held, is a matter of patent policy that a private estoppel rule cannot override. The patent laws, the Court held, “dedicate to public use the invention of an expired patent,” and do not contemplate that anyone may “withhold from the public the use of the invention of an expired patent.”

Why Case is Relevant: Four Problems, One Remedy — Listed among the cases affirming that what a patent covered belongs to the public at expiration. The Patent Clause Prong — Authority a plaintiff sets before the court: a patent holder cannot use a private contract to keep control after the patent expires. The Takings Clause Prong — Cited for describing the expired invention as dedicated to public use — the vested right the prong protects. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Referenced in the examination's synthesis of the public-domain cases (with Graham and Singer).

24

Brown v. Board of Education (1954)

347 U.S. 483 (1954)

Score: 9x0

General Description: Chief Justice Warren, for a unanimous Court, held that separate educational facilities are inherently unequal and that state-imposed school segregation violates the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment.

The work of Kenneth and Mamie Clark appears in footnote eleven of the opinion, in a list of social-science authorities. The book cites the case for what that footnote does and does not show: the Clarks were psychologists, neither was a lawyer, and their studies did not decide the case so much as supply a sentence the Court was prepared to write.

Why Case is Relevant: When Outsiders Advance Constitutional Law and Other Disciplines — Cited against the popular version of its own story. Outsiders furnished evidence; lawyers and Justices carried it to a holding.

25

Bolling v. Sharpe (1954)

347 U.S. 497 (1954)

Score: 9x0

In Exam

General Description: The federal companion to Brown v. Board of Education, decided the same day. Brown held that state-mandated school segregation violated the Fourteenth Amendment’s Equal Protection Clause.

Bolling faced the parallel question for the District of Columbia, which is not a state and to which the Fourteenth Amendment does not apply. The Court held that the Fifth Amendment’s Due Process Clause incorporates an equal-protection guarantee against the federal government. Federal action classifying similarly situated persons unequally is subject to constitutional review under the Fifth Amendment even where the Fourteenth Amendment has no purchase. The case is what makes any federal-equal-protection framing of the regulatory wall possible at all.

Why Case is Relevant: The Patent Clause Prong — Cited for the equal-protection obligation the Fifth Amendment imposes on the federal government (the wall is federal), though economic classifications get only rational-basis review. The Takings Clause Prong — Supplies the bridge applying equal protection to the federal government, reaffirmed in Adarand. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: the Fifth Amendment's Due Process Clause imposes a federal equal-protection obligation.

26

Williamson v. Lee Optical of Oklahoma, Inc. (1955)

348 U.S. 483 (1955)

Score: 8x0

General Description: Oklahoma forbade opticians to fit or duplicate lenses without a prescription. Justice Douglas, for the Court, upheld the statute; Justice Harlan took no part.

The opinion is the canonical statement of rational-basis review for economic regulation: the law may be needless and wasteful, and it is for the legislature rather than the courts to balance advantage against disadvantage. A court need only be able to conceive of a state of facts that would justify the classification.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — The reason equality cannot be pleaded as a standalone third prong. An equal-protection claim against the regulatory wall would meet rational-basis review and lose, which is why equality operates inside each of the two prongs rather than beside them.

27

Aro Manufacturing Co. v. Convertible Top Replacement Co. (1961)

365 U.S. 336 (1961)

Score: 6x3*2

General Description: The Supreme Court’s repair-and-reconstruction decision, arising from replacement tops for Ford and General Motors convertibles.

The Court held that replacing a worn, unpatented element of a patented combination is permissible repair, not infringing reconstruction, and that no unpatented element of a combination is entitled to the patent monopoly, however essential to the combination it may be. Cited for the principle that a combination patent reaches only the totality of its claimed elements.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — Cited on combination patents: no single unpatented element carries the monopoly — answering an objection about component-level rights.

28

Eastern Railroad Presidents Conference v. Noerr Motor Freight, Inc. (1961)

365 U.S. 127 (1961)

Score: 9x0

General Description: Twenty-four eastern railroads ran a publicity campaign against the trucking industry, and truckers sued under the Sherman Act. Justice Black, for a unanimous Court, held that the campaign was not an antitrust violation even though its purpose was to injure competitors.

With United Mine Workers v. Pennington (1965) the case established the Noerr-Pennington doctrine, which immunizes petitioning the government from antitrust liability. The doctrine is why the regulatory wall cannot be attacked as a conspiracy: the lobbying that raised it is protected activity.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — Cited to close an alternative theory rather than to open one. A plaintiff who reached for an antitrust account of how the wall was built would meet Noerr-Pennington, which is one reason the case must be constitutional.

29

Gideon v. Wainwright (1963)

372 U.S. 335 (1963)

Score: 9x0

General Description: Clarence Earl Gideon, tried in Florida without counsel, wrote his own petition to the Supreme Court in pencil. Justice Black wrote for a unanimous Court, overruling Betts v. Brady and holding that the Sixth Amendment right to counsel binds the States through the Fourteenth.

Gideon is the archetype everyone reaches for when an outsider is said to have changed constitutional law, and the book cites it to show that the archetype does not fit. The Court appointed Abe Fortas to argue the case, and the winning argument was his. Gideon supplied the vehicle, not the theory.

Why Case is Relevant: When Outsiders Advance Constitutional Law and Other Disciplines — The case that most nearly answers the objection that an outsider never wins, examined and found not to answer it. The two jobs — finding the injury and arguing the law — sat in different people.

30

Sears, Roebuck & Co. v. Stiffel Co. (1964)

376 U.S. 225 (1964)

Score: 9x0*11

General Description: Stiffel's pole-lamp patents were held invalid for want of invention. Sears copied the lamp and sold it cheaply. Stiffel won below on an Illinois unfair-competition theory.

Justice Black, for a unanimous Court, reversed. An article on which the patent has expired, like an unpatentable article, is in the public domain and may be made and sold by whoever chooses to do so. A State may not use unfair-competition law to give what amounts to a patent monopoly on an unpatented article, though it may require labeling to prevent deception as to source. The holding runs against a State, not against a federal agency. The same opinion, at 230, restates what expiration does: when the patent expires the monopoly expires with it, and the right to make the article — “including the right to make it in precisely the shape it carried when patented” — passes to the public, citing Kellogg and Singer. That passage is the Court’s reasoning, not its holding, and is cited as such.

Why Case is Relevant: The Patent Clause Prong — The Court's plainest statement that the expired or unpatentable article belongs to the public and may be made by anyone. Cited for that principle. The holding is a state-law preemption holding: it bars a State from re-erecting patent-like protection. The Court has never applied the principle against a federal regulator, and this argument does not claim it has.

31

Brulotte v. Thys Co. (1964)

379 U.S. 29 (1964)

Score: 8x1

General Description: A hop-picking machine was sold under a license requiring royalties that ran past the expiration of every patent embodied in it. Justice Douglas, for the Court, held the post-expiration royalty provisions unenforceable. Justice Harlan dissented.

The patents in the case expired on schedule and no term was extended by a day. The Court struck the arrangement down anyway, because the practical consequence of expiration — the public’s unrestricted freedom in the released invention — had been withheld by another instrument. Reaffirmed on stare decisis grounds in Kimble v. Marvel Entertainment (2015).

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — First rung of the ladder answering the enumerated-powers objection. Had “limited Times” been a scheduling instruction, Brulotte would have been decided the other way in a paragraph.

32

Graham v. John Deere Co. (1966)

383 U.S. 1 (1966)

Score: 8x0

In Exam

General Description: A dispute over the validity of a patent on a clamp for plow shanks. The Supreme Court used the case to set out how courts decide whether an invention is “non-obvious” enough to deserve a

patent — and, more important for this book, to describe the constitutional nature of the Patent Clause itself. The Court called the clause both a grant of power to Congress and a limitation on that power. In the Court’s own words, the clause “is both a grant of power and a limitation,” and “Congress in the exercise of the patent power may not overreach the restraints imposed by the stated constitutional purpose.” The Court continued, at 5–6, that Congress “may not authorize the issuance of patents whose effects are to remove existent knowledge from the public domain, or to restrict free access to materials already available.”

Why Case is Relevant: Four Problems, One Remedy — Listed among the cases affirming that an expired patent's subject belongs to the public. The Patent Clause Prong — The chapter's keystone authority: Graham holds the Patent Clause is “both a grant of power and a limitation upon the exercise of that power.” Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: the Patent Clause is both a grant of power and a limitation on it; Congress may not overreach in exercising the patent power.

33

Menominee Tribe of Indians v. United States (1968)

391 U.S. 404 (1968)

Score: 6x2

General Description: The Menominee Tribe sued the United States in the Court of Claims for compensation after federal legislation terminated the tribe’s recognized status.

The Supreme Court held that treaty-guaranteed hunting and fishing rights survived the termination, because Congress had not clearly abrogated them. In this book the case is one of three showing a settled pattern: a tribe may bring a compensation claim against the federal government through ordinary litigation, breaking no law to do so.

Why Case is Relevant: You No Longer Need to Break a Law to Challenge One — Example of the older plaintiff pattern — the Tribe filed in the Court of Claims for compensation — illustrating that a plaintiff need not break a law to sue.

34

United States Department of Agriculture v. Moreno (1973)

413 U.S. 528 (1973)

Score: 7x2

In Exam

General Description: Held that a food-stamp restriction aimed at excluding “hippie” households failed rational-basis review; bare desire to harm a group is not a legitimate interest.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — Paired with Romer as authority that a classification resting only on a bare desire to harm a disfavored group fails rational-basis review. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: a classification fails rational-basis review where its only rationale is a bare desire to harm a disfavored group.

35

Kewanee Oil Co. v. Bicron Corp. (1974)

416 U.S. 470 (1974)

Score: 6x2*13

General Description: Ohio trade-secret law protected the process Harshaw Chemical used to grow synthetic crystals. The Sixth Circuit held that federal patent law preempted the state protection. The Supreme Court reversed.

Chief Justice Burger, for the Court, held that state trade-secret law survives federal patent preemption. The reasoning: trade-secret protection is weaker than a patent, it withdraws nothing from the public domain, and it poses no real risk of deterring inventors from seeking patents, so partial preemption as to patentable subject matter would burden the States without serving federal patent policy. Justice Marshall concurred in the result; Justice Douglas, joined by Justice Brennan, dissented on the view that a State may not confer protection on what Congress left free. The holding is about a parallel state regime. It does not address what a federal agency may do to a design whose patent has already expired; that distinction is this argument’s, not the Court’s.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — Adverse authority — cited for the proposition that federal patent policy does not displace every other legal regime touching an invention, and distinguished on the ground that trade-secret law leaves the public free to reverse-engineer and copy what it learns, while the certification barrier forbids the lawful sale of the copy itself.

36

Warth v. Seldin (1975)

422 U.S. 490 (1975)

Score: 5x4

Not in Exam

General Description: Low- and moderate-income residents of the Rochester, New York, area and allied organizations challenged the zoning ordinance of the suburb of Penfield, alleging that it kept out the affordable housing they would otherwise have been able to live in.

Justice Powell, for the Court, held that the plaintiffs lacked standing. They had not shown that any specific project would have been built absent the ordinance, or that relief would let them live in Penfield; their injury depended on choices builders not before the court might never make. Justice Douglas dissented, and Justice Brennan, joined by Justices White and Marshall, dissented.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — The government’s closest authority against a buyer whose relief depends on a producer’s decision to build. The chapter concedes it governs a buyer suing alone and answers it with Village of Arlington Heights, decided two years later.

37

Simon v. Eastern Kentucky Welfare Rights Organization (1976)

426 U.S. 26 (1976)

Score: 8x0*23

Not in Exam

General Description: Indigent plaintiffs and welfare-rights organizations challenged an Internal Revenue Service ruling that relaxed the free-care condition hospitals had to meet to keep their tax exemption, alleging that hospitals had turned them away.

Justice Powell, for the Court, held that the plaintiffs lacked standing. Their injury could be traced to the ruling only through the independent decisions of hospitals not before the court, and it was speculative whether a judgment against the Treasury officials would lead any hospital to serve them.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — Not cited in the chapter, which answers the same objection through Warth v. Seldin. Listed because a defender of the wall would cite it as the original holding that a plaintiff’s injury cannot rest on choices by third parties not before the court.

38

Village of Arlington Heights v. Metropolitan Housing Development Corp. (1977)

429 U.S. 252 (1977)

Score: 5x3*24

Not in Exam

General Description: A nonprofit developer contracted for a site in a Chicago suburb and planned a specific low- and moderate-income housing project there. The village refused to rezone the site, and the developer and prospective residents sued.

Justice Powell, for the Court, held that the developer had standing: the refusal to rezone was the barrier to a specific project, and the remaining uncertainty over financing and other contingencies did not defeat that injury. A prospective resident who sought housing near his job had standing as well. On the merits the Court found no proof of discriminatory purpose and reversed.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — The authority for the standing of a would-be builder with a definite project blocked by law — the reason the objection’s answer puts an independent remanufacturer among the plaintiffs.

39

Hunt v. Washington State Apple Advertising Commission (1977)

432 U.S. 333 (1977)

Score: 8x0

General Description: North Carolina passed a statute requiring closed shipping containers of apples to display the U.S. grade rather than any state grade.

The Washington State Apple Advertising Commission, representing Washington apple growers, sued, claiming the statute burdened interstate commerce. The Supreme Court used the case to set out the three-prong test for when a trade or membership association may sue on behalf of its members: at least one member must have standing in his own right, the interests at stake must be germane to the association’s purpose, and neither the claim nor the relief may require the participation of individual members. The case is the doctrinal basis for associational standing — the theory under which trucker associations, farm associations, and remanufacturer associations could sue in the patent-bargain case.

Why Case is Relevant: Direct and Associational Plaintiff Categories — The governing associational-standing rule: an association may sue on its members' behalf when they would have standing to sue individually. Why You Want to Be the Plaintiff — Cited for the same associational-standing rule in the chapter on choosing the plaintiff.

40

Penn Central Transportation Co. v. City of New York (1978)

438 U.S. 104 (1978)

Score: 6x3

In Exam

General Description: New York City’s landmarks law blocked the owner of Grand Central Terminal from constructing an office tower above the station. The owner claimed the restriction took its property.

The Supreme Court ruled for the city and, in doing so, set out the framework that still governs most regulatory-takings claims: courts weigh the economic impact of the regulation on the owner, its interference with reasonable investment-backed expectations, and the character of the government action. Penn Central is the balancing test for regulations that diminish a property’s value without destroying it.

Why Case is Relevant: The Environmental Question — The investment-backed-expectations test, cited to show the strategic builder who enters after a regulation has weak takings footing. The Takings Clause Prong — The multi-factor regulatory-takings balancing test — one of the two roads the prong analyzes. Objections Considered — Cited (with Lucas) to show a latecomer loses the takings analysis for lack of investment-backed expectations. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: whether a regulation is a taking is judged by weighing economic impact, interference with expectations, and the character of the government action.

41

Aronson v. Quick Point Pencil Co. (1979)

440 U.S. 257 (1979)

Score: 9x0

In Exam

General Description: An inventor licensed an unpatented keyholder design to a manufacturer in exchange for a royalty, with a contractual provision reducing the royalty if no patent ever issued. No patent did.

The manufacturer eventually argued that the royalty contract was unenforceable as inconsistent with federal patent policy, since the keyholder’s design was free for all to copy. The Supreme Court rejected the argument. Federal patent law leaves the unpatented public domain free for everyone, but it does not bar private parties from contracting on their own terms for the use of a design, and a state contract law that lets them do so is not preempted. The case is the modern restatement of the Patterson v. Kentucky principle that patent law and ordinary regulation coexist.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — Cited as the modern restatement that federal patent law does not displace ordinary state law governing a product. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: federal patent law does not displace ordinary state regulation of a product.

42

Andrus v. Allard (1979)

444 U.S. 51 (1979)

Score: 9x0

In Exam

General Description: The federal Eagle Protection Act, joined by the Migratory Bird Treaty Act, made it unlawful to sell artifacts incorporating eagle parts even where the parts had been lawfully obtained before the statute.

The owners of pre-existing inventories of Indian artifacts containing eagle feathers sued, claiming the sale ban was a Fifth Amendment taking of their property. The Supreme Court unanimously rejected the claim. A regulation that destroys one of the strands in the bundle of property rights — here, the right to sell — is not necessarily a taking, the Court reasoned, because possession, transport, and noncommercial transfer of the artifacts remained lawful. The case is the leading adverse Takings precedent the patent-bargain case will have to distinguish; the chapter on objections considered develops the distinction.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — Opens the adverse-precedent discussion: a federal ban on commercial sale is not necessarily a taking where other uses remain. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: a ban on the commercial sale of lawfully held property is not necessarily a taking where other uses remain.

43

United States v. Sioux Nation of Indians (1980)

448 U.S. 371 (1980)

Score: 8x1

General Description: Congress seized the Black Hills of South Dakota from the Sioux by an 1877 Act, in violation of an earlier treaty.

The Supreme Court held the seizure a taking that required just compensation, though the land was held by the Sioux collectively rather than by any individual owner. The book uses the case two ways: as proof that a taking claim can be litigated without anyone breaking a law, and as authority that common, non-individually-titled property falls within the Just Compensation Clause. The book does not claim the case reached the Expired Patent Commons; it concerned Indian treaty lands. It claims only that the case settles the governing principle — property need not be privately and individually titled to be compensable — and that extending that principle to the commons is a short, logical step the chapter asks the court to take.

Why Case is Relevant: You No Longer Need to Break a Law to Challenge One — The most directly relevant plaintiff-pattern case — the Tribe sued for compensation for the Black Hills — no law needed to be broken. The Takings Clause Prong — Cited for the holding that Congress's 1877 seizure of the Black Hills was a taking requiring just compensation, though the land belonged to the tribe collectively.

44

Loretto v. Teleprompter Manhattan CATV Corp. (1982)

458 U.S. 419 (1982)

Score: 6x3

In Exam

General Description: A New York statute required landlords to permit cable-television operators to install a small cable box and a few feet of cable on the exterior of apartment buildings, in exchange for a one-time payment of one dollar set by a state commission.

Jean Loretto, a New York City landlord, challenged the mandate as a taking. The Supreme Court held that any permanent physical occupation of property authorized by government, however minor, is a per se taking — categorically requiring compensation regardless of the economic impact on the owner or the public interest in the occupation. The case is the per se physical-occupation line that runs through Horne v. Department of Agriculture (2015) and Cedar Point Nursery v. Hassid (2021).

Why Case is Relevant: The Environmental Question — Listed among the takings categories (physical occupation) the Patent Clause remedy does not disturb. The Takings Clause Prong — The per se physical-occupation rule that predates Lucas: “a permanent physical occupation of property is a taking,” however small. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: a permanent physical occupation of property is a per se taking.

45

Alfred L. Snapp & Son, Inc. v. Puerto Rico (1982)

458 U.S. 592 (1982)

Score: 8x0

General Description: Puerto Rico sued Virginia apple growers alleging discrimination against Puerto Rican farmworkers in favor of temporary foreign labor. Justice White, for the Court, held that Puerto Rico had parens patriae standing; Justice Powell took no part.

The opinion is the modern statement of the doctrine: a sovereign may sue on a quasi-sovereign interest, which includes the health and well-being — physical and economic — of a substantial segment of its population, provided the sovereign articulates an interest apart from those of particular private parties.

Why Case is Relevant: Sovereign and Public-Fiduciary Plaintiffs — The controlling authority for a State attorney general suing on behalf of households injured by the wall. Supplies the quasi-sovereign interest the chapter’s sovereign plaintiff categories rest on.

46

Railway Labor Executives’ Association v. Gibbons (1982)

455 U.S. 457 (1982)

Score: 9x0*15

General Description: Congress enacted a statute governing the liquidation of a single bankrupt railroad and the claims of its employees. Justice Rehnquist, for the Court, held that the statute was a bankruptcy law, that it was not uniform, and that the Commerce Clause could not be used to enact it.

To hold that Congress may enact non-uniform bankruptcy laws under the commerce power, the Court reasoned, would eradicate from the Constitution the limitation the Bankruptcy Clause imposes. The case is the Court’s clearest application of the specific-governs-the-general canon to two clauses standing side by side in Article I, Section 8.

Why Case is Relevant: The Patent Clause Prong — The anchor for the argument that a limitation written into one clause of Article I, Section 8 cannot be escaped by legislating under another. Objections Considered — Answers the enumerated-powers objection directly. Carrying the reasoning from the Bankruptcy Clause to “limited Times” is the book’s step and not the Court’s, and the entry says so.

47

Ruckelshaus v. Monsanto Co. (1984)

467 U.S. 986 (1984)

Score: 7x1*3

In Exam

General Description: Monsanto challenged a federal pesticide statute that allowed the EPA to use and disclose the confidential test data that companies submitted for registration.

The Supreme Court held that a trade secret is property protected by the Takings Clause, even though it has no physical form, and that a regulation appropriating the value of that confidential data can be a taking — though whether it is depends on whether the company held a reasonable investment-backed expectation that the data would stay confidential. The case establishes that intangible rights of defined economic value are property for Fifth Amendment purposes.

Why Case is Relevant: The Takings Clause Prong — The case the prong leans on hardest — trade secrets are protected property under the Takings Clause. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: intangible property, such as a trade secret, is protected under the Takings Clause.

48

United States v. 50 Acres of Land (1984)

Score: 9x0

General Description: Held that a public condemnee is entitled to fair market value, not the cost of a substitute facility, as just compensation.

Why Case is Relevant: The Environmental Question — Compensation is fair market value at the time of the taking — the remanufacturer who tools a factory to attract a taking gets no windfall.

49

Chevron U.S.A., Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc. (1984)

467 U.S. 837 (1984)

Score: 6x0

General Description: Justice Stevens, for the Court, upheld the EPA’s plantwide definition of a stationary source under the Clean Air Act and set out the two-step framework that governed judicial review of agency statutory interpretation for forty years. Justices Marshall, Rehnquist, and O’Connor took no part.

Chevron deference is the reason the regulatory wall stood unexamined for so long: a court asked whether an agency had exceeded its statute deferred to the agency’s own answer wherever the statute was ambiguous. Overruled by Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo (2024), which is case 84 in this table.

Why Case is Relevant: Why Nobody Noticed: How a Constitutional Violation Hides in Plain Sight — The doctrinal reason the wall was never tested. The Filable Case: Why 2026 and Not Before — Its overruling is the first of the four conditions that make the case filable now and not before.

50

Heckler v. Chaney (1985)

470 U.S. 821 (1985)

Score: 9x0

General Description: An agency’s decision not to undertake an enforcement action is presumptively committed to the agency’s discretion and unreviewable under the Administrative Procedure Act.

Why Case is Relevant: The Anti-Backsliding Provision — Cited for the holding that an agency's decision not to enforce is presumptively unreviewable — relevant to how the anti-backsliding regime operates.

51

City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, Inc. (1985)

473 U.S. 432 (1985)

Score: 9x0*16

General Description: A Texas city denied a special-use permit for a group home. Justice White, for a six-Justice majority, declined to treat the intellectually disabled as a quasi-suspect class but struck the ordinance down as applied under rational-basis review. Justices Marshall, Brennan, and Blackmun concurred in the judgment in part and dissented in part.

The case is the leading example of rational basis with teeth: a classification resting on irrational prejudice fails even the deferential standard. Justice Marshall’s separate opinion objected that the Court’s review was heightened scrutiny under another name.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — The escape route from Williamson v. Lee Optical, and the reason the escape is not taken. Cleburne shows what a successful equality claim needs; the regulatory wall has stated rationales that Cleburne’s ordinance lacked.

52

Bowers v. Hardwick (1986)

478 U.S. 186 (1986)

Score: 5x4

General Description: Justice White, for a five-Justice majority, upheld a Georgia sodomy statute, stating in the course of the opinion that proscriptions against such conduct had ancient roots. Justices Blackmun and Stevens each dissented.

Seventeen years later a brief filed by historians in Lawrence v. Texas took the historical claim apart using sources that had been in print the whole time. The record did not say what Bowers said it said, and the category of person the opinion described did not exist in the period it pointed to. The Court overruled Bowers in 2003.

Why Case is Relevant: When Outsiders Advance Constitutional Law and Other Disciplines — The cleanest instance of the unasked question. Nothing was discovered; a court asserted a fact, everyone believed it, and for seventeen years nobody checked it against scholarship that already said otherwise.

53

First English Evangelical Lutheran Church of Glendale v. County of Los Angeles (1987)

482 U.S. 304 (1987)

Score: 6x3

In Exam

General Description: A county ordinance temporarily barred a church from rebuilding on flood-prone land it owned. The church argued the ordinance had taken its property for the period the ban was in force.

The Supreme Court used the case to settle what the Takings Clause requires: it does not forbid the government from taking property, but it requires the payment of just compensation when the government does. A finding that a regulation effects a taking establishes that compensation is owed; it is not, by itself, an order striking the regulation down.

Why Case is Relevant: The Takings Clause Prong — Cited for the remedy point: the Takings Clause does not forbid the taking; it requires compensation. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: the Takings Clause does not forbid government from taking property; it requires just compensation.

54

Nollan v. California Coastal Commission (1987)

483 U.S. 825 (1987)

Score: 5x4

General Description: The Nollans owned a beachfront lot in Ventura County and applied for a permit to replace their bungalow with a larger house. The California Coastal Commission granted the permit on condition that they dedicate a public easement across their beach.

The Supreme Court held that a regulatory condition must bear an essential nexus to the harm the regulation claims to address; an easement allowing the public to walk along the beach had no such nexus to the visual obstruction the larger house was said to cause. The case is the first prong of the modern proportionality test for regulatory conditions on property use, completed in Dolan v. City of Tigard (1994) and reaffirmed in Sheetz v. County of El Dorado (2024).

Why Case is Relevant: The Takings Clause Prong — Cited (with Dolan) on the exactions doctrine, and the argument that it reaches only individualized conditions — which the book answers via Sheetz.

55

Allegheny Pittsburgh Coal Co. v. County Commission of Webster County (1989)

488 U.S. 336 (1989)

Score: 9x0

General Description: The unanimous Supreme Court decision holding that a county tax-assessment system that valued recently-acquired coal properties at full current value while leaving long-held properties at

decades-old assessments violated the Equal Protection Clause.

Why Case is Relevant: The Takings Clause Prong — The equality-within-takings authority: a county taxed recently-acquired property far above comparable older holdings — the asymmetry the prong invokes. Objections Considered — The equality argument inside the Takings prong, walked at length — similarly situated property treated unequally.

56

Bonito Boats, Inc. v. Thunder Craft Boats, Inc. (1989)

489 U.S. 141 (1989)

Score: 9x0

General Description: Florida barred the use of a direct molding process to duplicate unpatented boat hulls. Bonito Boats, which had never sought a patent on its hull, sued a competitor under the statute.

Justice O'Connor, for a unanimous Court, held the Florida statute preempted. The Patent Clause reflects a balance between encouraging innovation and avoiding monopolies that stifle competition, and the Clause contains both a grant of power and limitations on its exercise. By giving patent-like protection to designs in general circulation that Congress left unprotected, Florida entered a field reserved to Congress. Like Sears, the holding runs against a State. The opinion also states, at 151, that “free exploitation of ideas will be the rule, to which the protection of a federal patent is the exception,” and that the ultimate goal of the patent system is to bring new designs and technologies into the public domain through disclosure.

Why Case is Relevant: The Patent Clause Prong — Reaffirms Sears and supplies the Court's modern statement that the Patent Clause is both a grant and a limitation. Cited for the public-domain principle and the grant-and-limitation reading. State-law preemption authority; the Court has not applied it to a federal agency, and the step from this holding to the federal wall is this argument's own.

57

Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife (1992)

504 U.S. 555 (1992)

Score: 6x3

General Description: Established the three-part constitutional standing test: injury in fact, causation, and redressability.

Why Case is Relevant: Sovereign and Public-Fiduciary Plaintiffs — The source of the three-part standing test — injury in fact, causation, redressability. Direct and Associational Plaintiff Categories — Cited for the generalized-grievance bar the chapter's plaintiff categories must clear. Objections Considered — Cited for the rule that a plaintiff who is the object of the government’s action ordinarily has little trouble showing injury and redress, the reason the would-be builder belongs among the plaintiffs.

58

Lucas v. South Carolina Coastal Council (1992)

505 U.S. 1003 (1992)

Score: 6x3

In Exam

General Description: A developer bought two beachfront lots, after which a new state coastal statute barred him from building anything on them.

The Supreme Court held that when a regulation deprives an owner of all economically beneficial use of his land, it is a taking without any case-by-case balancing — a taking per se. Total deprivation of use, the Court reasoned, is different in kind from the partial diminution of value that ordinary regulation produces, and it requires compensation categorically.

Why Case is Relevant: The Environmental Question — Listed among the per se takings categories (total economic wipeout) the remedy does not disturb. The Takings Clause Prong — The per se total-wipeout rule: a regulation denying all economically beneficial use is a taking — the shorter, steeper road. Objections Considered — Cited (with Penn Central) to show a post-regulation entrant fails the takings analysis. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: a regulation denying all economically beneficial use of property is a per se taking.

59

Nordlinger v. Hahn (1992)

505 U.S. 1 (1992)

Score: 8x1*17

General Description: Justice Blackmun, for the Court, upheld California’s acquisition-value property-tax scheme under Proposition 13, which assessed newly purchased property at purchase price while capping annual increases on long-held property at two percent. Justice Stevens dissented.

The Court confined Allegheny Pittsburgh Coal Co. v. County Commission of Webster County (1989) to the rare case in which the facts precluded any plausible inference that an acquisition-value policy explained the disparity. Justice Thomas concurred in part while stating that Allegheny Pittsburgh could not be distinguished.

Why Case is Relevant: The Takings Clause Prong — The stated limit on the chapter’s use of Allegheny Pittsburgh, conceded rather than left for an opponent to find. Objections Considered — The strongest authority against a standalone equality prong: a classification turning on when property was acquired, sustained on rational basis.

60

Nixon v. United States (1993)

506 U.S. 224 (1993)

Score: 9x0

General Description: The Supreme Court’s leading modern application of the political-question doctrine to internal Congressional procedure: the Senate’s procedure for trying an impeachment was held

nonjusticiable because the Constitution textually commits the matter to the Senate and the courts lack judicially manageable standards for second-guessing the chamber’s procedural choices.

Why Case is Relevant: Hybrid Legislative Voting — Cited on the political-question doctrine and impeachment procedure — courts' reluctance to police the chamber's internal voting mechanics, which shapes the hybrid-voting design.

61

Dolan v. City of Tigard (1994)

512 U.S. 374 (1994)

Score: 5x4

General Description: A hardware-store owner applied for a permit to expand her store and pave her parking lot. The city granted the permit on condition that she dedicate part of her property to a public greenway and bike path.

She objected that the conditions were unrelated in scope to the impact of her expansion. The Supreme Court agreed with her on the second prong of the modern proportionality test it had begun building seven years earlier in Nollan v. California Coastal Commission: a regulatory condition imposed on the use of property must bear an essential nexus to the harm the regulation claims to address (Nollan) and must be roughly proportional in scope to that harm (Dolan). The two-prong test underlies Sheetz v. County of El Dorado (2024), which extended it from individual administrative decisions to fees set by general legislation.

Why Case is Relevant: The Takings Clause Prong — Cited with Nollan on the exactions doctrine; the Court rejected the distinction limiting it to individualized conditions.

62

United States v. Lopez (1995)

514 U.S. 549 (1995)

Score: 5x4

General Description: The Gun-Free School Zones Act of 1990 made it a federal offense to possess a firearm within a school zone. A San Antonio high-school student carried a concealed handgun to school and was convicted under the Act. He argued that Congress lacked power under the Commerce Clause to regulate the possession of a gun near a school.

Chief Justice Rehnquist, for a five-Justice majority, held the Act beyond the commerce power. Possession of a gun in a school zone is not economic activity and does not substantially affect interstate commerce; the Court declined to pile inference on inference to reach it. The first decision in sixty years to hold a federal statute outside the commerce power.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — The model for the enumerated-powers question in The Rational-Basis Trap. The Court did not ask whether Congress could rationally have thought guns near schools harmful; it asked whether the activity was within the power. The Patent Clause prong must be pleaded in that form, or rational-basis review swallows it.

63

Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña (1995)

515 U.S. 200 (1995)

Score: 5x4*18

General Description: Justice O’Connor announced the judgment and delivered the opinion of the Court in relevant part, holding that all racial classifications imposed by any federal, state, or local governmental actor must be analyzed under strict scrutiny. Justices Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg each dissented.

The decision confirms that the equal-protection obligation Bolling v. Sharpe read into the Fifth Amendment binds the federal government with the same force the Fourteenth Amendment carries against the States. The strict-scrutiny holding governs racial classifications; the federal-obligation premise is what the book uses.

Why Case is Relevant: The Takings Clause Prong — Supplies the federal bridge for treating unequal federal treatment as a constitutional matter. The extension from race to a vintage classification is the argument’s own step, and the chapter says so.

64

Romer v. Evans (1996)

517 U.S. 620 (1996)

Score: 6x3

In Exam

General Description: Colorado voters adopted a constitutional amendment forbidding the state or any locality to extend antidiscrimination protections on the basis of sexual orientation.

The Supreme Court struck the amendment down on rational-basis review, holding that the only purpose the amendment could rationally be said to serve was disqualification of an unpopular group from political participation, which is not a legitimate state interest. The case extends the Cleburne line of “rational basis with bite” — the narrow set of cases in which an equal-protection challenge succeeds because the only rationale a court can find is identifiable animus.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — Paired with Moreno: a classification explained only by animus fails even rational-basis review. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: a classification fails rational-basis review where explained only by animus toward those it disadvantages.

65

United Food and Commercial Workers Union Local 751 v. Brown Group, Inc. (1996)

517 U.S. 544 (1996)

Score: 9x0

General Description: A union sued under the WARN Act seeking backpay for its members. Justice Souter, for a unanimous Court, held that the third prong of the Hunt associational-standing test is prudential rather than constitutional, and may therefore be abrogated by Congress.

The holding matters because Hunt v. Washington State Apple Advertising Commission (1977) had suggested that a damages action running solely to members would bar associational standing. Brown Group establishes that the bar is one Congress can lift, and that only the first prong is grounded in Article III.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — Answers the objection that an association cannot sue for member damages. The limitation is prudential, and the chapter distinguishes what Article III requires from what prudence has added.

66

Printz v. United States (1997)

521 U.S. 898 (1997)

Score: 5x4

General Description: The Brady Handgun Violence Prevention Act required county sheriffs and other local chief law-enforcement officers to perform federal background checks on prospective handgun purchasers.

Sheriff Jay Printz and Sheriff Richard Mack sued, arguing that the federal government could not compel state officers to enforce federal regulatory programs. The Supreme Court agreed. The federal government may not commandeer the legislative or executive officers of the states for federal regulatory ends; doing so violates the structure of dual sovereignty the Constitution establishes. The case does not address “limited Times,” but it is the constitutional ground for the sheriff plaintiff — the elected county sheriff suing as a constitutional officer to challenge a federal regulatory program that converts him into an enforcement adjunct.

Why Case is Relevant: You No Longer Need to Break a Law to Challenge One — The elected sheriff's precedent — two county sheriffs challenged a federal statute and won — a plaintiff pattern requiring no law be broken. Sovereign and Public-Fiduciary Plaintiffs — Cited for the elected sheriff's footing as a constitutional officer — a posture that depends on the sheriff being elected. Why You Want to Be the Plaintiff — Cited as a precise, recent precedent for the elected-official plaintiff.

67

Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank (1999)

527 U.S. 627 (1999)

Score: 5x4

In Exam

General Description: College Savings Bank sued a Florida state agency for infringing its patent. The question was whether Congress could strip the States of sovereign immunity and expose them to patent-infringement suits;

the Court held that, in the manner Congress had attempted, it could not. The premise the Court worked from is what matters for this book, and the Court stated it plainly: a patent is a form of property, protected as other property is.

Why Case is Relevant: The Patent Clause Prong — Completes the property point: patents are a species of property. The Takings Clause Prong — Cited again for the same holding on the takings side — patents are property, protected as other property. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: a patent is a form of property, protected as other property.

68

Geier v. American Honda Motor Co. (2000)

529 U.S. 861 (2000)

Score: 5x4

General Description: Held that a federal motor-vehicle safety standard preempted a state tort claim that conflicted with the federal regulatory objective.

Why Case is Relevant: The Wall Itself — Addresses the preemption question for the Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards — relevant to how the wall's statutes operate.

69

TrafFix Devices, Inc. v. Marketing Displays, Inc. (2001)

532 U.S. 23 (2001)

Score: 9x0

General Description: Marketing Displays held now-expired utility patents on a dual-spring mechanism that kept temporary road signs upright in wind. After the patents expired and a competitor began selling sign stands using the same mechanism, Marketing Displays sued for trade dress infringement, arguing that the visible spring design identified it as the source.

Justice Kennedy, for a unanimous Court, held that a functional product feature cannot receive trade dress protection under the Lanham Act. An expired utility patent is strong evidence that the features it claimed are functional, and where the expired patent claimed the feature in question, the party seeking trade dress protection bears a heavy burden of showing that the feature is merely ornamental, incidental, or arbitrary. The Court declined to let trade dress become the practical equivalent of an expired utility patent.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — The trademark-side counterpart to Bonito Boats and Sears. Establishes that when a utility patent expires, the functional design it covered cannot be recaptured through trade dress. The case marks the outer limit of what non-patent intellectual property law may do to prevent remanufacturing of a patent-expired design, and shows the Court policing that boundary on its own initiative. It sharpens the book’s central contrast: the intellectual property statutes release the expired design, while the regulatory statutes do not.

70

Eldred v. Ashcroft (2003)

537 U.S. 186 (2003)

Score: 7x2

In Exam

General Description: The Sonny Bono Copyright Term Extension Act of 1998 extended existing and future copyright terms by twenty years.

Eric Eldred and others who relied on works passing into the public domain argued that retroactively extending existing terms violated the Patent and Copyright Clause’s “limited Times” requirement and the First Amendment. The Supreme Court, seven to two, upheld the Act. Justice Ginsburg’s majority held that “limited Times” requires only that the term be limited, not perpetual, and that Congress could rationally decide to extend the period of incentive even retroactively. Justice Breyer’s dissent argued the extension produced a term economically equivalent to ninety-nine point eight percent of a perpetual copyright. The case is the leading adverse Patent Clause precedent; the chapter on objections considered distinguishes it as a copyright case in a calendar-adjustment posture, not a patent case in a structural-ratchet posture.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — The case behind the objection that a copyright-term extension survived a “limited Times” challenge — which the prong distinguishes. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: a copyright-term extension did not violate “limited Times”; Congress receives substantial deference.

71

Lawrence v. Texas (2003)

539 U.S. 558 (2003)

Score: 6x3*19

General Description: Justice Kennedy, for five Justices, held that a Texas statute criminalizing same-sex intimate conduct violates the Due Process Clause and overruled Bowers v. Hardwick. Justice O’Connor concurred in the judgment on equal-protection grounds. Justices Scalia and Thomas dissented.

The Court stated that Bowers was not correct when it was decided and is not correct today. The historians’ brief that supplied the corrected record was the work of scholars outside the legal profession, and the sources it used had been publicly available throughout the seventeen years Bowers stood.

Why Case is Relevant: When Outsiders Advance Constitutional Law and Other Disciplines — The correction, and the measure of how long a court will hold an unexamined historical assertion when no one puts the contrary record in front of it.

72

Kelo v. City of New London (2005)

545 U.S. 469 (2005)

Score: 5x4

General Description: Justice Stevens, for a five-Justice majority, held that a city’s transfer of private property to a private developer for economic development satisfies the public-use requirement of the Takings Clause. Justices O’Connor and Thomas each dissented.

Susette Kelo lost, the development was never built, and the company the project was meant to serve left New London in 2009. The decision produced legislative reform in a majority of States, which is the book’s point in citing it: a loss can produce a remedy, and the Institute for Justice, which carried the case, litigates in the same doctrinal territory this argument occupies.

Why Case is Relevant: What You Can Do: A Citizen’s Playbook — Named among the public-interest firms that take takings cases without charging a plaintiff. When Outsiders Advance Constitutional Law and Other Disciplines — An injury rather than a discovery, and a loss that still moved the law.

73

MedImmune, Inc. v. Genentech, Inc. (2007)

549 U.S. 118 (2007)

Score: 8x1

General Description: Justice Scalia, for the Court, held that a patent licensee need not breach or terminate its license before seeking a declaratory judgment that the patent is invalid, unenforceable, or not infringed. Justice Thomas dissented.

The decision removed the reasonable-apprehension-of-suit requirement the Federal Circuit had imposed and restored the ordinary actual-controversy standard of the Declaratory Judgment Act. A party need not expose itself to liability to test the legal position it is being held to.

Why Case is Relevant: You No Longer Need to Break a Law to Challenge One — The case the chapter is named for. The Filable Case: Why 2026 and Not Before — One of the conditions that make a pre-enforcement challenge available to a would-be remanufacturer who has built nothing yet.

74

Massachusetts v. Environmental Protection Agency (2007)

549 U.S. 497 (2007)

Score: 5x4

General Description: Twelve states and several local governments sued the EPA over the agency’s refusal to regulate greenhouse-gas emissions from new motor vehicles.

The Supreme Court held that Massachusetts had standing to sue, because the state had suffered concrete injury to its territory (rising sea levels eroding coastline) and to the welfare of its citizens, and because the federal government’s failure to regulate was traceable to that harm and redressable by a court order. The decision announced the doctrine of special solicitude: a sovereign plaintiff is owed a more permissive standing analysis than the showing required of an ordinary private plaintiff. The case is the inflection point for sovereign and quasi-sovereign standing in modern federal litigation, and the door through which states will be among the first plaintiffs in the patent-bargain case.

Why Case is Relevant: Why Nobody Noticed — Cited among the doors the Court has opened over the past quarter-century. You No Longer Need to Break a Law to Challenge One — The 2007 turning point — states sued the EPA over a regulatory failure and established a route that needs no broken law. Sovereign and Public-Fiduciary Plaintiffs — The anchor for sovereign standing — a state suing the federal government is owed special solicitude. The Filable Case — Cited as part of the recent access toolkit — a sovereign plaintiff may sue over a federal regulatory failure. Why You Want to Be the Plaintiff — Cited for the parens patriae / sovereign standing a state invoked successfully.

75

Golan v. Holder (2012)

565 U.S. 302 (2012)

Score: 6x2

General Description: Section 514 of the Uruguay Round Agreements Act restored United States copyright to foreign works that had already passed into the American public domain. Conductors, teachers, and film archivists who had built on those works sued. The Court upheld the statute.

Justice Ginsburg, for the Court, held that the “limited Times” language does not bar Congress from extending protection to works already in the public domain, and rejected the premise that the Constitution renders the public domain largely untouchable by Congress; the First Amendment was satisfied by the idea-expression distinction and fair use. The opinion refers to the provision as the Copyright and Patent Clause. Justice Breyer, joined by Justice Alito, dissented, arguing the restoration withdrew from the public what it already held without promoting the creation of anything new. Justice Kagan took no part. The decision reviews an act of Congress under the Copyright Clause; it does not reach whether an executive agency may, by regulation, make a patent-expired design unlawful to build. That distinction is this argument’s, not the Court’s.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — Adverse authority, paired with Eldred v. Ashcroft — the Court has said the public domain is not beyond the reach of Congress. Answered on the ground that Golan reviewed an enacted statute in a copyright posture, while the wall at issue here was built by agencies under general delegations and has never been tested under the Patent Clause.

76

Arkansas Game & Fish Commission v. United States (2012)

568 U.S. 23 (2012)

Score: 8x0

In Exam

General Description: The federal government, operating a dam, released water in a pattern that repeatedly flooded a state-owned wildlife area during the tree-growing season over a span of years, then stopped.

The State sued, claiming the recurring flooding had killed timber and worked a taking. The government argued that flooding can be a taking only when it is permanent. The Supreme Court rejected that categorical rule. Whether government action is a taking, the Court held, depends on its actual effect on the property — not on whether the invasion is labeled temporary or permanent.

Why Case is Relevant: Four Problems, One Remedy — One of four recent decisions the book cites as making the case ripe now. The Takings Clause Prong — Cited for the holding that even temporary government-induced invasions (flooding) may be takings. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: even temporary government-induced invasions of property may constitute takings.

77

Clapper v. Amnesty International USA (2013)

568 U.S. 398 (2013)

Score: 5x4

General Description: Held that plaintiffs lacked standing to challenge surveillance where the threatened injury was too speculative.

Why Case is Relevant: Sovereign and Public-Fiduciary Plaintiffs — Polices the injury line — an injury must be concrete and imminent, not speculative — the standing bar plaintiffs must clear. Objections Considered — Cited by the objection that a buyer’s injury rests on a speculative chain; answered there with Diamond Alternative Energy.

78

Susan B. Anthony List v. Driehaus (2014)

573 U.S. 149 (2014)

Score: 9x0

General Description: The Susan B. Anthony List, an antiabortion advocacy organization, had distributed political messages criticizing a congressional candidate for his health-care vote.

The candidate filed a complaint under an Ohio statute criminalizing false statements in political campaigns. The advocacy organization sued in federal court before any prosecution had occurred, seeking a declaration that the statute violated the First Amendment. The Supreme Court held that pre-enforcement standing existed: a credible threat of enforcement under a statute the plaintiff intends to violate suffices to establish injury in fact, and the plaintiff need not wait for prosecution to challenge the statute. The case is the controlling modern statement of pre-enforcement standing, and the doctrine on which the patent-bargain case will rest at the standing stage.

Why Case is Relevant: You No Longer Need to Break a Law to Challenge One — The credible-threat doctrine — a third pre-enforcement-review case extending standing further. The Filable Case — Cited for the unanimous holding that a plaintiff facing a credible threat of enforcement may sue before enforcement.

79

Kimble v. Marvel Entertainment, LLC (2015)

576 U.S. 446 (2015)

Score: 6x3

General Description: Kimble sold Marvel a patent on a web-shooting toy and took royalties. When the patent expired, Marvel stopped paying. Kimble asked the Court to overrule Brulotte v. Thys Co., which bars post-expiration royalties.

Justice Kagan, for the Court, declined on stare decisis grounds and stated the governing principle: a patent typically expires twenty years from the filing date, and when it expires the patentee's prerogatives expire with it and the right to make or use the article, free from all restriction, passes to the public. Justice Alito dissented, joined by the Chief Justice and Justice Thomas, on the merits of Brulotte. The phrase “free from all restriction” means free of the patent monopoly; it does not hold that the article is immune from all regulation.

Why Case is Relevant: The Patent Clause Prong — The book's canonical gloss: at expiration the unrestricted right to make and use the invention passes to the public. Federal patent-law authority, decided against a private licensor. Cited for what passes to the public at expiration, not for any holding about regulatory power.

80

Horne v. Department of Agriculture (2015)

576 U.S. 351 (2015)

Score: 8x1*4

In Exam

General Description: A Depression-era federal marketing order required raisin growers to surrender a portion of each year’s crop to a government committee. The growers refused and were fined.

The government argued the requirement was a permissible regulation of the raisin market, not a taking. The Supreme Court disagreed. Compelling the growers to hand over their raisins was a physical appropriation of property and therefore a taking per se, and the government could not avoid that conclusion by characterizing the appropriation as a condition of doing business or a contribution to orderly marketing.

Why Case is Relevant: The Environmental Question — Listed among the per se categories (appropriation of personal property) the remedy does not disturb. The Takings Clause Prong — Applied the per se rule to government appropriation of personal property. Objections Considered — Cited on recent personal-property takings doctrine — the Takings Clause applies to personal property with full force. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: the per se takings rule applies with full force to the appropriation of personal property.

81

Star Athletica, L.L.C. v. Varsity Brands, Inc. (2017)

580 U.S. 405 (2017)

Score: 6x2*12

General Description: A cheerleading-uniform maker held copyright registrations for two-dimensional designs of lines, chevrons, and shapes appearing on the surface of its uniforms, and sued a competitor for infringement. The competitor argued that designs on useful articles are not copyrightable at all.

Justice Thomas, for the Court, established the governing separability test under 17 U.S.C. § 101: a feature incorporated into the design of a useful article is eligible for copyright only if it can be perceived as a two- or three-dimensional work of art separate from the article and would qualify as a protectable pictorial, graphic, or sculptural work on its own or in another medium if imagined separately. The Court expressly noted that the test does not render the underlying useful article itself eligible for copyright.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — The governing authority for how far copyright reaches into a manufactured product. A separable badge, emblem, or applied graphic may qualify; the vehicle carrying it may not. The case answers directly the objection that an entire car or truck is copyright-protected for ninety-five years as a corporate work made for hire: the term is real, but a useful article is not the kind of work to which it attaches. Together with TrafFix, it fixes the ceiling on what non-patent intellectual property law can protect after a utility patent expires.

82

Oil States Energy Services, LLC v. Greene’s Energy Group, LLC (2018)

584 U.S. 325 (2018)

Score: 7x2

General Description: Oil States, whose patent on hydraulic-fracturing wellhead equipment had been cancelled by the Patent Office in inter partes review, argued that only an Article III court could revoke an issued patent.

The Supreme Court disagreed, seven to two. Justice Thomas, for the Court, held that a patent is a public franchise and that reconsidering its validity is a matter of public rights Congress may assign to an executive agency. Justice Gorsuch, joined by Chief Justice Roberts, dissented, treating the issued patent as private property revocable only by a court. The majority called its holding narrow, reserved the retroactivity question, and said it was not deciding whether patents are property under the Due Process or Takings Clauses. It also repeated, from its own older cases, that the grant of a patent is a matter between the public, who are the grantors, and the patentee. The decision concerns the government’s power over a patent during its term; it does not reach the public’s right to build the design after the patent expires. That distinction is this argument’s, not the Court’s.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — Adverse authority raised by a scholar of administrative law: if an agency may cancel a live patent, the Court is unlikely to police what agencies do after expiration. Answered on the ground that Oil States sits on the grant side of the Clause and expressly reserved the Takings question, and that the public-franchise vocabulary names the public as the party the franchise came from and goes to when the term is over.

83

Trump v. Hawaii (2018)

585 U.S. 667 (2018)

Score: 5x4

General Description: Chief Justice Roberts, for a five-Justice majority, upheld the entry restrictions of Presidential Proclamation 9645. Justices Breyer and Sotomayor each dissented.

The book cites the case for a single sentence in it. The Court stated that Korematsu v. United States had been gravely wrong the day it was decided and has no place in law under the Constitution — a repudiation offered seventy-four years after the fact, and only once the suppressed record had long been public.

Why Case is Relevant: When Outsiders Advance Constitutional Law and Other Disciplines — Cited for how a court speaks when it learns the original proceeding was tainted, and for how long the interval can be.

84

Google LLC v. Oracle America, Inc. (2021)

593 U.S. 1 (2021)

Score: 6x2*21

General Description: Google built the Android platform to let programmers use the Java language and copied roughly 11,500 lines of declaring code from the Java SE application programming interface so that familiar calls would work on the new platform, writing its own implementing code. Oracle, which had acquired Java, sued for copyright infringement.

Justice Breyer, for the Court, assumed without deciding that the declaring code was copyrightable and held Google’s copying fair use. The declaring code was functional and tied to the programmers’ investment in learning it; Google took only what was needed to let programmers work in a new environment; and the copying advanced the constitutional purpose of copyright by enabling new creative expression.

Why Case is Relevant: Objections Considered — The second of three reasons the firmware copyright is narrower than its term: the Court has protected the room a rewriter needs. A remanufacturer may study the original engine-control module to learn what signals the engine expects and write new code to produce them.

85

Cedar Point Nursery v. Hassid (2021)

594 U.S. 139 (2021)

Score: 6x3

In Exam

General Description: A California regulation gave labor organizers a right to enter an agricultural employer’s property to speak with workers for several hours a day during much of the year. The growers said the forced access took their property.

The Supreme Court agreed. A regulation that appropriates a right to physically enter private land and hands it to others is a per se taking, the Court held, even though the access was intermittent and the owners kept every other use of their land. The decision is part of the modern Court’s broadened reading of what counts as a taking.

Why Case is Relevant: Four Problems, One Remedy — One of the four recent decisions cited as making the case ripe. The Environmental Question — Listed among the per se categories (physical occupation for limited hours). The Takings Clause Prong — Extended the physical-occupation branch to forced access for limited hours. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: a regulation granting third parties a right of physical access, even for limited hours, is a per se taking.

86

American Hospital Association v. Becerra (2022)

Score: 9x0

General Description: Held that HHS could not vary drug-reimbursement rates for a subset of hospitals without the survey the statute required; decided without Chevron deference.

Why Case is Relevant: You No Longer Need to Break a Law to Challenge One — Illustrates how broadly the regulatory wall reaches — the Association sued HHS and prevailed. Direct and Associational Plaintiff Categories — Cited as an associational plaintiff — the Association represents roughly 5,000 hospitals and brings real litigating capacity.

87

West Virginia v. Environmental Protection Agency (2022)

597 U.S. 697 (2022)

Score: 6x3

In Exam

General Description: Several States challenged the EPA’s authority to reshape the nation’s electricity generation through climate regulation.

The Supreme Court ruled against the agency and articulated the “major questions doctrine”: when an agency claims the power to decide a matter of vast economic and political significance, it must point to clear authorization from Congress, not infer the power from a vague or ancillary statutory phrase.

Why Case is Relevant: Four Problems, One Remedy — One of the four recent decisions cited as making the case ripe. The Filable Case — Cited for the fullest statement of the major-questions doctrine. The Patent Clause Prong — The major-questions doctrine: an agency claiming power of vast economic and political significance must point to clear congressional authorization. Why You Want to Be the Plaintiff — Cited as shorthand for a single decision that reset a field — named for its plaintiff. Reason the Constitutional Argument Yourself — Exam holding: under the major-questions doctrine, an agency asserting authority over a matter of vast significance needs clear congressional authorization.

88

Sackett v. Environmental Protection Agency (2023)

598 U.S. 651 (2023)

Score: 9x0*5

General Description: A landowning couple challenged the reach of a federal Clean Water Act rule over their own property and prevailed at the Supreme Court without first incurring a penalty.

Why Case is Relevant: Why You Want to Be the Plaintiff — Cited as a directly-regulated single-landowner plaintiff who challenged the EPA and won.

89

Tyler v. Hennepin County, Minnesota (2023)

598 U.S. 631 (2023)

Score: 9x0

General Description: A county sold a delinquent taxpayer’s home for $40,000 to satisfy a $15,000 tax debt and kept the surplus. Chief Justice Roberts, for a unanimous Court, held the retention a classic taking. Justice Gorsuch concurred, joined by Justice Jackson.

The State argued that its own law had stripped the owner of any property interest in the surplus. The Court answered that property rights cannot be so easily manipulated, and that a government may not sidestep the Takings Clause by disavowing traditional property interests long recognized under state law. The principle reaches a right the government declines to recognize as well as one it concedes.

Why Case is Relevant: The Takings Clause Prong — The answer to the objection that the public’s post-expiration right is not property because current federal law does not treat it as property. Objections Considered — Cited for the same proposition where the objection is stated in its strongest form.

90

Sheetz v. County of El Dorado (2024)

601 U.S. 267 (2024)

Score: 9x0

In Exam

General Description: A county charged a landowner a substantial traffic-impact fee as a condition of a building permit. The fee was fixed by a general schedule the county’s legislature had adopted, not by an official’s individual decision.
